Additional lemmas for the proofs of Section
S4.0.4

Lemma S4.3
.

Let
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{S}^{k_{2}-1}
. If
\mathbf{v}_{[1]}<0
, then the optimum value of the program

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}{\mathrm{min}}\quad\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

(S4.10)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}}{\mathrm{min}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{min}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{j}

\displaystyle=0,

is
\upkappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{p})
if
k_{1}=3
and
k_{2}=2
, where

\displaystyle\upkappa(\mathbf{v},\mathbf{p})=\begin{cases}\begin{array}[]{@{}l@{}}\mathbf{v}_{[1]}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\max(\mathbf{v}_{[2]},-\mathbf{v}_{[1]})(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})\\
+\mathbf{v}_{[2]}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})\end{array}&\text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2,\\[10.0pt]
\begin{multlined}-|\min(\mathbf{v}_{[2]},-\mathbf{v}_{[1]})(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|\\
\\
+\mathbf{v}_{[2]}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+|\mathbf{v}_{[2]}|(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})\end{multlined}&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

If
\mathbf{v}_{[1]}\geq 0
, then the optimal value of the program in (
S4.10
) is
\mathbf{v}_{1}+\mathbf{v}_{2}
.

Proof.

Let us define

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j}).

(S4.11)

Note that

\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})\geq\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\mathbf{v}_{i}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)=(k_{2}-1)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\mathbf{v}_{i}

for all
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
.
If
\mathbf{v}_{[1]}\geq 0
, then any
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
with
\max(\mathbf{y})\leq\mathbf{v}_{[1]}
satisfies
\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\mathbf{v}_{i}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)
. In particular, we can take
\mathbf{y}=\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}}
.
The result for
\mathbf{v}_{[1]}>0
follows noting
\mathbf{p}\in\mathcal{S}^{k_{2}-1}
, which implies
\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(1-\mathbf{p}_{j})=1
when
k_{2}=2
. Hence, we consider
\mathbf{v}_{[1]}<0
.
Note also that
the optimum value of the program (
S4.10
)
equals

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}{\mathrm{min}}\quad\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{[i]},\mathbf{y}_{j})

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}}{\mathrm{min}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{min}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{j}

\displaystyle=0.

Therefore, it suffices to show the proof considering
\mathbf{v}
is sorted, i.e.,
\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}<0
.
In this case, from Lemma
S4.4
it follows that the optimum value of the optimization program in (
S4.10
) equals the optimal value of the program

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}{\mathrm{min}}\quad\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

(S4.12)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}}{\mathrm{min}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{min}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{j}

\displaystyle=0,\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}_{j}

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{v}_{1}

\displaystyle\text{ for all }j\in[k_{2}].

When
k_{2}=2
, we can represent
\mathbf{y}
by
\mathbf{y}=(x,-x)
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}
. Then the optimization problem in (
S4.12
) becomes

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{min}}\quad\mathbf{p}_{2}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{1},x)+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},x)\Big)+\mathbf{p}_{1}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{1},-x)+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-x)\Big)\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

(S4.13)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{x\in\mathbb{R}}}{\mathrm{min}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{min}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle x,-x

\displaystyle\geq\mathbf{v}_{1}.

When
x,-x\geq\mathbf{v}_{1}
, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{2}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{1},x)+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},x)\Big)+\mathbf{p}_{1}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{1},-x)+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-x)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{2}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},x)+\mathbf{p}_{1}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-x).

Thus the optimization problem in (
S4.13
) rewrites as

\displaystyle\underset{\displaystyle x\in\mathbb{R}}{\mathrm{min}}\quad x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{2}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},x)+\mathbf{p}_{1}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-x)\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle\hfil\hfil\displaystyle\displaystyle

(S4.14)

\displaystyle\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\underset{\displaystyle\phantom{x\in\mathbb{R}}}{\mathrm{min}}$}][c]{\mathmakebox[\widthof{$\mathrm{min}$}][l]{\mathrm{\kern 1.00006pts.t.}}}\quad

\displaystyle|x|

\displaystyle\leq-\mathbf{v}_{1},

where we used the fact that
x,-x\geq\mathbf{v}_{1}
is equivalent to
|x|\leq-\mathbf{v}_{1}
when
\mathbf{v}_{1}<0
.
We overload notation and define

\tilde{\phi}(x):=x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{2}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},x)+\mathbf{p}_{1}\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-x).

Let us define
-v_{1}=c>0
. Therefore,
the optimal value of (
S4.14
) equals
\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)
.
Since
v_{2}\geq v_{1}
, there can be three cases.

Case 1:
\mathbf{v}_{2}=\mathbf{v}_{1}

Since
x\in[-c,c]
, it follows that
x,-x\geq c_{2}
implying
\tilde{\phi}(x)=2x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})
. Then

\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=-2|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|c=-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2})|

\displaystyle\argmin\limits_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}c&\ \mathbf{p}_{1}>\mathbf{p}_{2}\\
-c&\mathbf{p}_{2}<\mathbf{p}_{1}\\
[-c,c]&\ \text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}=\mathbf{p}_{2}.\end{cases}

Let us call the above number
x^{*}
.
Suppose
\mathbf{y}^{*}=(x^{*},-x^{*})

Then

\displaystyle\argmax(\mathbf{y}^{*})=\begin{cases}1&\ \text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}>\mathbf{p}_{2}\\
2&\text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{2}<\mathbf{p}_{1}\\
\{1,2\}&\ \text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}=\mathbf{p}_{2}.\end{cases}

Case 2:
\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{v}_{2}<0

In this case,
-c<\mathbf{v}_{2}<0<-\mathbf{v}_{2}<c
, indicating

\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}x&\text{ if }x\in[-c,\mathbf{v}_{2})\\
2x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})&\text{ if }x\in(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{2}]\\
2x\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}x+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}&x\in(-\mathbf{v}_{2},c].\end{cases}

Since
\tilde{\phi}(x)
is a piecewise linear function, the knots will be in
\argmin_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)
where the knots belong to the set
\{-c,\mathbf{v}_{2},0,-\mathbf{v}_{2},c\}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}2\mathbf{p}_{1}c-\mathbf{p}_{2}c+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{if }x=-c\\
2\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})&\text{if }x=\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
-2\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})&\text{if }x=-\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
2c\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}c+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{if }x=c.\end{cases}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\min\Big(2\mathbf{p}_{1}c-\mathbf{p}_{2}c+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2},\ -2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|,\ 2c\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}c+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}\Big).

Let us denote

h_{c}(y;c,v_{2})=2yc-(1-y)c+(1-y)\mathbf{v}_{2}=3yc-c+(1-y)\mathbf{v}_{2}.

Then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\min\Big(h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}),\ h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{2}),\ -2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|\Big).

It can be easily seen that
h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1})\gtreqless h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{2})
iff
\mathbf{p}_{1}\gtreqless\mathbf{p}_{2}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\min(h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}),h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{2}))=h_{c}(\min(\mathbf{p}_{1},\mathbf{p}_{2})).

(S4.15)

Hence,

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\min\Big(h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}),\ -2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|\Big).

Now note that

h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})=2c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})(\mathbf{v}_{2}-c)=-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}).

(S4.16)

Therefore,

h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})\gtreqless-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|

if and only if

-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})\gtreqless 2\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|

where we used the fact that
\mathbf{v}_{2}<0
. The last display is equivalent to

(\mathbf{v}_{2}+c)(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})\gtreqless(\mathbf{v}_{2}+c)|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|.

Since
c+\mathbf{v}_{2}>0
, the above is equivalent to

\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\gtreqless|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|\text{ or }\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\gtreqless\frac{\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}}{2}.

Therefore, we obtained that

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})&\text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq\frac{\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}}{2}\\
-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

Case 3:
0\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}<c

In this case,
-c<0\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}<c
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}x&\text{ if }x\in[-c,-\mathbf{v}_{2})\\
x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }x\in(-\mathbf{v}_{2},\mathbf{v}_{2}]\\
2x\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}x+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}&x\in(\mathbf{v}_{2},c]\end{cases}

Note that

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}2\mathbf{p}_{1}c-\mathbf{p}_{2}c+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{if }x=-c\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}+1)&\text{if }x=-\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}+1)&\text{if }x=\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
2c\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}c+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{if }x=c.\end{cases}

Therefore,
\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)
equals

\displaystyle\min\Big(2\mathbf{p}_{1}c-\mathbf{p}_{2}c+\mathbf{p}_{2}\mathbf{v}_{2},\ -\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|+\mathbf{v}_{2},\ 2c\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}c+\mathbf{p}_{1}\mathbf{v}_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \min\Big(h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}),\ h(\mathbf{p}_{2}),\ -\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \min\Big(h_{c}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}),\ -\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \min\Big(-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}),\ -\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}\Big).

where (a) follows from (
S4.15
) and (b) follows from (
S4.16
).
It can be easily seen that

-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})\lesseqgtr-\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}

if and only if

(c-\mathbf{v}_{2})(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})\lesseqgtr(c-\mathbf{v}_{2})|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|.

Since
c>\mathbf{v}_{2}
in this case, the above is equivalent to
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\lesseqgtr|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|
or
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\lesseqgtr(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
. Therefore, we have obtained that

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}-c|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+c(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})&\text{ if }\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2\\
-|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

Case 4:
c\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}

In this case,
-\mathbf{v}_{2}\leq-c<c\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}
. Thus
-x,x\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}
if
x\in[-c,c]
Therefore,

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}(x)=x(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})+\mathbf{v}_{2}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=-|\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}|c+\mathbf{v}_{2}

Combining cases 1-4 for
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2

The above steps imply that if
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}2\mathbf{v}_{2}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|&\text{ if }\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{v}_{2}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|-\mathbf{v}_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})&\text{ if }\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{v}_{2}<-\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }-\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}.\end{cases}

When
\mathbf{v}_{1}=\mathbf{v}_{2}
,

-\mathbf{v}_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})=\mathbf{v}_{1}\Big(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\Big)=\mathbf{v}_{1}\absolutevalue{\mp_1-\mp_2}.

Therefore, if
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|-\mathbf{v}_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})&\text{ if }\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}<-\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }-\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}.\end{cases}

However, when
-\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{1}\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2},

and when
-\mathbf{v}_{1}>\mathbf{v}_{2}
,

\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|-\mathbf{v}_{1}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})=\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}).

Therefore,
if
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}\leq(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[-c,c]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\max(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2}).

(S4.17)

Combining cases 1-4 for
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}>(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2

The above cases imply that when
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}>(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
, then

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[\mathbf{v}_{1},-\mathbf{v}_{1}]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|&\text{ if }\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{v}_{2}<0\\
-|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }0\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}<-\mathbf{v}_{1}\\
\mathbf{v}_{1}|\mathbf{p}_{1}-\mathbf{p}_{2}|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }-\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{2},\end{cases}

which is equivalent to

\displaystyle\inf_{x\in[\mathbf{v}_{1},-\mathbf{v}_{1}]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=\begin{cases}-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|&\text{ if }\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{v}_{2}<0\\
-|\min(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}&\text{ if }0\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}\end{cases}

When
\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{v}_{2}<0
,

\displaystyle-2|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -|\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})-\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -|\min(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})-\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -|\min(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+|\mathbf{v}_{2}|(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}).

When
0\leq\mathbf{v}_{2}
,

\mathbf{v}_{2}=\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})=\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+|\mathbf{v}_{2}|(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}).

Therefore, we have obtained that when
\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2}>(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})/2
,

\inf_{x\in[\mathbf{v}_{1},-\mathbf{v}_{1}]}\tilde{\phi}(x)=-|\min(\mathbf{v}_{2},-\mathbf{v}_{1})(\mathbf{p}_{2}-\mathbf{p}_{1})|+\mathbf{v}_{2}(\mathbf{p}_{1}\vee\mathbf{p}_{2})+|\mathbf{v}_{2}|(\mathbf{p}_{1}\wedge\mathbf{p}_{2})

(S4.18)

Hence, the proof follows combining (
S4.17
) and (
S4.18
).

∎

Lemma S4.4
.

Consider the optimization problem in (
S4.10
).
If
\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\ldots\leq\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1}<0
, then for any
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 2
, the optimal value of (
S4.10
) equals

\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}{\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{j}\Big)\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq A_{1}}\max(\mathbf{v}_{i},\mathbf{y}_{j})},

where

\mathcal{C}_{1}=\bigg\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}:\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{j}=0,\ \mathbf{y}_{i}\geq\mathbf{v}_{1}\bigg\}.

Proof of Lemma
S4.4
.

Let us denote the set
\mathcal{C}=\{\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}:\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{y}_{j}=0\}
. Hence, the optimal value of (
S4.10
) equals
\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})
where
\tilde{\phi}
is as defined in (
S4.11
).
We will show that
\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})
.
If possible, suppose
i\in[k_{2}]
is such that
\mathbf{y}_{i}<\mathbf{v}_{1}
. Let
l\neq i
and
l\in[k_{2}]
. Our next step is to show that

\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})\geq\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
where
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
is the vector obtained by replacing
\mathbf{y}_{i}
with
\mathbf{v}_{1}
and replacing
\mathbf{y}_{l}
with
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}_{l}=\mathbf{y}_{l}+\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{v}_{1}
.
Clearly,
\mathbf{y}_{l}^{\prime}<\mathbf{y}_{l}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{\prime}>\mathbf{y}_{i}
. Note that
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}\in\mathcal{C}
and
\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})-\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
equals

\displaystyle\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{i}\Big)\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{i})+\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{l}\Big)\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l})

\displaystyle\ -\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{i}\Big)\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{v}_{1})-\Big(1-\mathbf{p}_{l}\Big)\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l}+\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1-\mathbf{p}_{i})\bigg(\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{i})-\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{v}_{1})\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle\ +(1-\mathbf{p}_{l})\bigg(\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l}+\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{v}_{1})\Big)\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (1-\mathbf{p}_{l})\bigg(\sum\limits_{r\in[k_{1}-1]}\Big(\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l})-\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{l}+\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{v}_{1})\Big)\bigg)

where the last equality follows because
\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{y}_{i})=\max(\mathbf{v}_{r},\mathbf{v}_{1})=\mathbf{v}_{r}
or all
r\in[k_{1}-1]
since
\mathbf{y}_{i}<\mathbf{v}_{1}\leq\mathbf{v}_{r}
for all
r\in[k_{1}-1]
. However, since
\mathbf{y}_{l}+\mathbf{y}_{i}-\mathbf{v}_{1}<\mathbf{y}_{l}
, we obtain
\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})\geq\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y}^{\prime})
. We can keep replacing
\mathbf{y}_{i}
with
\mathbf{v}_{1}
if
\mathbf{y}_{i}<\mathbf{v}_{1}
until all elements of
\mathbf{y}^{\prime}
are greater than equal to
\mathbf{v}_{1}
. Thus we have shown that
\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})\leq\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})
, which, combined with
\mathcal{C}_{1}\subset\mathcal{C}
, implies
\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}_{1}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})=\inf_{\mathbf{y}\in\mathcal{C}}\tilde{\phi}(\mathbf{y})
.

∎

S5
Proof of Theorem
3.1

S5.1
Proof preparation

Before proceeding with the proof of Theorem
3.1
, we first gather some key facts that will be utilized throughout the proof and briefly outline the main steps involved.

We say
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is lower semicontinuous at
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
if
\liminf_{\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{x}_{0}}f(\mathbf{x})\geq f(\mathbf{x}_{0})
. Following Definition 1.2.3., pp. 78 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
,
f
is lower semicontinuous everywhere if and only if it is closed.
Following Section 3.2 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
, we define a 0-coercive convex function as follows.

Definition S5.1

(0-coercivity)
.

A closed convex function
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is called 0-coercive if
f(x)\to\infty
as
\|x\|_{2}\to\infty
.

A 0-coercive convex function is necessarily bounded below.
An associated notion is that of the recession function.

Definition S5.2
.

Suppose the function
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is closed and convex. Then for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, the function
f_{\infty}^{\prime}
defined by

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{f(tx)-f(0)}{t}

is known as the recession function of
f
.

It is evident that
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(0)=0
. The behavior of
f_{\infty}^{\prime}
for
x\neq 0
is closely related to the coercivity of
f
and can inform us on the existence of a minimum. The following result, synthesizing Remark 3.2.7 and Proposition 3.2.4 from
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
, establishes key properties of recession functions in relation to coercivity.

Fact S5.1
.

Suppose
f
is convex and bounded below. Then

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)\geq 0
for all
x\neq 0
. In fact, there are only two possibilities: (1)

f
is 0-coercive, and the set of minimum points of
f
is a non-empty compact set (singletone if
f
is strictly convex) – this happens if
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)>0
for all
x\neq 0
, or (2) the minimum of
f
is unattained – this happens if
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(x)=0
for some
x\neq 0
.

Fact
S5.1
suggests that we can deduce whether the minimum of
f
is achieved by evaluating the recession functions of
f
. The subsequent fact provides a useful tool for this purpose.

Fact S5.2

(Proposition 3.2.8 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
)
.

here

a

Consider
m\in\mathbb{N}
and let
a_{1},\ldots,a_{m}
be positive numbers. Then for closed convex functions
f_{1},\ldots,f_{m}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, we have

\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}f_{i}\Big)_{\infty}^{\prime}=\sum_{i=1}^{m}a_{i}(f_{i})_{\infty}^{\prime}.

b

Suppose
h(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=f(a_{1}x_{1},\ldots,a_{k}x_{k})
where
a_{i}\in\{\pm 1\}
for
k=1,\ldots,m
and
f
is convex. Then

h_{\infty}^{\prime}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{k})=f_{\infty}^{\prime}(a_{1}x_{1},\ldots,a_{k}x_{k}).

c

Let
\text{Range}(A)
denote the range of
A
for any matrix
A
with
k
columns, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
h(\mathbf{x})=f(A\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
for a convex function
f
and
\text{dom}(f)\cap\text{Range}(A)\neq\emptyset
, then
(f\circ A)^{\prime}_{\infty}=f_{\infty}^{\prime}\circ A
.

Now we will collect some initial results that will be used throughout the proof.
From
Wang and Scott (2023b)
, it follows that
if
\upsilon:[k_{1}]\mapsto[k_{1}]
,
\tilde{\pi}:[k_{2}]\mapsto[k_{2}]
are two permutations and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
, then

\displaystyle\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w};i,j)=\psi(\upsilon(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\pi}(\mathbf{w});\upsilon^{-1}(i),\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(j))=\psi(\upsilon^{-1}(\mathbf{x}),\tilde{\pi}^{-1}(\mathbf{w});\upsilon(i),\tilde{\pi}(j)).

(S5.1)

We refer to (
S5.1
) as the clockwise permutation symmetry of
\psi
.
The above implies
\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w};i,j)
is permutation equivariant w.r.t.
\mathbf{x}
and
i
when
\mathbf{w}
and
j
are fixed and vice versa.
Using this result, we can prove that
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
under the setup of Theorem
3.1
. Lemma
S5.1
establishes this fact. The proof of Lemma
S5.1
can be found in Section
S5.4.1
.

Lemma S5.1
.

Suppose
\psi
is a PERM loss with template
\eta
, which is concave and bounded above. If
\cap_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\cap_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\text{int}(\text{dom}(\psi(\cdot;i,j))\neq\emptyset
, then
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
.

Since
-\eta
is bounded below, if
\text{dom}(-\eta)
is non-empty, then
-\eta
is proper. Therefore Lemma
S5.1
implies
-\eta
is a proper convex function.

S5.1.1
The main steps

We will prove this theorem in two main steps.
The main goal of the first step is to reduce the general problem to a problem with
k_{1}=2
and
k_{2}=2
.
In the second step, from a high-level, we first show that the concavity of
\psi
enforces some geometric restrictions on
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
in the corresponding binary-treatment problem. Then we will show that if
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then it also induces some restrictions on
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
. Using a bad set of distributions, we will show that these restrictions get violated in presence of the abovementioned concavity-induced restrictions. The structure of this section is as follows: Step 1 is proved in Section
S5.2
, and Step 2 is proved in Section
S5.3
. The main lemmas are proved in Section
S5.4
, while the proofs of additional results are deferred to Section
S5.5
.

S5.2
Step 1

The first task of this step is to construct a sublass of distributions for which the
\tilde{f}
corresponding to our
\psi
exists and the forms of
\tilde{f}
,
\tilde{d}
,
d^{*}
, etc. are tractable. Then we will find a further subclass of distributions, for which, the DTR optimization problem for
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
treatment levels reduce to a DTR optimization problem with 2 treatment levels per stage.

We consider a subset
\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
of
\mathcal{P}^{0}
, the space all distributions satisfying Assumptions I-V. The distributions in
\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
entail the following setting.
For these distributions,
Y_{1}=0
and
O_{1},O_{2}=\emptyset
. Therefore, there is no covariate. We could add covariates in this counterexample, but the main idea of the construction of the counterexample would be similar. However, the calculation would be more technical. Since
\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}\subset\mathcal{P}^{0}
, it follows that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid i,j]>0
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
.

Since
H_{1}=\emptyset
, under this setting, the first stage class-score function
f_{1}
can be represented by a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. The corresponding
d_{1}=\argmax_{i\in[k_{1}]}f_{1}(i)
will be a number. Also, since
H_{2}=A_{1}
,
\mathcal{H}_{2}=[k_{1}]
. Thus
f_{2}
is a map from
[k_{1}]
to
\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
and the second stage DTR
d_{2}
is a map from
[k_{1}]
to
[k_{2}]
. For each
i\in[k_{1}]
, the class score
f_{2}(i)
is a
k_{2}
-dimesnional real vector. Therefore, the function
f_{2}
can be completely explained by the
k_{1}
vectors
\{f_{2}(1),\ldots,f_{2}(k_{1})\}
.
Since
H_{2}=A_{1}
for distributions in
\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
, the expectation
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}+Y_{2}\mid H_{2},A_{2}]=\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1},A_{2}]
.
For
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
, let us denote
\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1}=i,A_{2}=j]
by
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
. Since for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
,
\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1},A_{2}]>0
, it follows that
\mathbf{p}_{ij}>0
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. Note that every
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
elicits one set of
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
’s. Moreover, given any set
\{\mathbf{p}_{ij}\}_{i\in[k_{1}],j\in[k_{2}]}
with positive
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
’s, we can construct a
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
so that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1}=i,A_{2}=j]=\mathbf{p}_{ij}
. It can also be shown that for any DTR
d=(d_{1},d_{2})
, the value function
V(d_{1},d_{2})=\mathbf{p}_{d_{1},d_{2}(d_{1})}
. Thus, the contribution of
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
on the value function reflects only via
(\mathbf{p}_{ij})
.

For each
A_{1}=i
, any number in the set
\argmax_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij}
is a candidate of the optimal second stage treatment assignment
d^{*}_{2}(i)
. Also, since
H_{1}=\emptyset
, it follows that
d_{1}^{*}
can be any number in the set
\argmax_{i\in[k_{1}]}Q^{*}_{1}(i)
.
Under our setting, it follows that

Q^{*}_{1}(i)=\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1},A_{2}=j]\mid A_{1}=i]=\mathbb{E}[\max_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{A_{1}j}\mid A_{1}=i]=\max\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij}

Thus any member of
\argmax_{i\in[k_{1}]}(\max_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij})
is a candidate for
d_{1}^{*}
. Also, since
V_{*}=\max_{i\in[k_{1}]}Q^{*}_{1}(i)
,

\displaystyle V_{*}=\max\limits_{i\in[k_{1}],j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij}.

(S5.2)

The surrogate value function for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
takes the form

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(f_{1},f_{2}):=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{Y_{2}}{\pi_{1}(A_{1})\pi_{2}(A_{2}\mid A_{1})}\psi(f_{1},f_{2}(A_{1});A_{1},A_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1},A_{2}]}{\pi_{1}(A_{1})\pi_{2}(A_{2}\mid A_{1})}\psi(f_{1},f_{2}(A_{1});A_{1},A_{2})\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1},A_{2}=j]}{\pi_{1}(A_{1})}\psi(f_{1},f_{2}(A_{1});A_{1},j)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid i,j]\psi(f_{1},f_{2}(i);i,j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij}\psi(f_{1},f_{2}(i);i,j).

(S5.3)

Therefore
V^{\psi}(f_{1},f_{2})
depends on
\mathbb{P}
only via
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
’s as long as
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
. Let us denote
\mathbf{p}=(\mathbf{p}_{ij})
. From now on, we will denote
V^{\psi}(f_{1},f_{2})
by
V^{\psi}(f_{1},f_{2};\mathbf{p})
to make the dependence on the underlying probability distribution
\mathbb{P}
explicit.

We will introduce some more new notation. Since
f_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
f_{2}(i)\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
,
f_{1}
can be represented by a vector
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and each
f_{2}(i)
can be represented by a vector
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
,
i\in[k_{1}]
.
As
f_{2}=(f_{2}(1),\ldots,f_{2}(k_{1}))
, we can describe
f_{2}
by the
k_{1}k_{2}
-dimensional vector
\mathcal{Y}=(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
. For the rest of this proof, unless otherwise specified, we will use the notation
\mathbf{x}
instead of
f_{1}
and
(\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
or
\mathcal{Y}
instead of
f_{2}
unless otherwise specified. Therefore,

V^{\psi}(f_{1},f_{2};\mathbf{p})
will also be denoted by
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})
for the rest of this proof. Using the above notation, (
S5.3
) can be rewritten as

V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{p}_{ij}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i};i,j).

(S5.4)

Suppose
\argmax_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})
is non-empty for some
\mathbf{p}
and

\displaystyle(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{2}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))

\displaystyle\ \equiv(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{2}}^{*})

\displaystyle\ \in\argmax_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p}).

(S5.5)

Then for any
\mathbb{P}
corresponding to
\mathbf{p}
,
\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is a candidate for
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\mathbf{y}_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is a candidate for
\tilde{f}_{2}(i)
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
. If
\tilde{d}_{1}
and
\tilde{d}_{2}
are the policies generated by
\tilde{f}_{1}
and
\tilde{f}_{2}
, then
\tilde{d}_{1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
and
\tilde{d}_{2}(i)=\text{pred}(\mathbf{y}_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
for each
i\in[k_{1}]
. Since
V(d_{1},d_{2})=\mathbf{p}_{d_{1},d_{2}(d_{1})}
for any DTR
d
,
V(\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2})=\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}
.

For an arbitrary
\mathbf{p}
, there is no guarantee that the maximizer of
V^{\psi}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
exists.
However, Lemma
S5.2
, which is proved in Section
S5.4.2
, shows that

\argmax_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})
is none-empty when
\mathbf{p}_{ij}=1
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. We will later see that the existence of the maximizer for one
\mathbf{p}
is sufficient for showing the existence of maximizer for all
\mathbf{p}
’s.

Lemma S5.2
.

Suppose

\mathcal{C}_{e}=\bigg\{(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}},y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}},\ldots,y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}(1+k_{2})}:x,y\in\mathbb{R}\bigg\}

and
\mathbf{p}_{ij}=1
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
, the
V_{\psi}
defined in (
S5.4
) satisfies

\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})\supset\mathcal{C}_{e}.

We now consider a further subset
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{k_{1},k_{2}}\subset\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
so that for any
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
,
\mathbf{p}_{1,j}=\mathbf{p}_{12}
for all
j\geq 2
,
\mathbf{p}_{i,1}=\mathbf{p}_{21}
for all
i>1
, and
\mathbf{p}_{ij}=\mathbf{p}_{22}
for all
j>1
and
i>1
. Since it is possible to construct a
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
such that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{2}\mid A_{1}=i,A_{2}=j]=\mathbf{p}_{ij}
for any
\mathbf{p}\equiv{\mathbf{p}_{ij}}{i\in[k_{1}],j\in[k_{2}]}
with positive
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
values, it follows that for any
(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12},\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, there exists a
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
corresponding to these values. Since we will only consider
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{b}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
from now on, by an abuse of notation, we will denote
(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12},\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
by
\mathbf{p}
.
The surrogate loss reduces to

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{11}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1};1,1)+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1};1,j)

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i};i,1)+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i};i,j).

(S5.6)

Since
\psi
is PERM with template
\eta
, (
3.5
) implies that
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})
equals

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{11}-\mathbf{y}_{12},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{11}-\mathbf{y}_{1k_{2}})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1j}-\mathbf{y}_{11},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{1j}-\mathbf{y}_{1,k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{i1}-\mathbf{y}_{i2},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{i1}-\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{i1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}})

(S5.7)

where terms such as
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{ij}
are omitted from the argument of
\eta
.
Let us denote
\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{i}
for
i\in[2:k_{1}]
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij}=\mathbf{y}_{i1}-\mathbf{y}_{ij}
for
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[2:k_{2}]
. Observe that
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j}=\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1}+(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{j})=\Delta x_{j}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{ij^{\prime}}=\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{i1}+(\mathbf{y}_{i1}-\mathbf{y}_{ij^{\prime}})=\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij^{\prime}}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij}
. Let
\Delta\mathbf{x}=(\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}})
, and for
i\in[k_{1}]
, let
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}=(\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i2},\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}})
.
With this notation, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
, the quantity
V^{\psi}
reduces to

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\Delta\mathbf{x},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\eta(\Delta\mathbf{x},-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1j},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{12}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1j}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1k_{2}}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1j})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\eta(-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i},\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=2}^{k_{1}}\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\eta\begin{pmatrix}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i},\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{i},&\\
-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i2}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ij}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\Delta\mathbf{x},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\Delta\mathbf{x},-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1,1+j},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{12}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1,1+j}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1k_{2}}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1,1+j})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i},\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta\begin{pmatrix}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i},\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}}-\Delta\mathbf{x}_{1+i},-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i,1+j},\\
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i.2}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i,1+j}\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i,k_{2}}-\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1+i.1+j}\end{pmatrix}.

For
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
, by an slight abuse of notation, let us define the function

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},-\mathbf{v}_{1j},\mathbf{v}_{11}-\mathbf{v}_{1j}\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{v}_{1j})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{1+i})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta\begin{pmatrix}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},&\\
-\mathbf{v}_{1+i,j},\mathbf{v}_{1+i,1}-\mathbf{v}_{1+i,j}\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1+i,k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{v}_{1+i.j}\end{pmatrix},

(S5.8)

where terms such as
\mathbf{u}_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{i}
and
\mathbf{v}_{ij}-\mathbf{v}_{ij}
are omitted from the argument of
\eta
in the above definition. Note that
V^{\eta}
is different from
V^{\psi}
as the domain of
\psi
and
\eta
are different.
Since
\Delta\mathbf{x}=(\Delta\mathbf{x}_{2},\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}})
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}=(\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i2},\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}})
for
i\in[k_{1}]
, letting

\Delta x=\mathbf{u}
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{v}_{i}
, we can show that

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\Delta\mathbf{x},\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

(S5.9)

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{2}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
.
Therefore,
V^{\psi}
depends on
\mathbf{x}
and the
\mathbf{y}_{i}
’s only via the
\Delta\mathbf{x}
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}
’s. To maximize
V^{\psi}
with respect to
\mathbf{x}
and the
\mathbf{y}_{i}
’s, one actually need to maximize it over the
\Delta\mathbf{x}
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}
’s which are in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
, respectively.
If
\mathbf{u}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
maximizes
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
, then any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
with
\Delta\mathbf{x}=\mathbf{u}^{*}
and
\Delta\mathbf{y}_{i}=\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}
(
i\in[k_{1}]
) is a maximizer of
V^{\psi}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
. In particular

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}^{*}=(0,-\mathbf{u}^{*}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}=(0,-\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}=(0,-\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*})

(S5.10)

is a version of
(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
.
Alternatively,

(\Delta\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\Delta\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\Delta\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\in\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}}V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}})

(S5.11)

if
(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
exists. However, it is not obvious when

(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
exists.
To this end, first we derive a simpler expression for
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
.

Since
\eta
is blockwise permutation symmetric by (
3.4
), for any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
,

\eta(\mathbf{u},-\mathbf{w}_{j},\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{w}_{j}\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{w}_{j})=\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{w}_{j}\ldots,-\mathbf{w}_{j},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{w}_{j})

and

\eta(-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{w})

for any
i\in[k_{1}-1]
,
j\in[k_{2}-1]
,
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
. In the above, terms such as
\mathbf{u}_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{i}
and
\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j}
are omitted from the argument of
\eta
. Thus

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{11}-\mathbf{v}_{1j}\ldots,-\mathbf{v}_{1j},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1,k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{v}_{1j})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\mathbf{v}_{i+1})

(S5.12)

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta\begin{pmatrix}\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i}\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},&\\
\mathbf{v}_{i+1,1}-\mathbf{v}_{i+1,j}\ldots,-\mathbf{v}_{i+1,j},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{i+1,k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{v}_{i+1,j}.\end{pmatrix}

Let us define
\mathbb{A}_{0}=I_{k_{1}-1}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&\mathbf{0}^{T}_{k_{1}-2}\\
-\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}&I_{k_{1}-2}\end{bmatrix}\text{ and }\mathbb{A}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{i-1}&-\mathbf{1}_{i-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(i-1)\times(k_{1}-1-i)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{i-1}^{T}&-1&-\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1-i}^{T}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{1}-1-i)\times(i-1)}&-\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1-i}&I_{k_{1}-1-i}\end{bmatrix}

(S5.13)

for
i\in[2:k_{1}-1]

in case the set
[2:k_{1}-1]
is non-empty.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u}=(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{i},\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{i})\text{ for all }i\in[k_{1}].

One can verify that we can obtain
\mathbb{A}_{i}
from
\mathbb{A}_{j}
by swapping the
i
th column with the
jth
column and then swapping the ith row with the jth row. Therefore,
\mathbb{A}_{i}=P_{ij}\mathbb{A}_{j}P_{ij}
where, by a slight abuse of notation, we define
P_{ij}
to be the permutation matrix of appropriate dimesnion for swapping ith row with jth row. Also, we let
\mathbb{B}_{0}
be
I_{k_{2}-1}
and

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}-1&\mathbf{0}^{T}_{k_{2}-2}\\
-\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}&I_{k_{2}-2}\end{bmatrix}\text{ and }\mathbb{B}_{j}=\begin{bmatrix}I_{j-1}&-\mathbf{1}_{j-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(j-1)\times(k_{2}-1-j)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{j-1}^{T}&-1&-\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1-j}^{T}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{2}-1-j)\times(j-1)}&-\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1-j}&I_{k_{2}-1-j}\end{bmatrix}

(S5.14)

for
j\in[2:k_{2}-1]
in case the set
[2:k_{2}-1]
is non-empty.
It can similarly be shown that
\mathbb{B}_{j}=P_{1j}\mathbb{B}_{1}P_{1j}
. Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{j}\mathbf{w}=(\mathbf{w}_{1}-\mathbf{w}_{j},\ldots,-\mathbf{w}_{j},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{w}_{j})\text{ for any }\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}.

With this new notation, (
S5.12
) reduces to

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j}\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j}\mathbf{v}_{i+1})

(S5.15)

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
.

We will now show that the maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is attained for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.
If possible, suppose there exists
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
so that the maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is not attained.
Then Lemma
S5.3
implies that the maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is not attained for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. The proof of Lemma
S5.3
is provided in Section
S5.4.3
. Note that the function
f
in Lemma
S5.3
does not represent scores, and
h
denotes a function rather than a history.

Lemma S5.3
.

Let
r,k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
C_{1},\ldots,C_{m}
are matrices in
\mathbb{R}^{r\times k}
and
h:\mathbb{R}^{r}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is a convex and bounded below. Further suppose
\mathbf{0}_{k}\in\text{dom}(h)
. Define

f(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w})=\sum_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{w}_{i}h(C_{i}\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}.

Suppose there exists
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
so that the minimum of the convex function
f(\cdot;\mathbf{w}_{0})

is not attained in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then the minima of
f(\cdot;\mathbf{w})
is not attained in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for any
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
.

Here Lemma
S5.3
applies because
-\eta
is convex and bounded below and
\text{dom}(-\eta)

\ni\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
by Lemma
S5.1
. In particular, the maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is not attained when
\mathbf{p}_{11}=\mathbf{p}_{12}=\mathbf{p}_{21}=\mathbf{p}_{22}=1
. Then the maximizer of
V^{\psi}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is also not attained because otherwise (
S5.10
) would give a maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
. However, Lemma
S5.2
implies that
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}})
is a maximizer of
V^{\psi}
when the
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
’s are all one, which presents a contradiction. Therefore, the maximizer of
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is attained for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.
Since
\eta
is strictly concave,
V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is strictly concave, implying that the maximizer is unique. Let us denote this maximizer by
(\mathbf{u}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
. The following lemma, which is proved in Section
S5.4.4
, shows that the maximizer lies in the lower dimensional space

\begin{split}\mathcal{C}=\bigg\{(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}(k_{2}-1)}:\mathbf{u}=x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},\\
\ \mathbf{v}_{1}=y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ \mathbf{v}_{2}=\ldots=\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}=z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ \text{where }x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}\bigg\}.\end{split}

(S5.16)

Lemma S5.4
.

For
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, let

(\mathbf{u}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))=\argmax\limits_{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}}V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p}).

Then
(\mathbf{u}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\in\mathcal{C},

where
\mathcal{C}
is as defined in (
S5.16
).

For all
x
,
y
,
z\in\mathbb{R}
, let us define
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{1,2\}^{2}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
i as follows:

\displaystyle\vartheta(x,y;1,1)=

\displaystyle\ \eta(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\vartheta(x,y;1,2)=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},yB_{j}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\vartheta(x,y;2,1)=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(xA_{i}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\vartheta(x,y;2,2)=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(xA_{i}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},yB_{j}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}),

(S5.17)

where for
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
, and the
\mathbb{A}_{i}
and
\mathbb{B}_{j}
’s are as defined in (
S5.13
) and (
S5.14
), respectively.
For
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}
, define

\displaystyle\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p})=-\mathbf{p}_{11}\vartheta(x,y;1,1)-\mathbf{p}_{12}\vartheta(x,y;1,2)-\mathbf{p}_{21}\vartheta(x,z;2,1)-\mathbf{p}_{22}\vartheta(x,z;2,2).

(S5.18)

Note that
\Phi
is well defined because
\eta
is bounded above. If
\mathbf{u}=x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}
,

\mathbf{v}_{1}=y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
, and
\mathbf{v}_{2}=\ldots=\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}=z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
, then for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
and
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}
,

V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p}).

(S5.19)

Therefore
Lemma
S5.4
and (
S5.15
) imply that

\displaystyle\sup_{(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}})\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}(k_{2}-1)}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\sup_{x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p}),

It is possible to show that if
\psi
is Fisher consistent for the class
\mathcal{P}_{b}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
, then
\vartheta
induces a relative-margin-based Fisher consistent loss for 2-stage DTR problem with binary treatments. When we mention “relative-margin-based loss induced by
\vartheta
”, we refer to the loss defined by
\vartheta^{\text{loss}}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2};i,j)=\vartheta(x_{1}-x_{2},y_{1}-y_{2};i,j)
for all
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
. For the purpose of showing the contradiction in Step 2, it will be sufficient to show that the Fisher consistency of
\psi
induces a weaker version of Fisher consistency on
\vartheta^{\text{loss}}
or
\vartheta
, which is later elicited in Property
1
. Therefore, we will not get into the Fisher consistency of
\vartheta^{\text{loss}}(\cdot;i,j)
. However, before discussing the weaker version of Fisher consistency, we need to establish more properties of
\Phi
and the
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s.

Lemma
S5.5
establishes that
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
has a unique minimizer for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. This lemma also establishes that
-\vartheta
and
\Phi
are proper, closed, and strictly convex, where a proper convex function was defined in Section
S5.1
. Lemma
S5.5
is proved in Section
S5.4.5
.

Lemma S5.5
.

Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
, the
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
defined in (
S5.18
) has a unique minimizer over
\mathbb{R}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Moreover,

(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\equiv(x^{*},y^{*},z^{*})=\argmin\limits_{x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p}).

(S5.20)

satisfies
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, and
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{v}^{*}_{21}(\mathbf{p})
.
Moreover, the
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s, as well as
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
, are closed and proper strictly convex functions for all
i,j\in[2]
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Moreover,
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is 0-coercive for all
i,j\in[2]
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.

An important quantity for us will be

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p})),

which uniquely exists for each
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
by Lemma
S5.5
.
When
\mathbf{p}
is clear from the context, we will drop
\mathbf{p}
from the notation of
x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, and
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.

S5.3
Step 2

In this step, we will show that the
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
defined in (
S5.17
) satisfies some restrictions due to the concavity of
\vartheta
. Then we will show that,
if
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then
\vartheta
satisfies some properties, which violates the abovementioned restrictions. This contradiction would imply that
\psi
can not be Fisher consistent, thus completing the proof.

It is hard to find a closed form expression of
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
or analyze its behavior for any arbitrary
\mathbf{p}
. Especially, for an arbitrary
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, it is not guaranteed that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
lies in the interior or the relative interior of
\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
. However, if
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\notin\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
, we will need to deal with several pathological issues for applying the tools of convex analysis. Therefore, for the rest of the proof, we will focus on a neighborhood of a special
\mathbf{p}
, namely
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}^{0}=(1,1,1,1)
. We will later show that if
\mathbf{p}
is in a small neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
, which makes application of convex analysis tools easier. Lemma
S5.6
implies that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})=(0,0,0)
.

Lemma S5.6
.

Suppose
\mathbf{p}^{0}=(1,1,1,1)
. Then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})=(0,0,0)
.

Proof of Lemma
S5.6
.

This lemma follows trivially from (
S5.17
) and Lemma
S5.2
.

∎

We break down step 2 into further substeps.

2a.

In this step, we establish smoothness properties of
\vartheta
and related functions in neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

2b.

In this step, we establish the continuity properties of the map
\mathbf{p}\mapsto\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.
We will also show that
\mathbf{p}\mapsto y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
and
\mathbf{p}\mapsto z^{*}(\mathbf{p})
are locally Lipschitz at
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}^{0}
. These results enable control over the behavior of
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for
\mathbf{p}
’s in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

2c.

We will show that the concavity of
\vartheta
induces some restrictions on
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for all
\mathbf{p}
in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

2d.

We will show that if
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then
\vartheta
satisfies some properties, which will collectively be referred to as Property
1
.

2e.

We will use the restrictions on
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
derived in Step 2c to show that
\vartheta
can not satisfy Property
1
, which would imply
\psi
can not be Fisher consistent.

S5.3.1
Step 2a: properties of
\vartheta
and related functions

In this step, we will mainly establish different properties of
\vartheta
,
\Phi
, and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. The function
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is an important function for us because
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
relies on it and, as mentioned previously, the proof hinges on
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Another important function for us is

\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\inf_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p}),\ \mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}.

(S5.21)

Note that as
\Lambda^{*}(p)
is the solution to above minimization problem, we also have

\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})

for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Fact
S5.3
below implies that
-\Phi^{*}
is well-behaved in that it is a convex function that is continuous and bounded on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. This fact requires
-\eta
to be bounded below, which is satisfied under the setup of Theorem
3.1
. Fact
S5.3
is proved in Section
S5.4.6
.

Fact S5.3
.

Suppose
-\eta
is bounded below. Then

-\Phi^{*}
is a convex function with
\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\Phi^{*}))\supset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Also
\Phi^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Moreover,

|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\infty
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.

Lemma
S5.7
below implies that the
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are thrice continuously differentiable at
(0,0)
for
i,j\in[2]
. In particular, there is an open neighborhood
U_{0}
of
0
so that the
\vartheta(\cdot;i.j)
’s are thrice continuously differentiable at
U_{0}^{2}
for all
i,j\in[2]
and
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is thrice continuously differentiable at
U_{0}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.
Lemma
S5.7
is proved in Section
S5.4.7
.

Lemma S5.7
.

Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
,
there exists an open neighborhood
U_{0}
of
0
so that

1.

U_{0}^{2}\subset\text{int}(\text{dom}(\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)))
for all
i,j\in[2]
.

2.

U_{0}^{3}\subset\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})))
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.

3.

The
\vartheta(\cdot;i.j)
’s are thrice continuously differentiable at
U_{0}^{2}
for all
i,j\in[2]
.

4.

\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is thrice continuously differentiable at
U_{0}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.

S5.3.2
Step 2b: continuity properties of
\Lambda^{*}

In this section, we will prove the continuity properties of
\Lambda^{*}
. In particular, we will show that
\Lambda^{*}
is continuous. Moreover, we will show that
y^{*}
and
z^{*}
are locally Lipschitz at
\mathbf{p}^{0}
. Lemma
S5.8
proves the continuity of the map
\mathbf{p}\mapsto\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. This lemma is proved in Section
S5.4.8
.

Lemma S5.8
.

Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
,
\mathbf{p}\mapsto\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is a continuous map at any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.

One immediate corollary of the continuity of
\Lambda^{*}
is that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}
in a small neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

Lemma S5.9
.

Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
, there exists
\delta>0
so that if
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
, where
U_{0}
is as in Lemma
S5.7
.

Proof of Lemma
S5.9
.

Lemma
S5.6
implies that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})=(0,0,0)
. By the continuity of
\Lambda^{*}
by Lemma
S5.8
, we can choose
\delta
small enough so that if
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
.

∎

Lemma
S5.10
below establishes the Lipschitz continuity of
y^{*}
and
z^{*}
at
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

Lemma S5.10
.

Consider the setup of Theorem
3.1
. Then there exists a
\delta>0
and
C\geq 0
such that for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
,

|y^{*}(\mathbf{p})|\leq C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2}

and

|z^{*}(\mathbf{p})|\leq C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2}.

Lemma
S5.10
is proved in Section
S5.4.9
.
Since
\Lambda(\mathbf{p}^{0})=(0,0,0)
by Lemma
S5.6
, the above can also be re-written as

|y^{*}(\mathbf{p})-y^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})|\leq C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2}\text{ and }|z^{*}(\mathbf{p})-z^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})|\leq C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2},

which yields the Lipschitz continuity of
z^{*}
and
y^{*}
at
\mathbf{p}^{0}
.

S5.3.3
Step 2c: geometric restrictions on
\vartheta

In this step, we show that
\vartheta
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
need to satisfy a system of inequalities when
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
where
\delta
is smaller than the
\delta
in Lemma
S5.9
.
For such
\mathbf{p}
’s,
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
, which is necessary to guarantee the differentiability of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, as well as to ensure that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})))
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)))
for all
i,j\in[2]
.
Suppose
x\in U_{0}
, which indicates
(x,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\in U_{0}^{3}
. Therefore,
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is differentiable at
(x,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
by Lemma
S5.7
. Since
\Lambda^{*}(p)-(x,y^{*}(p),z^{*}(p))=(x^{*}(p)-x,0,0)
and
\Phi
is convex, from the definition of subdifferential
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, pp. 167, (1.2.1),)
, it follows that

\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})-\Phi(x,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(0);\mathbf{p})\geq(x^{*}(\mathbf{p})-x)\partialderivative{\Phi(x,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{x}.

In particular, if
x\neq x^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, then by strict convexity, the above inequality is strict.
Thus, we have for any
x\neq x^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,

(x^{*}(\mathbf{p})-x)\partialderivative{\Phi(x,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{x}<0.

Since
0\in U_{0}
, we can choose
x=0
, which implies that for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
,

x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\Phi(0,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{x}<0.

(S5.22)

Similarly, we can show that

\displaystyle z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\Phi(x^*(\mp),y^*(\mp),0;\mp)}{z}<

\displaystyle\ 0,

\displaystyle y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\Phi(x^*(\mp),0,z^*(\mp);\mp)}{y}<

\displaystyle\ 0

(S5.23)

for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. Note that the concavity of
\vartheta
, which led to the convexity of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
, is the main reason behind the above inequalities.

Now we will calculate the partial derivatives. To this end, note that

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\Phi(x^*(\mp),0,z^*(\mp);\mp)}{y}=

\displaystyle\ -\mathbf{p}_{11}\partialderivative{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),0;1,1)}{y}-\mathbf{p}_{12}\partialderivative{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),0; 1,2)}{y}

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\Phi(x^*(\mp),z^*(\mp),0;\mp)}{z}=

\displaystyle\ -\mathbf{p}_{21}\partialderivative{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),0;2,1)}{z}-\mathbf{p}_{22}\partialderivative{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),0; 2,2)}{z}

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\Phi(0,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{x}=

\displaystyle\ -\mathbf{p}_{11}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,y^*(\mp);1,1)}{x}-\mathbf{p}_{12}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,y^*(\mp); 1,2)}{x}

\displaystyle\ -\mathbf{p}_{21}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,z^*(\mp);2,1)}{x}-\mathbf{p}_{22}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,z^*(\mp); 2,2)}{x}

(S5.24)

for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
, provided
\delta
is smaller than the
\delta
in Lemma
S5.9
.
The inequalities (
S5.22
) and (
S5.23
) entail a restriction on
\vartheta
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.

S5.3.4
Step 2d: implication of Fisher consistency

In this section, we will show that the Fisher consistency of
\psi
enforces some restriction on
\vartheta
. To this end, we define a set of conditions, which we will refer to us Property
1
. One may think of these conditions as a weak version Fisher consistency.
As mentioned earlier, one can show that when
\psi
is Fisher consistent, the margin-based loss defined by
\vartheta^{\text{loss}}(x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2};i,j)=\vartheta(x_{1}-x_{2},y_{1}-y_{2};i,j)
for all
x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
is Fisher consistent in the two-stage binary treatment case. However, to prove this theorem, we do not need to show the above result, which would be technically more cumbersome. For the purpose of this proof, it is enough to show that, when
\psi
is Fisher consistent,
\vartheta
satisfies some conditions, which are collected in Property
1
.

Property 1
.

The function
\vartheta:\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\{1,2\}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies Property
1
if there exists
\delta>0
so that
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, and
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})
defined in (
S5.20
) satisfy the following for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
:

P1.

If
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
, then
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
. Also, if
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})<\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
, then
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
.

P2.

Suppose
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
. Then then
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
if
\mathbf{p}_{11}>\mathbf{p}_{12}
and
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
if
\mathbf{p}_{11}<\mathbf{p}_{12}
. Suppose
\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})
. Then
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
if
\mathbf{p}_{21}>\mathbf{p}_{22}
and
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
if
\mathbf{p}_{21}<\mathbf{p}_{22}
.

Lemma
S5.11
, which is proved in Section
S5.4.10
, entails that
\vartheta
satisfies Property
1
if
\psi
is Fisher consistent.

Lemma S5.11
.

If
\psi
is fisher consistent, then the
\vartheta
defined in (
S5.17
) satisfies Property
1
under the setup of Theorem
3.1

Lemma
S5.11
implies that to prove that
\psi
is not Fisher consistent, it suffices to show that
\vartheta
fails to satisfy Property
1
.
We have
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
in Property
1
because in this case Lemma
S5.9
ensures that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U^{2}_{0}
for sufficiently small
\delta>0
, ,which, in combination with Lemma
S5.7
, yields that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
. This ensures that we can use tools of convex analysis on
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
to prove Lemma
S5.11
. Unless
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\notin\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
, showing P1 and P2 of Property
1
is difficult for arbitrary
\mathbf{p}
’s. Proving Lemma
S5.11
, which relies on the continuity of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, is challenging in this case.

S5.3.5
Step 2e: proving the contradiction

This is the final step of the proof. In this step, we will show that
\vartheta
can not satisfy Property
1
. Then Lemma
S5.11
would imply that
\psi
can not be Fisher consistent, completing the proof.
Since
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
is differentiable at
(0,0)
by Lemma
S5.7
for all
i,j\in[2]
, at least one of the following three cases must hold: (i)
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,1)/\partial x
,
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x
,
\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,1)/\partial x
, and
\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,2)/\partial x
are all zero,
(ii) at least one number between
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,1)/\partial x
and
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x
is non-zero, and (iii) at least one number between
\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,1)/\partial x
and
\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,2)/\partial x
is non-zero. We will show that in none of theses cases,
\vartheta
satisfies Property
1
. Since the proofs are similar for case (ii) and (iii), we will show the proof only for case (i) and (ii).

Case (i):
\partial\vartheta(0,0;i,j)/\partial x
’s are zero for all
i,j\in[2]

The following lemma implies that if the
\partial\vartheta(0,0;i,j)/\partial x
’s are all zero, then
\vartheta(0,0;i,j)
’s can not satisfy Property
1
.

Lemma S5.12
.

Under the setup of Theorem
3.1
, if

\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,1)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,1)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,2)/\partial x=0,

then the
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s can not satisfy Property
1
.

Proof of Lemma
S5.12
.

Note that
\partial\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p})/\partial x=0
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
if

\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,1)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,1)/\partial x=\partial\vartheta(0,0;2,2)/\partial x=0.

Consider
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
of the form
(c_{1},c_{1},c_{2},c_{2})
where
c_{1}>c_{2}>0
and
\mathbf{p}
. If
\vartheta
satisfies Property
1
, then
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
for all such
\mathbf{p}
provided
c_{1}
and
c_{2}
are sufficiently close to one. However, for
\mathbf{p}
’s of such form, (
S5.46
) implies that
\partial\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p})/\partial y=0
and
\partial\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p})/\partial z=0
.
Therefore,
\gradient\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p})=0
. Since
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is strictly convex for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
,
\gradient\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p})=0
implies
(0,0,0)
is the unique minimizer of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Theorem 2.2.1,
pp. 177,)
. Therefore, for our chosen
\mathbf{p}
,
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,0,0)
or
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
although
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
. Given any
\delta>0
, we can find
c_{1}>c_{2}>0
such that
(c_{1},c_{1},c_{2},c_{2})\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. Therefore, Property
1
can not hold.
∎

Case (ii):
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,1)/\partial x
and/or
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x
is non-zero

Our first task is to choose a
\delta_{0}>0
. The choice of
\delta
is explained below.
Since the
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are thrice continuously differentiable at (0,0) by Lemma
S5.7
,
there exists
\epsilon_{0}>0
so that

forall

.ByLemma\ref{lemma: CC: continuity of Lambda},thereexists

\bigg|\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{2};1,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|<\bigg|{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|+1.\\
\bigg|\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{3};2,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|<\bigg|{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,0;2,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|+1.\\
\bigg|\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{4};2,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|<\bigg|{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,0;2,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\bigg|+1.\end{gathered}Inaddition,since$\delta_{0}$ischosentobesmallerthanone,$|\Delta_{1}|,|\Delta_{2}|<1$.Thus\begin{aligned} &(1+\Delta_{1})\frac{y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{1};1,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}+(1+\Delta_{2})\frac{y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{2};1,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\\
&\ +\frac{z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{3};2,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}+\frac{z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{4};2,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\leq C(y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}+z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2})\end{aligned}wheretheconstant$C>0$dependsonlyon$\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)$^{\prime}s.However,byLemma\ref{lemma: CC: Lipschitz},$y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}$and$z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}$areboundedby$C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2}^{2}=C(\Delta_{1}^{2}+\Delta_{2}^{2})$where$C>0$isaconstantdependingonthe$\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)$^{\prime}s.Therefore,\begin{aligned} &(1+\Delta_{1})\frac{y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{1};1,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}+(1+\Delta_{2})\frac{y^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{2};1,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\\
&\ +\frac{z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{3};2,1)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}+\frac{z^{*}(\mathbf{p})^{2}}{2}{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(0,\xi_{4};2,2)}{\partial{}y^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial x}}\leq C(\Delta_{1}^{2}+\Delta_{2}^{2}).\end{aligned}Theabove,combinedwith\eqref{intheorem: cc: main proof big expansion inequality}implies\eqref{intheorem: cc: final: key inequality}.\qed\end@proof\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par\par Nowwearereadytoshowthatundercase(ii),
ϑ
doesnotsatisfyProperty\ref{property: CC}.Wewilldividecase(ii)intwosubcases:(a)either
∂
ϑ
(0,0;1,1)/∂x¡ 0
or

ϑ
(0,0;1,2)/∂x¡ 0
and(b)either
∂
ϑ
(0,0;1,1)/∂x¿ 0

∂
