(0,0;1,2)/∂x¿ 0
.If(ii)holdstheneither(ii)aor(ii)bneedstohold.Therecanbeoverlapbetweencases(a)and(b),butitwillnotaffecttheproof.Wewillshowthat,if
ϑ
satisfiesProperty\ref{property: CC},thenwewillencountercontradictionineithercases.\par

Case (ii) a:
\partial{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}/\partial{x}<0
or
\partial{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}/\partial{x}<0

First consider the case
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}<0
. If possible, suppose Property
1
holds.
Consider
\Delta_{1}=1/k
and
\Delta_{2}=1/k^{2}
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
is a large number so that the resulting
\mathbf{p}_{k}=(1+1/k,1+1/k^{2},1,1)
is in
B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{0})
. Observe that
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{p}_{k}
satisfies
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
. Therefore Property
1
implies
x^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Hence, (
) holds by Lemma
. For this
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
, (
) reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{k}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big)>0

for all
\mathbf{p}_{k}
of the form
(1+1/k,1+1/k^{2},1,1)
for all sufficiently large
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Multiplying both sides with
k
, we get

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{k}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big)>0.

Letting
k\to\infty
, and observing Lemma
S5.8
implies
y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\to 0
as
k\to\infty
we obtain

\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}\geq 0,

which leads to a contradiction.
Therefore,
\vartheta
can not satisfy Property
1
if

\partial{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}/\partial{x}<0
.
Similarly, we can show that
\vartheta
can not satisfy Property
1
if
\partial{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}/\partial{x}<0
. by taking
\Delta_{1}=1/k^{2}
and
\Delta_{2}=1/k
. Therefore, we have showed that if either
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}<0
or
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}<0
, Property
1
can not hold.

Case ii(b):
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}>0
or
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}>0

First consider the case
\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}>0
. If possible, suppose
\vartheta
satisfies Property
1
. Take
\Delta_{1}=-1/k
and
\Delta_{2}=1/k^{2}
. Let us refer to the resulting sequence
(1-1/k,1+1/k^{2},1,1)
by
\mathbf{p}_{k}
. Note that
\mathbf{p}_{k}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{0})
if
k
is sufficiently large and
x^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})>0
for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
by Property
1
. Then (
) holds by Lemma
.
In this case, (
) reduces to

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{k}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}{x}-C\frac{1}{k}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big)>0

for all sufficiently large
k
.
Multiplying both sides by
k
, we get

\displaystyle-\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}{x}-C\frac{1}{k}\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\frac{1}{k}\Big(\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}+y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}{x}+C\frac{1}{k^{2}}\Big)>0.

Letting
k\to\infty
and observing
y^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{k})\to 0
by Lemma
S5.8
, we obtain that

\displaystyle-\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}\geq 0,

which is a contradiction. Therefore,
\vartheta
can not satisfy Property
1
in this case. The proof for the
\partial\vartheta(0,0;1,2)/\partial x>0
follows similarly by taking
\Delta_{1}=1/k^{2}
and
\Delta_{2}=-1/k
and letting
k\to\infty
.

S5.4
Proofs of main lemmas

S5.4.1
Proof of Lemma
S5.1

Proof of Lemma
S5.1
.

In this proof, we will work with
-\eta
because it is convex.
Because
\cap_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\cap_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\text{int}(\text{dom}(\psi(\cdot;i,j))\neq\emptyset
, there exist
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
so that

(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\psi(\cdot;i,j))
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
.
Let us fix
i\in[k_{1}]
. Our first task is to show that
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
.
Let us denote

\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}=(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i-1},\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}}).

Then for all
j\in[k_{2}]
,

\displaystyle-\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}})<\infty

where the term
\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{i}
is omitted.
Since
\eta
is blockwise permutation symmetric by (
S5.1
), for all
j\in[2:k_{2}]
,

\displaystyle-\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+2}\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}})<\infty.

For the sake of algebra, we will introduce
\mathbf{w}_{j}
for
j>k_{2}
. We extend the definition of
\mathbf{w}_{j}
to
\mathbb{N}
letting
\mathbf{w}_{j}=\mathbf{w}_{j\mod k_{2}}
for all
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Therefore, we can write
\mathbf{w}_{1}=\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}+1}
,
\ldots
,
\mathbf{w}_{j-1}=\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1}
, and so on. According to our notation

\displaystyle\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j-1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1})

for all
j\in[k_{2}]
. Therefore, using this notation,

\displaystyle-\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1})<\infty

(S5.26)

for all
j\in[k_{2}]
.
On the other hand, since
-\eta
is convex, Jensen inequality implies that

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{k_{2}}\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ -\eta\bigg(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\frac{\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1})}{k_{2}},\ldots,\frac{\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1})}{k_{2}}\bigg)

(S5.27)

where
\mathbf{w}_{j}=\mathbf{w}_{j\mod 2}
as per our notation. Moreover,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1})=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j+1}=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}+1}=0

because
\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}+1}=\mathbf{w}_{1}
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1})=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1}=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}},

which vanishes because
\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j+k_{2}-1}=\sum_{j=2}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j-1}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\mathbf{w}_{j}
. In general, for any
1\leq j\leq k_{2}-1
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j+j}=\sum_{j=1}^{k_{2}-j}\mathbf{w}_{j+j}+\sum_{j=k_{2}-j+1}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j+j}=\sum_{j=j+1}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j}+\sum_{j=k_{2}+1}^{k_{2}+j}\mathbf{w}_{j}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j=j+1}^{k_{2}}\mathbf{w}_{j}+\sum_{j=1}^{j}\mathbf{w}_{j}=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbf{w}_{j}.

Therefore,
\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]}(\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+j})=0
for any
1\leq j\leq k_{2}-1
, which, combined with (
S5.26
) and (
S5.27
), implies that

-\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})<\infty
.
Since
i
was arbitrary, we obtain
-\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})<\infty
for each
i\in[k_{1}]
.

For each
i\in[k_{1}]
, the block-wise permutation-symmetry of
\eta
as in (
S5.1
) implies that

\displaystyle\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})=\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1}),

where for
i>k_{1}
,
\mathbf{x}_{i}=\mathbf{x}_{i\mod k_{1}}
. Since

-\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})<\infty

for all
i\in[k_{1}]
, by Jensen’s inequality, it follows that

\displaystyle\infty>

\displaystyle\ -\frac{1}{k_{2}}\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{2}]}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ -\eta\left(\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1})}{k_{1}},\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+2})}{k_{1}},\ldots,\frac{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+k_{1}-1})}{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1}\right).

Proceeding as in the case for
\mathbf{w}
, we can prove that the right hand side of the above inequality equals
-\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-2})
. Thus we have shown that
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{dom}(-\eta)
.

It remains to prove
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
, which will be proved by contradiction.
If possible, suppose
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\notin\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
. Then we must have
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\partial(\text{dom}(-\eta))
. We claim that, in that case, there exists an open orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
, which we will soon define, whose intersection with
\text{dom}(-\eta)
is the nullset. For any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\bm{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k}
, we define the open orthant
\mathcal{Q}(\bm{\epsilon})
in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as

\mathcal{Q}(\bm{\epsilon})=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\epsilon_{i}>0\text{ for all }i\in[k]\}.

Note that an open orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
is of the form
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
where
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})
is an open orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
corresponding to some
\bm{\epsilon}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
is an open orthant in
\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
corresponding to some
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k_{2}-1}
.

Fact S5.4
.

If
\eta
is concave with
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\partial(\text{dom}(-\eta))
, then there exist
\bm{\epsilon},\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k}
so that the open orthant
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
satisfies
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})\cap\text{dom}(-\eta)=\emptyset
.

Fact
S5.4
is proved in Section
S5.5.4
. We will prove that under the setup of the current lemma,
\{\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})\}\cap\text{dom}(-\eta)\neq\emptyset
for all
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}
in
\{\pm 1\}^{k}
, which will lead to the desired contradiction. Fix
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}
in
\{\pm 1\}^{k}
. We denote the number of positive elements in
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}
by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, respectively. Since
\cap_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\cap_{j=1}^{k_{2}}\text{int}(\text{dom}(\psi(\cdot;i,j))\neq\emptyset
, there is an open set
U\subset\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}
so that for all
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w})\in U
,
-\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w};i,j)<\infty
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. In particular, we can choose
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
such that the elements in
\mathbf{x}
and
\mathbf{w}
are all distinct.
Suppose
r\in[k_{2}]
is such that
\mathbf{w}_{r}=\mathbf{w}_{(a_{2}+1)}
.
Since
\mathbf{w}
has distinct elements,
r
is unique and there are
a_{2}
many
j
’s such that
\mathbf{w}_{j}<\mathbf{w}_{r}
and
k_{2}-1-a_{2}
many
\mathbf{w}_{j}
’s are strictly greater than
\mathbf{w}_{r}
. Note that

\displaystyle\infty>-\psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w};i,j)=

\displaystyle\ -\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}})

where the term
\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j}
is omitted.
The vector
\mathbf{v}=(\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{i}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}})
has exactly
a_{2}
many positive and
k_{2}-1-a_{2}
many negative elements. Therefore, we can permute the vector
\mathbf{v}
in a way so that
\upsilon(\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
. However, by (
S5.1
),

\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v})=\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}))

for any permutation
\upsilon:[k_{2}-1]\mapsto[k_{2}-1]
. Therefore, there is a permutation
\upsilon
so that
\upsilon(\mathbf{v})\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
and
(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}))\in\text{dom}(-\eta)
both holds. Thus we have shown that
for each
i\in[k_{1}]
, there exists
\mathbf{v}_{i}\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
such that

\displaystyle(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{i})\in\text{dom}(-\eta).

Since the elements of
\mathbf{x}
is distinct, there is an
i\in[k_{1}]
so that
\mathbf{x}_{(a_{1}+1)}=\mathbf{x}_{i}
. Then
a_{1}
many elements of
\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}
is positive, and the rest are negative. The block permutation symmetry of (
S5.1
)implies that
\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{i})=\eta(\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}_{i})
for any permutation
\upsilon:[k_{1}]\mapsto[k_{1}]
. In particular, we can choose
\upsilon
in a way so that
\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})
. Therefore,
\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x})\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})
and
-\eta(\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}_{i})<\infty
. This implies
(\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}_{i})\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
. However, since
\eta(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x},\mathbf{v}_{i})=\eta(\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}_{i})
, it holds that
-\eta(\upsilon(\Delta_{i}\mathbf{x}),\mathbf{v}_{i})<\infty
. Hence
\{\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})\}\cap\text{dom}(-\eta)
is non-empty. Then Fact
S5.4
implies
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\notin\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
can not hold. Hence, we must have
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
.

∎

S5.4.2
Proof of Lemma
S5.2

Proof of Lemma
S5.2
.

Note that (
S5.7
) implies when
\mathbf{p}_{ij}=1
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
, then

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{i1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{ij}-\mathbf{y}_{ik_{2}}),

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
.
Note that if
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})\in\mathcal{C}_{e}
, then

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})=V^{\psi}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}};\mathbf{p}).

Since
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
by Lemma
S5.1
, the above implies that
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-V^{\psi}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})))
.
The above display also implies that the proof will be complete if we can show that
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}})
lies in

\argmax_{\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}}V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})
. Note that
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}})
is just the zero-vector
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
and
(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})

is just the zero-vector
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
. To streamline notation, we will use the expressions
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
and
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
for those vectors from now on.

Let us denote
\mathbb{G}=-V^{\psi}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
so that
\mathbb{G}
is a convex function with
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}\in\text{dom}(\mathbb{G})
. Thus we need to show that
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
is a minimizer of
\mathbb{G}
. Hence, it is enough to prove
\partial\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}})\ni\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, Theorem 2.2.1, pp. 177,)
. In particular, we will show that
\partial\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}})
is singletone, and equals
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
. For
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
, let us define

\displaystyle\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w})=-\eta(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k_{1}},\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k_{2}})

\displaystyle\ \ \text{and}\ \mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})=\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i}),

(S5.28)

where
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
,
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
,
\mathbf{y}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
for
i\in[k_{1}]
, and the terms
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i}
and
\mathbf{y}_{j}-\mathbf{y}_{j}
are omitted in the above definitions. Then by Theorem 4.1.1 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
,

\displaystyle\partial\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})=\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})

where for sets
C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}
, the sum
C_{1}+\ldots+C_{n}
is the Minkowski sum defined in Section
S3
.
If
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
lies in
\text{int}(\text{dom}(\mathbb{H}_{ij}))
for some
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
, then the corresponding
\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
is non-empty
(see theorem 23.4, pp.214, of
Rockafellar, 1970
, for a proof)
. Otherwise, it is just an empty set. Thus the above expression continues to hold even if
(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})\notin\text{int}(\text{dom}(\mathbb{H}_{ij}))
for some
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
.

Consider
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. Equation
S5.28
implies that among the
\mathbf{y}_{r}
’s (
r\in[k_{1}]
),
\mathbb{H}_{ij}
depends only on
\mathbf{y}_{i}
. Hence, if
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k_{1}})\in\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
for some
\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{w}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
, then
\mathbf{w}_{r}=0
for
r\neq i

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Remark 4.1.2, pp. 184,)
.

Moreover,
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}}\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{i},\ldots,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}})
is in
\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
if and only if
(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w}_{i})\in\partial\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i})
. Using the above, we can specify
\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{y}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
as follows:

\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{1},\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{.\hfil.\hfil.},\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}})=\{(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}}\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{.\hfil.\hfil.},\mathbf{w},\makebox[10.00002pt][c]{.\hfil.\hfil.},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}}):(\mathbf{t},\mathbf{w})\in\partial\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}_{i}),\mathbf{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}},\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}\}.

(S5.29)

Therefore, we need to find
\partial\tilde{\phi}_{ij}
if we want to infer on
\partial\mathbb{H}_{ij}
. Equation

S5.28
also implies that for each
i,j
,
\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y})=h(C_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{y}))
where
C_{ij}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbb{A}_{i}&\mathbf{0}_{(k_{1}-1)\times k_{2}}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{2}-1)\times k_{1}}&\mathbb{B}_{j}\end{bmatrix}

is a matrix with
\mathbb{A}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k_{1}-1)}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbb{B}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{(k_{2}-1)}\times\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
satisfying

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{x}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i-1}\\
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{i+1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{k-1}\end{bmatrix}\quad\text{and}\quad\mathbb{B}_{j}\mathbf{w}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j-1}\\
\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{j+1}\\
\vdots\\
\mathbf{w}_{j}-\mathbf{w}_{k-2}\end{bmatrix}

for any
i\in[k_{1}]
,
j\in[k_{2}]
,
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
, and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
. The above implies

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{A}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}&-I_{k_{1}-1}\end{bmatrix},\ \mathbb{A}_{k_{1}}=\begin{bmatrix}-I_{k_{1}-1}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{B}_{1}=\begin{bmatrix}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}&-I_{k_{2}-1}\end{bmatrix},\ \mathbb{B}_{k_{2}}=\begin{bmatrix}-I_{k_{2}-1}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\end{bmatrix},

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{A}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{I}_{i-1}&\mathbf{1}_{i-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(i-1)\times(k_{1}-i)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{1}-i)\times(i-1)}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-i}&-\mathbf{I}_{k_{1}-i}\end{bmatrix},

(S5.30)

and

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{B}_{j}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{I}_{j-1}&\mathbf{1}_{j-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(j-1)\times(k_{2}-i)}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{2}-j)\times(j-1)}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-j}&-\mathbf{I}_{k_{2}-j}\end{bmatrix}

for
i\in[2:k_{1}-1]
and
j\in[2:k_{2}-1]
. Here we did a slight overload of notation because
\mathbb{A}_{i}
and
\mathbb{B}_{j}
’s are different from those defined in Section
S5.2
.
Since
k_{1},k_{2}\geq 2
by our assumption on
k_{1}
and
k_{2}
, the above matrices are well-defined.
Therefore,
\partial\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w})=C_{ij}^{T}\partial(-\eta)(C_{ij}(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{w}))
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Theorem 4.2.1 of)
. Since
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
by Lemma
S5.1
and
-\eta
is differentiable by our assumption, it follows that
-\eta
is differentiable at
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
. Thus
\partial(-\eta)(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})
is singletone and equals
-\gradient\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})
. Therefore,
\tilde{\phi}_{ij}
is differentiable at
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}}
and

\displaystyle\gradient\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}})=-C_{ij}^{T}\gradient\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}).

(S5.31)

Equation
S5.28
implies,
\mathbb{H}_{ij}
, and consequently,
\mathbb{G}=-V^{\psi}
, also are differentiable at
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
.

We will calculate
\gradient\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})
now. To this end, we will use (
S5.1
), which implies

\eta(\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})=\eta(\mathbf{u}_{2},u_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})\text{ for any }\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}\text{ and }\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1},

leading to

\partialderivative{\eta(\mbu_1,\mbu_2,\ldots,\mbu_{k_1-1},\mv)}{\mbu_1}=\partialderivative{\eta(\mbu_2,\mbu_1,\ldots,\mbu_{k_1-1},\mv)}{\mbu_1}

whenever
\eta
is differentiable at
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
. Therefore,

\eta_{1}(\mathbf{u}_{1},\mathbf{u}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})=\eta_{2}(\mathbf{u}_{2},\mathbf{u}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v}).

In particular, when
\mathbf{u}=\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1}
, then it follows that
\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})=\eta_{2}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})
. In the same way, we can show that
\eta_{i}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v})
is constant across
i\in[k_{1}-1]
. Similarly, we can show that
\eta_{k_{1}-1+j}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})
is constant across
j\in[k_{2}-1]
. In particular,

\eta_{i}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})=\eta_{1}\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\text{ for all }i\in[k_{1}]\text{ and }\eta_{j}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})=\eta_{k_{1}}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})

for all
j\in[k_{1}:k_{1}+k_{2}-2]
.
Thus

\gradient\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})=(\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},\eta_{k_{1}}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}).

Then (
S5.31
) implies that

\gradient\tilde{\phi}_{ij}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}})=-C_{ij}^{T}\gradient\eta(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})=-\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}\\
\eta_{k_{1}}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\end{bmatrix}.

We have already shown that
\mathbb{G}
is differentiable at
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
.
It remains to calculate
\gradient\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}})
. To this end, (
S5.28
) and (
S5.29
) imply that

\displaystyle\gradient\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}})=

\displaystyle\ -\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\\
\eta_{k_{1}+1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\ (i\text{th position})\\
0\\
\vdots\\
0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\begin{bmatrix}\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}\\
\eta_{k_{1}+1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\ \\
\vdots\\
\eta_{k_{1}+1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ -\begin{bmatrix}k_{2}\eta_{1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}\\
\eta_{k_{1}+1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\ \\
\vdots\\
h_{k_{1}+1}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2})\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}\end{bmatrix}.

Note that
(
S5.30
) implies that

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{I}_{i-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(i-1)\times(k_{1}-i)}\\
\mathbf{1}_{i-1}^{T}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-i}^{T}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{1}-i)\times(i-1)}&-\mathbf{I}_{k_{1}-i}\end{bmatrix}\ \text{and}\ \mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{I}_{j-1}&\mathbf{0}_{(j-1)\times(j-1)}\\
\mathbf{1}_{j-1}^{T}&\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-j}^{T}\\
\mathbf{0}_{(k_{2}-j)\times(j-1)}&-\mathbf{I}_{k_{2}-j}\end{bmatrix}.

Hence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{1}_{i-1}\\
k_{1}-1\\
-\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-i}\end{bmatrix}\text{ and }\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}=\begin{bmatrix}-\mathbf{1}_{j-1}\\
k_{2}-1\\
-\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-j}\end{bmatrix}.

Thus
\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1}]}\mathbb{A}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}=\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}}
and
\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{2}]}\mathbb{B}_{j}^{T}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}=\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}}
, which indicates

\gradient\mathbb{G}(\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}})=\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{1}k_{2}}
, which completes the proof.
∎

S5.4.3
Proof of Lemma
S5.3

Proof of Lemma
S5.3
.

Since
\mathbf{0}_{k}\in\text{dom}(h)
and
\mathbf{0}_{k}\in\text{dom}(h)\cap\text{Range}(C_{i})
for each
i\in[m]
,
(h\circ C_{i})^{\prime}_{\infty}=h_{\infty}^{\prime}\circ C_{i}
for each
i\in[m]
by part (c) of Fact
S5.2
. Using part (a) of the same Fact, we obtain that for each
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
,

f_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w})=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{w}_{i}h_{\infty}^{\prime}(C_{i}\mathbf{x}),\quad\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},

where
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(\cdot;\mathbf{w})
is the recession function of
f(\cdot;\mathbf{w})
. Since
h
is convex and bounded below,
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(\cdot;\mathbf{w})
is convex and bounded below for each
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
. If there exists
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
so that the minima of the convex function
f(\cdot;\mathbf{w}_{0})

is not attained in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then
f_{\infty}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{w}_{0})=0
for some
\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
by Fact
S5.1
. Since
\mathbf{w}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}
, it must hold that
h_{\infty}^{\prime}(C_{i}\mathbf{x})=0
for each
i\in[m]
for this
\mathbf{x}
.
Therefore,

\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{w}_{i}h_{\infty}^{\prime}(C_{i}\mathbf{x})=0\quad\text{ for all }\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}_{>0}.

Another application of Fact
S5.1
yields that the minima of the convex function
\mathbf{x}\mapsto\sum\limits_{i=1}^{m}\mathbf{w}_{i}h(C_{i}\mathbf{x})
is not attained for any
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
. Hence, proved.
∎

S5.4.4
Proof of Lemma
S5.4

Proof of Lemma
S5.4
.

We will fix
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
and denote
\mathbf{u}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
by
\mathbf{u}^{*}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}
, respectively.
We will prove Lemma
S5.4
in three steps.

(a)

We will show that there exists
x\in\mathbb{R}
so that
\mathbf{u}^{*}=x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}
.

(b)

We will next show that

\mathbf{v}_{2}^{*}=\ldots=\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}
.

(c)

Finally, we will show that

\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}=c\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
for each
i\in[k_{1}]
.

Proving (a) the
\mathbf{u}_{i}^{*}
’s are equal

For fixed
i
and
j
in
[k_{1}]
, let us denote
C_{ij}=\{\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}:\mathbf{u}_{i}=\mathbf{u}_{j}\}
. We will first show that
\mathbf{u}^{*}\in C_{ij}
for all
i,j\in[k_{1}]
. Since the proof is trivial for
i=j
, we assume
i\neq j
.

Fix
i,j\in[k_{1}]
so that
i\neq j
.
Let us denote by
\upsilon
the permutation on
[k_{1}]
that swaps
i
with
j
, i.e.,
\upsilon(i)=j
and
\upsilon(j)=i
. To show
\mathbf{u}^{*}\in C_{ij}
, our first step is to prove

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})

(S5.32)

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
.
To that end, note that
\upsilon\mathbf{u}=P_{ij}\mathbf{u}
where
P_{ij}
is the permutation matrix for swapping
i
th row with
j
th row.
For any
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
, it follows that

\eta(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})

by the blockwise permutation symmetry of
\eta
in (
S5.1
). Also,

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}P_{ij}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\eta(P_{ij}\mathbb{A}_{j}P_{ij}^{2}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\eta(P_{ij}\mathbb{A}_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\eta(P_{ij}^{2}\mathbb{A}_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}),

which equals
\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
,
where (a) uses the fact that
\mathbb{A}_{i}=P_{ij}A_{j}P_{ij}
for any
i,j\in[k_{1}]
, (b) uses the fact that
P_{ij}^{2}=I
and (c) uses the blockwise permutation symmetry of
\eta
as in (
S5.1
). Similarly we can show that
\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
.
Now suppose
r\neq i\neq j
. Consider the case
i<r<j
. Then for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
,

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbb{A}_{r}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbb{A}_{r}P_{ij}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{1}-\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,-\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{i}-\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{j}-\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\upsilon\mathbf{u}_{r},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{j}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \eta(\mathbf{u}_{1}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{i}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{j}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\ldots,\mathbf{u}_{k_{1}-1}-\mathbf{u}_{r},\mathbf{w})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \eta(\mathbb{A}_{r}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w}),

where (a) follows by the blockwise permutation symmetry of
\eta
. For all other orderings of the triplet
(i,r,j)
, we can similarly prove that
\eta(\mathbb{A}_{r}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})=\eta(\mathbb{A}_{r}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{w})
.
Therefore (
S5.15
) implies that

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1}).

By our previous calculations,

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

and

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{21}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i+1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{j+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{j+1})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{21}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{j}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i+1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{j+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{j+1}),

which, noting
j=\upsilon(i)
and
i=\upsilon(j)
, equals

\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1}).

Combining all the above pieces, we have

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in[k_{1}]:i^{\prime}\neq i,j}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}\in(i,j)}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1}),

which can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{1})

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1,}^{k_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(i^{\prime})+1})

because
\upsilon(i^{\prime})=i^{\prime}
for
i^{\prime}\neq(i,j)
. However, the above implies

V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})

(S5.33)

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
.
Now observe that since
\upsilon=\upsilon^{-1}
, we have

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p}).

However, (
S5.33
) applied on
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(k_{1}-1)+1})
leads to

V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon^{-1}(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p}),

which proves (
S5.32
).
Now we will prove that
\mathbf{u}^{*}\in C_{ij}
.

Suppose
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{i}\neq\mathbf{u}^{*}_{j}
. Since
\eta
is bounded, there exists
c\in\mathbb{R}
so that

V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=c
. Then (
S5.32
) implies that

V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})=c

as well. Since
V^{\eta}
is a sum of strictly concave functions, it is strictly concave. Therefore, Jensen’s inequality yields that

\displaystyle c=\frac{V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})+V^{\eta}(\upsilon\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(1)+1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1};\mathbf{p})}{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{<}}V^{\eta}\bigg(\frac{\mathbf{u}^{*}+\upsilon\mathbf{u}^{*}}{2},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\frac{\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2}+\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(1)+1}}{2},\ldots,\frac{\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}}+\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(k_{1}-1)+1}}{2}\bigg)

where the strict inequality holds because of strict concavity.
If
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}=\frac{\mathbf{u}^{*}+\upsilon\mathbf{u}^{*}}{2}
, then
\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbf{u}^{\prime}_{j}
. Then the above implies that if
\mathbf{u}_{i}\neq\mathbf{u}_{j}
, then

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})<\sup_{\mathbf{u}\in C_{ij},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}}V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p}),

(S5.34)

which contradicts with the fact that
(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*})
is the unique maximizer of
V^{\eta}
. Hence, we must have
\mathbf{u}^{*}_{i}=\mathbf{u}^{*}_{j}
. Since
i
and
j
were arbitrary, we must have
\mathbf{u}^{*}=x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}
for some
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Showing
\mathbf{v}_{j}^{*}
’s are equal for
j\geq 2
:

Suppose
\upsilon
is a permutaion of
[2:k_{1}]
. Then
(\upsilon(2)-1,\ldots,\upsilon(k_{1})-1)
is a permutaion of
[k_{1}-1]
. Let us denote
\upsilon(2)-1=\varsigma(1),\ldots,\upsilon(k_{1})-1=\varsigma(k_{1}-1)
. Then
\varsigma
is a permutaion of
[k_{1}-1]
. Therefore, we have shown that for
i\in[k_{1}-1]
, we can write
\upsilon(i+1)=\varsigma(i)+1
for a permutaion
\varsigma
of
[k_{1}-1]
. Therefore,

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(k_{1})};\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(1+1)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(1+k_{1}-1)};\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1+\varsigma(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1+\varsigma(k_{1}-1)};\mathbf{p}).

However, applying (
S5.32
) on
(\varsigma^{-1}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}})
we obtain that

V^{\eta}(\varsigma^{-1}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{1+\varsigma(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1+\varsigma(k_{1}-1)};\mathbf{p}).

Therefore, we have showed that

V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{\upsilon(k_{1})};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\varsigma^{-1}(\mathbf{u}),\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p}).

In particular,

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(k_{1})};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ V^{\eta}(\varsigma^{-1}(\mathbf{u}^{*}),\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

because we have proved in the last step that

\mathbf{u}^{*}_{1}=\ldots=\mathbf{u}^{*}_{k_{1}-1}
. However,
(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}})
is the unique maximizer of
V^{\eta}
, implying
(\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(2)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{\upsilon(k_{1})})=(\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}})
. Since
\upsilon
is any arbitrary permutation of
[2:k_{1}]
, this implies that
\mathbf{v}_{2}^{*}=\ldots=\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}
.

Showing
v_{j}
’s have equal elements

Suppose
\upsilon
is any arbitrary permutation of
[k_{2}-1]
. Then by (
S5.15
),

\displaystyle V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}),\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}\eta(\mathbf{u},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))+\mathbf{p}_{12}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))

\displaystyle+\mathbf{p}_{21}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k_{1}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1})+\mathbf{p}_{22}\sum\limits_{i^{\prime}=1}^{k_{1}-1}\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbb{A}_{i^{\prime}}\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\mathbf{v}_{i^{\prime}+1}),

(S5.35)

where the
\mathbb{B}_{j}
’s and
\mathbb{A}_{i}
’s are as in (
S5.13
) and (
S5.14
). First note that

\eta(\mathbf{u},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1})
by (
S5.1
). By definition of
\mathbb{B}_{j}
,

\displaystyle\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=

\displaystyle\ \eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(1)}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(k_{2}-1)}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}).

However, by (
3.4
),
\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=\eta(\mathbf{u},\upsilon^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1})))
. Let

\displaystyle l=\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1})=(\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(1)}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(k_{2}-1)}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}).

We want to figure out what is
\upsilon^{-1}(l)
. Suppose
i=j
. Then

\upsilon^{-1}(l_{i})=l_{\upsilon^{-1}(i)}=\begin{cases}\mathbf{v}_{1i}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}&\text{ if }i\neq\upsilon(j)\\
-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}&\text{ if }i=\upsilon(j)\end{cases}

Therefore,

\upsilon^{-1}(\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{1i}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1,\upsilon(j)-1}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}}_{\upsilon(j)-1\text{ elements}},-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},&\\
\underbrace{\mathbf{v}_{1,\upsilon(j)+1}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{1k_{1}}-\mathbf{v}_{1\upsilon(j)}}_{k_{2}-1-\upsilon(j)\text{ elements}}\end{smallmatrix}\right),

which is just
\mathbb{B}_{\upsilon(j)}\mathbf{v}_{1}
.
Thus we have shown that
\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{\upsilon(j^{\prime})}\mathbf{v}_{1})
, indicating that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j^{\prime}}\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}))=\sum\limits_{j^{\prime}=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{\upsilon(j^{\prime})}\mathbf{v}_{1})=\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k_{2}-1}\eta(\mathbf{u},\mathbb{B}_{j}\mathbf{v}_{1}).

Hence, (
S5.35
) implies

V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}),\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

for all
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
and
\mathbf{v}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}}
.
In particular,

V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\upsilon(\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1}),\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})=V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{2},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{k_{1}};\mathbf{p})

The uniqueness of
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}
implies
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=\upsilon(\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*})
. Since
\upsilon
is an arbitrary permutaion, it follows that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}=c\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
for some
c\in\mathbb{R}
. The proof follows similarly for other
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}
’s replacing
\mathbf{u}
by
\mathbb{A}_{i-1}\mathbf{u}
, and hence skipped.

∎

S5.4.5
Proof of Lemma
S5.5

Proof of Lemma
S5.5
.

We have previously shown that the minimizer of
-V^{\eta}(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is unique, and
by Lemma
S5.4
, it is of the form
(u_{1}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{11}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\mathbf{v}^{*}_{21}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}^{*}_{21}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})
for some
\mathbf{u}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
, possibly depending on
\mathbf{p}
. Therefore, if
(x,y,z)\neq(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*})
, it holds that

\displaystyle-V^{\eta}(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ldots,z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})

\displaystyle\ >-V^{\eta}(\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}),

where the strict inequality follows from the uniqueness of the minimum. The above implies

\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p})>\Phi(\mathbf{u}^{*}_{1},\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*},\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*};\mathbf{p})\text{ for all }x,y,z\in\mathbb{R}

because (
S5.15
) implies that

\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p})=-V^{\eta}(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},y\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1},\ldots,z\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}).

Therefore,

\Phi(x,y,z;\mathbf{p})
has a unique minimizer
(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
and
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{u}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{v}_{11}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{v}_{21}^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, thus proving the first part of the lemma.

Now we will show that
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)^{\prime}s
and
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
are proper, closed, and strictly convex for any
i,j\in[2]
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Note that
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s and
\Phi
are strictly convex because
-\eta
is strictly convex. To show a convex function is proper, we need to show that (1) it never attains the value
-\infty
and (2) its domain is non-empty
(
Rockafellar, 1970
, pp. 24 of)
.
Since
-\eta
is bounded below, (1) follows trivially for
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)^{\prime}s
and
\Phi
. Since
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+K_{2}-2}\in\text{dom}(-\eta)
by Lemma
S5.1
, (
S5.17
) implies that
(0,0)\in\text{dom}(-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j))
for all
i,j\in[2]
. Similarly, (
S5.18
) implies that
(0,0,0)\in\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Therefore, (2) follows for
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)^{\prime}s
and
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
. Therefore
-\eta
,
-\vartheta
, and
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
are proper. Using Facts
S11.1
and
S11.2
(see Section
S11
), one can easily verify that
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s and
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
are closed.
We have shown that, for each
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, the minimum of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is attained. Therefore,
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is 0-coercive by Fact
S5.1
.

∎

S5.4.6
Proof of Fact
S5.3

Proof of Fact
S5.3
.

Note that

\displaystyle-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\{-\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})\}

\displaystyle\ =\sup_{(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Big(\mathbf{p}_{11}\vartheta(x,y;1,1)+\mathbf{p}_{12}\vartheta(x,y;1,2)+\mathbf{p}_{21}\vartheta(x,z;2,1)

\displaystyle\ +\mathbf{p}_{22}\vartheta(x,z;2,2)\Big)

\displaystyle\ =\varsigma_{Im(\vartheta)}(\mathbf{p})

(S5.36)

where
\varsigma
is the support function defined in (
1.1
) and

\displaystyle Im(\vartheta)=

\displaystyle\ \{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}:\mathbf{w}=\{\vartheta(x,y;1,1),\vartheta(x,y;1,2),\vartheta(x,z;2,1),\vartheta(x,z;2,2)\}

\displaystyle\ \text{ for some }(x,y,z)\in\mathbb{R}^{3}\}

is the image set of
\vartheta
.
Thus
-\Phi^{*}
is the support function of
Im(\vartheta)
. Thus
-\Phi^{*}
is a closed convex function. Hence, it is continuous on the relative interior of its domain
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Remark 3.1.3, pp. 104,)
. However, because
\vartheta
is bounded above,
-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})<\infty
for
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Hence,

\displaystyle\text{dom}(-\Phi^{*})\supset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}.

(S5.37)

Since
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
is open,
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}\subset\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\Phi^{*}))
. Thus
\Phi^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
everywhere.

To show the finiteness of
\Phi^{*}
, note that, if
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\inf_{\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})=\infty
for some
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, then
\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})=\infty
for all
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
, which would imply that
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is improper. However, Lemma
S5.5
implies that
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is proper. Thus
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})<\infty
. Also,
-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})<\infty
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
because
-\Phi^{*}
is a convex function with
\mathbf{p}\in\text{dom}(-\Phi^{*})
by (
S5.37
). Therefore,
|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\infty
.
∎

S5.4.7
Proof of Lemma
S5.7

Proof of Lemma
S5.7
.

First we prove the results on
\vartheta
.
Lemma
S5.1
implies that under the setup of Theorem
3.1
,
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
.
If
x,y
are in a small neighborhood
U_{0}
of
0
, then
(xA\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},yB\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})
is in a small neighborhood of
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
for all matrices
A
and
B
of appropriate dimensions. Noting
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
, we deduce that if
U_{0}
is small enough, then
(xA_{i}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}},yB_{j}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\eta))
for all
(x,y)\in U_{0}
and
i\in\{0,\ldots,k_{1}-1\}
and
j\in\{0,\ldots,k_{2}-1\}
where the
\mathbb{A}_{i}
and
\mathbb{B}_{j}
’s are as defined in (
S5.13
) and (
S5.14
). Thus,
U_{0}^{2}\subset\text{int}(\text{dom}(\vartheta(\cdot,i,j)))
for
i,j\in[2]
, which proves part 1 of the current lemma. On the other hand, if
(xA_{i}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1},yB_{j}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(-\eta))
,
\eta
is thrice continuously differentiable at
(xA_{i}\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}},yB_{j}\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})
. Therefore,
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
is also thrice continuously differentiable on
U_{0}^{2}
if
U_{0}
is a sufficiently small neighborhood, which proves part 3 of the current lemma.

Now we will prove the results on
\Phi
.
Since for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
, the function
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is a non-negative linear combination of convex functions, namely the
-\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s, by (
S5.18
), it follows that
\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))=\cap_{i,j\in[2]}\text{dom}(\vartheta(\cdot;i,j))

(
Rockafellar, 1970
, pp. 33)
. Therefore, part 2 follows from part 1. Part 4 follows from part 3 due to (
S5.18
).
∎

S5.4.8
Proof of Lemma
S5.8

Proof of Lemma
S5.8
.

The bulk of the proof is devoted to showing that there exists
\delta^{*}>0
so that if
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
for some
\delta\leq\delta^{*}
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})
is uniformly bounded in the sense that it lies in a compact set depending only on
\mathbf{p}
and
\vartheta
. Denote by

\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}=\{v\in\mathbb{R}^{3}:\Phi(v;\mathbf{q})\leq r\},\quad\text{ for all }\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0},

which means
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}
is the level-
r
sublevel set of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{q})
. We will prove the boundedness of
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})
by proving two main results. The first step is to show that

\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+C_{1}\delta}

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. The second step will show that
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{C(\mathbf{p},r)}
for some bounded function
C(\mathbf{p},r)
for all
r\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
as long as
\delta<\min(\mathbf{p})/2\wedge 1
. The rest of the boundedness proof will follow from the uniqueness of the minimizer of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
, which follows from Lemma
S5.5
. We will take
\delta^{*}=\min(\mathbf{p})/2\wedge 1
. Note that if
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
with
\delta\leq\delta^{*}
, then
\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
automatically because
\min(\mathbf{q})>\min(\mathbf{p})-\delta\geq\min(\mathbf{p})/2>0
.

First of all, since
-\Phi^{*}
is a convex function, it is locally Lipschitz on its domain
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, Theorem 3.1.2, pp. 103,)
. However, (
S5.37
) implies that
\text{dom}(-\Phi^{*})\supset\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Therefore, for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
,

|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{q})|\leq L_{\mathbf{p}}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}

for some constant
L_{\mathbf{p}}>0
. Therefore, for
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
,

\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q});\mathbf{q})=\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{q})\leq\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{\mathbf{p}}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}.

Noting
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
by Fact
S5.3
, we deduce that when
\delta<\delta^{*}
,

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{\mathbf{p}}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}}\text{ for all }\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta),

(S5.38)

which implies

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{\mathbf{p}}\delta}\text{ for all }\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta).

(S5.39)

Now we will prove that if
\delta<\delta^{*}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{r+8\delta\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}}\quad\text{for all }\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta).

(S5.40)

Suppose
v=(x,y,z)\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}
for some
r\in\mathbb{R}
. Thus

-\vartheta(x,y;1,1)\mathbf{q}_{11}-\vartheta(x,y;1,2)\mathbf{q}_{12}-\vartheta(x,y;2,1)\mathbf{q}_{21}-\vartheta(x,y;2,2)\mathbf{q}_{22}\leq r.

Note that the
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are bounded above by some constant
C>0
for
i,j\in[2]
by Lemma
S5.11
. Thus
-\vartheta(x,y;i,j)>-C
for all
i,j\in[2]
. Therefore, the above display implies

-\vartheta(x,y;1,1)\mathbf{q}_{11}-C(\mathbf{q}_{12}+\mathbf{q}_{21}+\mathbf{q}_{22})\leq r.

Therefore, we have obtained that

-\vartheta(x,y;1,1)\leq\frac{C(\mathbf{q}_{11}+\mathbf{q}_{21}+\mathbf{q}_{22})+r}{\mathbf{q}_{11}}<\frac{C\|\mathbf{q}\|_{1}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{q})},

where we used the fact that
\min(\mathbf{q})>0
.
Note that

\vartheta(x,y;1,1)\leq C\leq\frac{C\|\mathbf{q}\|_{1}}{\min(\mathbf{q})}.

Therefore,

|\vartheta(x,y;1,1)|\leq\frac{C\|\mathbf{q}\|_{1}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{q})}.

We will express the above bound in terms of
\mathbf{p}
.
By the triangle inequality,

\|\mathbf{q}\|_{1}\leq\|\mathbf{q}-\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}\leq 2\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}

because
\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{1}\leq 2\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}
by Cauchy Schwartz inequality. Also,

\min(\mathbf{q})\geq\min(\mathbf{p})-\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{\infty}\geq\min(\mathbf{p})-\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}

because
\|\mathbf{p}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathbf{p}\|_{2}
for any vector
\mathbf{p}
. Since
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
and
\delta<\delta^{*}
, we have
\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}<\delta^{*}<\min(\mathbf{p})/2
. Hence
\min(\mathbf{p})-\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\geq\min(\mathbf{p})/2
, and the following bound holds:

|\vartheta(x,y;1,1)|\leq\frac{C\|\mathbf{q}\|_{1}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{q})}\leq 2\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}.

Similarly, we can show that

|\vartheta(x,y;1,2)|,|\vartheta(x,z;2,1)|,|\vartheta(x,z;2,2)|\leq 2\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}.

Therefore, if
v\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}
, then

\displaystyle|\Phi(v;\mathbf{p})-\Phi(v;\mathbf{q})|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \Big(|\vartheta(x,y;1,1)|+|\vartheta(x,y;1,2)|+|\vartheta(x,z;2,1)|+|\vartheta(x,z;2,2)|\Big)\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})},

(S5.41)

where we again used the fact that
\|\mathbf{p}\|_{\infty}\leq\|\mathbf{p}\|_{2}
for any vector
\mathbf{p}
.
Since
\Phi(v;\mathbf{q})\leq r
and
\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}<\delta\leq\delta^{*}\leq 1
, it follows that for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
,

\displaystyle\Phi(v;\mathbf{p})\leq

\displaystyle\ r+8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ r+8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})},

(S5.42)

which completes the proof of (
S5.40
).

Now suppose
r=\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}
.
Then

\displaystyle r+8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}+8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C+|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\underbrace{\Big(L_{p}+8\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+2C+|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|+L_{p}}{\min(\mathbf{p})}\Big)}_{C_{p}}

(S5.43)

where we used the fact that
\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}\leq\delta\leq\delta^{*}\leq 1
. Since
|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\infty
by Fact
S5.3
,
C_{p}<\infty
.
Hence, if
\delta\leq\delta^{*}
, then (
S5.38
), (
S5.42
) and (
S5.43
) imply that

\displaystyle\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+C_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}}\subset\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\delta C_{p}}.

(S5.44)

for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
.
Since
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is 0-coercive for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
by Lemma
S5.11
, the sublevel sets of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
are compact by Proposition 3.2.4, pp. 107, of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
. Hence,
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\delta C_{p}}
is compact.

Now it remains to prove that if
\mathbf{q}_{k}\to\mathbf{p}
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\to\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. First of all, note that if
\mathbf{q}_{k}\to\mathbf{p}
, then given any small
\delta>0
,

\mathbf{q}_{k}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
for all sufficiently large
k
. Let us take
\delta<\delta^{*}
. Then the above calculations imply that the sequence
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})
lies in a compact set for all large
k
and hence is bounded. To show
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})\to\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, it suffices to show that given any subsequence of
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})
, there exists a further subsequence that converges to
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Since any subsequence of
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})
is bounded, we can always extract a converging subsequence from it. We will show that limit is
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. If possible, suppose that limit is
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}
for some subsequence where
\mathbf{w}
may depend on the particular subsequence. The proof will be complete if we can show that
\mathbf{w}=\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for any such subsequence. To streamline notation, we will denote this subsequence by
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})
as well.
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is closed by Lemma
S5.5
. By Proposition 1.2.2 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
, the sublevel sets of a closed function are closed (possibly empty). Since
\mathbf{q}_{k}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{*})
for all sufficiently large
k
, using (
S5.44
) we obtain that
\mathbf{q}_{k}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\delta C_{p}}
for all large
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Since
\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\delta C_{p}}
is closed, if
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})\to\mathbf{w}
, then
\mathbf{w}\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{p}}_{\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\delta C_{p}}
. Thus
\mathbf{w}\in\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
. However, we do not know if
\mathbf{w}\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})))
. Therefore, we do not know if
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is continuous at
\mathbf{w}
. However, since
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is closed,
\liminf_{k}\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\geq\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})
since
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})\to_{k}\mathbf{w}

(
Rockafellar, 1970
, for a lower-semi continuous function
f
,
\liminf_{y\to x}f(y)\geq f(x)
; cf. pp. 55 of)
.
Note that

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w},\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \limsup_{k}\bigg[\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\bigg\}+\bigg\{\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}\bigg]

(S5.45)

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\bigg\}=\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\liminf_{k}\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\leq 0

because we have already argued that
\liminf_{k}\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\geq\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})
.

(
S5.39
) implies that

\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\in\mathcal{S}^{\mathbf{q}}_{r}
with
r=\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+L_{\mathbf{p}}\delta^{*}
as long as
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{*})
. Then by (
S5.41
),

\displaystyle\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\leq 8\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}_{k}\|_{2}\frac{C\|\mathbf{p}\|_{1}+4C\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}_{k}\|_{2}+|r|}{\min(\mathbf{p})}.

Since
|\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\infty
, it follows that
|r|<\infty
. Therefore, using the fact that
\mathbf{q}_{k}\to\mathbf{p}
, we deduce that

\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}=0.

Hence,

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\bigg\}+\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}

is well-defined and is non-positive. Therefore,

\displaystyle\limsup_{k}\bigg[\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\bigg\}+\bigg\{\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}\bigg]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})\bigg\}+\limsup_{k}\bigg\{\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k}),\mathbf{p})-\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})\bigg\}

is non-positive.
Therefore, (
S5.45
) implies that

0\geq\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\liminf_{k}\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k});\mathbf{q}_{k})=\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\liminf_{k}\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{q}_{k})=\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})

where the last step follows from (
S5.37
) because
-\Phi^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
by Lemma
S5.3
. Therefore, we have obtained that
\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})\leq\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Lemma
S5.5
implies that
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
has unique minimum for each
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
. Therefore,
\mathbf{w}=\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, which completes the proof.

∎

S5.4.9
Proof of Lemma
S5.10

Proof of Lemma
S5.10
.

We will prove the result for
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
only because the proof for
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})
follows similarly.
Before we prove this lemma, we will collect some results that will be required later in the proof.
Since Lemma
S5.6
implies
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})=(0,0,0)
,
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}^{0})
is minimized at
(0,0,0)
.
Lemma
S5.7
implies that

\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is differentiable at
(0,0,0)
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}_{>0}
, and in particular, at
\mathbf{p}^{0}
, which implies
\gradient\Phi(0,0,0;\mathbf{p}^{0})=0
, yielding

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{x}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{x}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;2,1)}{x}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;2,2)}{x}=

\displaystyle\ 0,

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}=

\displaystyle\ 0,

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;2,1)}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;2,2)}{y}=

\displaystyle\ 0.

(S5.46)

Let us also define

\displaystyle M_{12}(x,y)=\partialderivative{\vartheta(x,y;1,1)}{x}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(x,y;1,2)}{x}{y}

\displaystyle N_{12}(x,y)=\partialderivative{\vartheta(x,y;2,1)}{x}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(x,y;2,2)}{x}{y},

(S5.47)

where
M_{12}(x,y)
and
N_{12}(x,y)
exist if
x,y\in U_{0}
, and in particular, at
(x,y)=(0,0)
. The following lemma, proved in Section
S5.5.1
, provides the values of
M_{12}(0,0)
and
N_{12}(0,0)
.

Lemma S5.14
.

M_{12}(0,0)=N_{12}(0,0)=0
when
M_{12}
and
N_{12}
are continuous functions.

The following lemma proves the Hölder continuity of
\Lambda^{*}
at all
\mathbf{p}
in a neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
. Lemma
S5.15
is proved in Section
S5.5.3

Lemma S5.15
.

Consider the setup in Theorem
3.1
.
Suppose
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta^{0})
where
\delta^{0}
is as in Lemma
S5.9
. Then there exists
\delta_{p}>0
, possibly depending on
\mathbf{p}
, such that if
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta_{p})
, then
\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}\leq C_{p}\sqrt{\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}}
where
C_{p}
depends only on
\vartheta
and
\mathbf{p}
.

We will choose
\delta>0
in a way so that a few conditions are met. In particular, we let
\delta=\min(\delta_{1},\delta_{2},\delta_{3},\delta_{4},\delta_{5})
where
\delta_{1}
,
\delta_{2}
,
\delta_{3}
,
\delta_{4}
, and
\delta_{5}
are as explained below.

1.

Let
\delta_{1}>0
be such that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U^{3}_{0}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{1})
. Such a
\delta_{1}
exists by Lemma
S5.9
.

2.

Let
\delta_{2}>0
be such that for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{2})
,

\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})\|_{\infty}=\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\|_{\infty}<2
. Such a
\delta_{2}
exists by Lemma
S5.8
.

3.

Let us denote

C=\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}+\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}.

Note that
C
is a negative number because
\vartheta
is strictly concave. If
\mathbf{p}
is in a small neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}=(1,1,1,1)
,
|\mathbf{p}_{11}-1|
and
|\mathbf{p}_{12}-1|
are small. If
\delta
is sufficiently small, then
(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
will be sufficiently close to
(0,0)
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
by Lemma
S5.8
. Also,
\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(x,y;1,1)}{y}
and
\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(x,y;1,2)}{y}
are close to
\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}
and
\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}
if
(x,y)
is sufficiently close to
(0,0)
because the second-order partial derivatives of
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are continuous at
(0,0)
by Lemma
S5.7
.
Therefore, there exists
\delta_{3}>0
so that if
\delta<\delta_{3}
, then for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
,

\displaystyle\bigg\{\mathbf{p}_{11}\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),\xi;1,1)}{y}+\mathbf{p}_{12}\partialderivative[2]{\vartheta(x^*(\mp),\xi';1,2)}{y}\bigg\}\in(2C,C/2)

(S5.48)

for all
\xi
and
\xi^{\prime}
between
0
and
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.

4.

Since the third order partial derivatives of
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are continuous on
U_{0}^{2}
by Lemma
S5.7
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is continuous and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}
in a small neighborhood of
\mathbf{p}^{0}
by Lemma
S5.8
, we
can choose
\delta
so small such that
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
,

\begin{split}\bigg|\mathbf{p}_{11}\immediate\immediate\immediate{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(\xi_{1},0;2,2)}{\partial{}x^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial y}}+\mathbf{p}_{12}{\frac{\partial^{\mskip 1.0mu3}\mskip 0.0mu\vartheta(\xi_{2},0;1,2)}{\partial{}x^{\mskip 1.0mu2}\mskip-1.0mu\mskip 2.0mu plus 1.0mu minus 1.0mu\partial y}}\bigg|\\

S5.4.10
Proof of Lemma
S5.11

Proof of Lemma
S5.11
.

By Lemma
S5.7
, there exists an open neighborhood
U_{0}\ni 0
such that
U_{0}^{2}\subset\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p}))
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
.
Lemma
S5.8
implies that we can choose a
\delta>0
so small such that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. We will consider that
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
for the rest of this proof.

Proving P1

Lemma
S5.5
implies that

\mathbf{u}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}
,
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
for all
i\in[2:k_{1}]
, where these vectors are as defined in Lemma
S5.4
.
Therefore (
S5.10
) implies that

\begin{split}&\ \mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}),\quad\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}),\\
&\ \mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})\end{split}

is a maximizer of
V^{\psi}
. Hence, a version of
\tilde{d}
is given by
\tilde{d}_{1}=\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})
and

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{2}(i)=\begin{cases}\text{pred}(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})&\text{ if }i=1\\
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})&\text{ if }i\in[2:k_{1}].\end{cases}

(S5.50)

Note that
\tilde{d}_{1}
(or
\tilde{d}_{2}
) can take only two values, one and
k_{1}
(or
k_{2}
).
We remind the reader that although we consider
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{4}_{>0}
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1}k_{2}}_{b}
, this
\mathbf{p}
is the abbreviation of
(\mathbf{p}_{ij})_{i\in[k_{1}],j\in[k_{2}]}
. However, for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1}k_{2}}_{b}
,

\mathbf{p}_{1j}=\mathbf{p}_{12}
if
j\geq 2
,
\mathbf{p}_{i1}=\mathbf{p}_{21}
for
i\geq 2
and
\mathbf{p}_{i2}=\mathbf{p}_{22}
for
i\geq 2
. Therefore, to avoid redundancy, we only consider the four elements of
\mathbf{p}
although
\mathbf{p}_{ij}
exists for all
i\in[k_{1}]
and
j\in[k_{2}]
. In light of the above, we can keep using the expression
V(\tilde{d})=\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}_{b}
as well. Since
V(d)=\mathbf{p}_{d_{1},d_{2}(d_{1})}
for any DTR
d
,
V(\tilde{d})=\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}
.

Subcase
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
:
If
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then Definition
2.1
implies that
V(\tilde{d})=V_{*}=\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})
. Therefore,
\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}=\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})
. Hence,
\tilde{d}_{1}\neq 2,3,\ldots,k_{1}
because in these cases,
\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}\leq\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})<V_{*}
. Thus
\tilde{d}_{1}=1
, which implies
\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})=1
, which implies
\max(\argmax(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})))=1
. Therefore,
0>-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})
or
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
.

Subcase
\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})
:
In this case, proceeding in the same way as the previous case, we can similarly prove that

\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})=k_{1}
, which implies
\max(\argmax(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}))=k_{1}
. However, this only implies that
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq 0
. Therefore, we still need to show that
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\neq 0
. If possible, suppose
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
for some
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. Then for this
\mathbf{p}
,
\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}}
. Consider the sequence
\mathbf{w}_{n}=(1/n,\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}-1})
.

Our next step is to show that
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})\to_{n}V^{\psi}_{*}
. To this end, it suffices to show that
V^{\eta}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})\to_{n}V^{\eta}_{*}
. Note that
\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n}=1_{k_{1}-1}/n
. Also, Lemma
S5.5
implies that
\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
and
\mathbf{v}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\ldots=\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}
.
Therefore, by (
S5.19
),

V^{\eta}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{v}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})=-\Phi(1/n,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})
and
V^{\eta}_{*}=-\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
\Phi(1/n,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})\to_{n}\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Since
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\in U_{0}^{3}
, it follows that
(0,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})))
. Since
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is convex by Lemma
S5.5
, the above implies that
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is continuous at
(0,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
because convex functions are continuous on the interior of its domain
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. pp. 104 of)
.
Therefore,

\lim_{n}\Phi(1/n,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})=\Phi(0,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})=\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p}),

where we used the fact that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(0,y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
.
Therefore, it follows that
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})\to_{n}V^{\psi}_{*}
. If
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then the above implies that
V(\tilde{d}_{n})\to V_{*}=\max(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})
where
the DTR
\tilde{d}_{n}=(\tilde{d}_{1n},\tilde{d}_{2n})
corresponds to
(\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
. Note that
\tilde{d}_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1n}=\text{pred}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n}),\quad\tilde{d}_{2n}(i)=\begin{cases}\text{pred}(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})&\text{ if }i=1\\
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})&\text{ if }i\in[2:k_{1}].\end{cases}

(S5.51)

Thus in this case,

\tilde{d}_{1n}=\text{pred}(0,-\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}/n)=1
. Since
V(\tilde{d})=V(d_{1},d_{2}(d_{1}))
for any DTR
d\equiv(d_{1},d_{2})
in
\mathcal{P}^{k_{1},k_{2}}
,
V(\tilde{d}_{n})=\mathbf{p}_{1,d_{2n}(1)}
. Therefore,

V(\tilde{d}_{n})\leq\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})<\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})=V_{*}.

The above implies

\limsup_{n}V(\tilde{d}_{n})<V_{*}
, which violates
V(\tilde{d}_{n})\to V_{*}
. Therefore, we arrive at a contradiction if we assume
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
. Therefore, if
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
, then
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
if
\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})
.

Proving P2

Case 1:
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})>\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})

Suppose
\mathbf{p}_{11}>\mathbf{p}_{12}
. Therefore,
V_{*}=\max(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{11}
and
\mathbf{p}_{ij}<V_{*}
unless
(i,j)=(1,1)
. If
\psi
is Fisher consistent, then
V(\tilde{d})=\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1},\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})}=\mathbf{p}_{11}
. Therefore,
d_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})=1
. We have already proved that
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
in this case, implying
\tilde{d}_{1}=\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})=1
. Therefore,
\tilde{d}_{2}(1)=1
. Hence, (
S5.50
) implies that
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})=1
, which indicates that
\max(\argmax(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}))=1
. Therefore,
0>-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, which implies
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
. Now if
\mathbf{p}_{11}<\mathbf{p}_{12}
, then proceeding as before, we arrive at

\max(\argmax(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}))=k_{2}
, which only ensures that
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq 0
. As before, let us assume
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
for some
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. We will show that we arrive at a contradiction if
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.

Consider the sequence
(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n,z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
, which
obviously converges to
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
as
n\to\infty
. Let us also denote
\mathbf{w}_{n}=(1/n,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})
.
Therefore, proceeding like the case of
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
