, we can show that

\Phi(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n,z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})\to_{n}\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p}).

As before, it follows that
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=V^{\psi}_{*}
and
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))=-\Phi(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n,z^{*}(\mathbf{p});\mathbf{p})
.
Therefore,
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{w}_{n},\mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{k_{1}}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))\to_{n}V_{*}^{\psi}
. Suppose
\tilde{d}_{n}
is the DTR associated with
(\mathbf{x}^{*},\mathbf{y}^{*}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{y}^{*}_{k_{1}})
. We can show that

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1n}=\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}),\quad\tilde{d}_{2n}(i)=\begin{cases}\text{pred}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n})&\text{ if }i=1\\
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})&\text{ if }i\in[2:k_{1}].\end{cases}

Fisher consistency implies that
V(\tilde{d}_{n})=\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1n},\tilde{d}_{2n}(\tilde{d}_{1n})}\to_{n}\mathbf{p}_{12}
. However, we have shown that
\tilde{d}_{1n}=1
in this case. Also,

\tilde{d}_{2n}(\tilde{d}_{1n})=\tilde{d}_{2n}(1)=\text{pred}(0,-\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}/n)=1.

Therefore, we have
V(\tilde{d}_{n})=\mathbf{p}_{11}<V^{\psi}_{*}=\mathbf{p}_{12}
where the last equality follows because because
\max(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{12}
.
Hence, we arrive at a contradiction, implying
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})
can not be zero.

Case 2:
\max(\mathbf{p}_{11},\mathbf{p}_{12})<\max(\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22})

We have shown that, in this case,
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
. Therefore,
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}))=\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1})=k_{1}
. Suppose
\mathbf{p}_{21}>\mathbf{p}_{22}
. In this case,
V_{*}=\max(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{21}
. Using Fisher consistency as in Case 1, we can show that
\tilde{d}_{2}(\tilde{d}_{1})=1
, implying
\tilde{d}_{2}(k_{1})=1
. Therefore,
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})=1
, which implies
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
. If
\mathbf{p}_{21}<\mathbf{p}_{22}
, then we can show that
\text{pred}(0,-z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))=k_{2}
, which implies
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq 0
. If possible, suppose
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
for some
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. In this case,
\max(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{22}
.

Consider the sequence
(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n)
. Let us denote
\mathbf{w}_{n}=(1/n,\mathbf{0}_{k_{2}-1})
. Proceeding as before, we can show that

\Phi(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n;\mathbf{p})\to_{n}\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p}).

From (
S5.19
), it follows that
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=V^{\psi}_{*}
and
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{w}_{n},,\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n})=-\Phi(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),1/n,)
.
Therefore,
V^{\psi}(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{w}_{n},,\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n})\to_{n}V_{*}^{\psi}
. If
\psi
is Fisher consistent, we must also have
V(\tilde{d}_{n})\to_{n}V_{*}
where
\tilde{d}_{n}
is the DTR corresponding to
(\mathbf{x}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{y}_{2}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{w}_{n},,\ldots,\mathbf{w}_{n})
. We can show that

\displaystyle\tilde{d}_{1n}=\text{pred}(0,-x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{1}-1}),\quad\tilde{d}_{2n}(i)=\begin{cases}\text{pred}(0,-y^{*}(\mathbf{p})\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1})&\text{ if }i=1\\
\text{pred}(\Delta\mathbf{w}_{n})&\text{ if }i\in[2:k_{1}].\end{cases}

Since
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
, the corresponding
\tilde{d}_{n}
satisfies
\tilde{d}_{1n}=k_{1}
. Therefore,
\tilde{d}_{2n}(\tilde{d}_{1n})=\tilde{d}_{2n}(k_{1})=\text{pred}(0,-\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}-1}/n)=1
. Hence,
\mathbf{p}_{\tilde{d}_{1n},\tilde{d}_{2n}(\tilde{d}_{1n})}=\mathbf{p}_{k_{1}1}
. However, since
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}^{k_{1}k_{2}}_{b}
,
\mathbf{p}_{k_{1}1}=\mathbf{p}_{21}
by definition. Therefore,
V(\tilde{d}_{n})=\mathbf{p}_{21}
. Since
\mathbf{p}_{21}<\mathbf{p}_{22}
, it follows that
\limsup_{n}V(\tilde{d}_{n})<V_{*}
, which implies
V(\tilde{d}_{n})
does not converge to
V_{*}
. Thus we again encounter a contradiction, implying
z^{*}(\mathbf{p})
can not be zero.

∎

S5.5
Proof of auxiliary results

S5.5.1
Proof of Lemma
S5.14

Proof of Lemma
S5.14
.

We prove the case for
M_{12}
because the proof for
N_{12}
follows in the similar way. Lemma
S5.7
implies that the second-order partial derivatives of
\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)
’s are continuous on
U_{0}^{2}
for all
i,j\in[2]
. Therefore,
M_{12}
and
N_{12}
are continuous on the open set
U_{0}^{2}
.
Suppose
M_{12}(0,0)\neq 0
. If possible, suppose
M_{12}(0,0)>C
for some
C>0
. Then there exists
\epsilon>0
such that
|x|,|y|<\epsilon
implies that

M_{12}(x,y)>C/2
. By Lemma
S5.8
, can choose
\delta_{1}>0
so small such that
|x^{*}(\mathbf{p})-x^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})|<\epsilon
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{1})
. Since
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
by Lemma
S5.6
, it follows that
|x^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\epsilon
for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{1})
.
By Lemma
S5.16
below (proved in Section
S5.5.2
), we can choose
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{1})
so that
\mathbf{p}_{11}=\mathbf{p}_{12}>\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22}
,
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
,
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
, and
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
.

Lemma S5.16
.

There exists
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta_{1})
so that
\mathbf{p}_{11}=\mathbf{p}_{12}>\mathbf{p}_{21},\mathbf{p}_{22}
,
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
,
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
, and
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
.

Noting (a)
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})=(x^{*}(\mathbf{p}),y^{*}(\mathbf{p}),z^{*}(\mathbf{p}))
is the minimizer of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
and (b)
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is thrice differentiable on
U_{0}^{3}
, and hence at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, we obtain that

0=\partialderivative{\Phi(x^*(\mp),y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{y}.

Then
by a first order Taylor series expansion (differentiating with respect to
x
), we have

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\Phi(0,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{y}+x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\Phi(\xi,y^*(\mp),z^*(\mp);\mp)}{x}{y}=0

where
\xi
is a number between
0
and
x^{*}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{11}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,y^*(\mp);1,1)}{y}+\mathbf{p}_{12}\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,y^*(\mp);1,2)}{y}

\displaystyle\ +x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\bigg(\mathbf{p}_{11}\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,y^*(\mp);1,1)}{x}{y}+\mathbf{p}_{12}\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,y^*(\mp);1,2)}{x}{y}\bigg)=0.

(S5.52)

Since
\mathbf{p}_{11}=\mathbf{p}_{12}
and
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
, we have

\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,1)}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(0,0;1,2)}{y}+x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,1)}{x}{y}+x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,2)}{x}{y}=0.

Using (
S5.46
) we obtain that

x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,1)}{x}{y}+x^{*}(\mathbf{p})\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,2)}{x}{y}=0.

Since
x^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
,

M_{12}(\xi,0)=\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,1)}{x}{y}+\partialderivative{\vartheta(\xi,0;1,2)}{x}{y}=0.

Therefore, we have obtained that
there exists
\xi\in[0,x^{*}(\mathbf{p})]
such that

M_{12}(\xi,0)=0
. However, since
|x^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\epsilon
,
\xi
satisfies
|\xi|<\epsilon
. However, we have previously shown that
M_{12}(x,y)>C/2
for all
x,y\in(-\epsilon,\epsilon)
. Therefore, we arrive at a contradiction. Hence, our assumption
M_{12}(0,0)>0
is incorrect Similarly, we can show that assuming
M_{12}(0,0)<0
leads to a contradiction as well. Thus,
M_{12}(0,0)=0
.

∎

S5.5.2
Proof of Lemma
S5.16

Proof of Lemma
S5.16
.

By Lemmas
S5.7
and
S5.11
, we can choose
\delta\in(0,\delta_{1})
so small such that for all
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
,
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
and
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
satisfies Properties P1 and P2. We consider a
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta/3)
of the form
(c,c,c_{1},c_{2})
where
c>c_{1},c_{2}>0
. Such a
\mathbf{p}
exists in the ball
B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta/3)
because
\mathbf{p}^{0}=(1,1,1,1)
.
Suppose, if possible,
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
. We will show that this leads to a contradiction. If
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
, we can find an
\epsilon>0
such that
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>\epsilon
. Since
\Lambda^{*}
is a continuous map by Lemma
S5.8
, we can find a
\delta^{\prime}>0
such that for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{\prime})
,
\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}\leq\epsilon/2
. We choose
\delta^{\prime}
to be smaller than
\delta/3
so that
B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{\prime})\subset B(\mathbf{p}^{0},2\delta/3)\subset B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
. If
(x^{*}(\mathbf{q}),y^{*}(\mathbf{q}),z^{*}(\mathbf{q}))=\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})
,
|y^{*}(\mathbf{p})-y^{*}(\mathbf{q})|\leq\epsilon/2
. Hence,
y^{*}(\mathbf{q})>0
. In particular, we consider
\mathbf{q}=(c,c+\delta^{\prime}/2,c_{1},c_{2})
. It is not hard to see that
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{\prime})
. However, since
B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{\prime})\subset B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta)
, the corresponding
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})
satisfies Properties P1 and P2. Since
\argmax(\mathbf{q})=(1,2)
, Property P2 implies
y^{*}(\mathbf{q})<0
, which leads to a contradiction. Thus
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
can not hold. In a similar way, we can show that
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})<0
can not hold. Hence, we must have
y^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
.

∎

S5.5.3
Proof of Lemma
S5.15

Proof of Lemma
S5.15
.

In this proof, we will use the fact that
(0,0)
lies inside
\text{int}(\text{dom}(\vartheta(\cdot;i,j)))
for all
i,j\in[2]
, which follows from Lemma
S5.7
.
If
\mathbf{p}\in B(\mathbf{p}^{0},\delta^{0})
where
\delta^{0}
is as in Lemma
S5.9
, then
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\in U_{0}^{3}
, where the latter is an open set containing the origin. Therefore,
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is thrice continuously differentiable at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Therefore, the hessian of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
exists and all of its elements are continuous at
U_{0}^{3}\ni\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Since
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is a strictly convex function, the hessian of
\mathbf{w}\mapsto\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})
is positive definite at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. The minimum eigenvalue of the hessian at
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is thus bounded below by
2c_{p}
for some
c_{p}>0
. Since the hessian of
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is continuous on
U_{0}^{3}
and the minimum eigenvalue of a matrix is a continuous function of the matrix
(
Horn and Johnson, 2012
, cf. Corollary 6.3.8, p.407 of)
, the minimum eigenvalue of the hessian of
\mathbf{w}\mapsto\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})
is a continuous function of
\mathbf{w}
on
U_{0}^{3}
. We can therefore choose a
\epsilon>0
so small such that the minimum eigenvalue of the hessian of
\Phi(\mathbf{w};\mathbf{p})
is bounded away from
c_{p}
for all
\mathbf{w}\in B(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\epsilon)\subset U_{0}^{3}
.

Thus
\Phi(\cdot;\mathbf{p})
is strongly convex on
B(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\epsilon)
with constant
c_{p}>0
that possibly depends only on
\vartheta
and
\mathbf{p}
. Since
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is continuous at
\mathbf{p}
, there is
\delta^{\prime}>0
so that
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\in B(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\epsilon)
for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta^{\prime})
.
Let us take
\delta_{p}=\min(\delta^{\prime},\delta^{*})
where
\delta^{*}
is as in the proof of Lemma
S5.8
. Note that
\delta^{*}
depends on
\mathbf{p}
. Suppose
\delta<\delta_{p}
. Then for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
,

\displaystyle\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}),\mathbf{p})\geq\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{p})+\langle s,\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})\rangle+\frac{c_{p}}{2}\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}^{2},

where
s\in\partial\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{p})
by Theorem 6.1.2, pp. 200 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
.
Since
\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})
is the minimizer of
\Phi(\cdot,\mathbf{p})
,
0\in\partial\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{p})
. Taking
s=0
, we obtain the following for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
:

\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}),\mathbf{p})\geq\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{p})+\frac{c_{p}}{2}\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}^{2}.

On the other hand, since
\delta<\delta_{p}\leq\delta^{*}
, (
S5.44
) implies that

\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}),\mathbf{p})\leq\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+C_{p}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}

for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
.
Because
\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{p})=\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, we thus have

\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\frac{c_{p}}{2}\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\Phi(\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q}),\mathbf{p})\leq\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+\frac{\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}}{2}C_{p}

for all
\mathbf{q}\in B(\mathbf{p},\delta)
, implying

\|\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Lambda^{*}(\mathbf{q})\|_{2}^{2}\leq\frac{2C_{p}}{c_{p}}\|\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{q}\|_{2}.

∎

S5.5.4
Proof of Fact
S5.4

Proof of Fact
S5.4
.

If
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}\in\partial(\text{dom}(-\eta))
, then by the supporting hyperplane theorem
(
Rockafellar, 1970
, pp. 100, Theorem 11.6)
, there exists a hyperplane
\mathfrak{T}^{T}\mathbf{u}+\mathfrak{C}^{T}\mathbf{v}+c=0
with
\mathfrak{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
,
\mathfrak{C}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
, and
c\in\mathbb{R}
so that

(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{C})\neq\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
and
\mathfrak{T}^{T}\mathbf{u}+\mathfrak{C}^{T}\mathbf{v}+c\leq 0
for all
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\text{dom}(-\eta)
and
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
lies on the hyperplane. The supporting hyperplane theorem applies because
\text{dom}(-\eta)
is a convex set due to
\eta
being concave. Since
\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
lies on the hyperplane,
c=0
and, hence, the hyperplane passes through the origin.
Suppose
\bm{\epsilon}=(\bm{\epsilon}_{i})_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k_{1}-1}
is such that
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}>0
if
\mathfrak{T}_{i}>0
and
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}<0
if
\mathfrak{T}_{i}<0
. If
\mathfrak{T}_{i}=0
, then
\bm{\epsilon}_{i}
can be either
1
or
-1
. Thus
\bm{\epsilon}
need not be unique. Simililarly, we define
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}=(\bm{\epsilon}_{i}^{\prime})_{i=1}^{k_{1}}\in\{\pm 1\}^{k_{2}-1}
such that
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}_{i}>0
if
\beta_{i}>0
and
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}_{i}<0
if
\beta_{i}<0
.
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}
need not be unique either since
\mathfrak{C}
can contain zero elements. Consider the open orthant
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
. Suppose
\mathbf{u}\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})
and
\mathbf{v}\in\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
. The definition of
\bm{\epsilon}
and
\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime}
ensures that
\mathfrak{T}_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}>0
if
\mathfrak{T}_{i}\neq 0
and
\beta_{j}\mathbf{v}_{j}>0
if
\beta_{j}\neq 0
.
Then

\mathfrak{T}^{T}\mathbf{u}+\mathfrak{C}^{T}\mathbf{v}=\sum\limits_{i:\mathfrak{T}_{i}\neq 0}|\mathfrak{T}_{i}\mathbf{u}_{i}|+\sum\limits_{j:\beta_{j}\neq 0}|\beta_{j}\mathbf{v}_{j}|.

Also
\mathfrak{T}
and
\mathfrak{C}
both can not be zero vectors because the theorem states that
(\mathfrak{T},\mathfrak{C})\neq\mathbf{0}_{k_{1}+k_{2}-2}
. Therefore, there exists at least one
\mathfrak{T}_{i}
or
\beta_{j}
such that
\mathfrak{T}_{i}\neq 0
or
\beta_{j}\neq 0
, leading to
\mathfrak{T}_{i}^{T}\mathbf{u}_{i}>0
or
\beta_{j}\mathbf{v}_{j}>0
. Therefore,
\mathfrak{T}^{T}\mathbf{u}+\mathfrak{C}^{T}\mathbf{v}>0
. Therefore,
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\notin\text{dom}(-\eta)
because
\mathfrak{T}^{T}\mathbf{u}+\mathfrak{C}^{T}\mathbf{v}\leq 0
for all
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})\in\text{dom}(-\eta)
. Since
(\mathbf{u},\mathbf{v})
was an arbitrary element of
\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})
, it follows that

\{\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon})\times\mathcal{Q}^{0}\cap\mathcal{Q}_{1}(\bm{\epsilon}^{\prime})\}\cap\text{dom}(-\eta)=\emptyset
.

∎

S6
Proofs of Theorem
4.1
and Proposition
1

This section is organized as follows. In Section
S6.0.1
, we introduce some terminologies that will be used in the proofs of Theorem
4.1
and Proposition
1
. Section
S6.1

lists some preliminary results on
\psi
’s satisfying Conditions
N1
-
N2
. These results will be used not only for the proofs of this section, but also for proving Theorem
4.2
and other results of Section
4
. Then, in
Section
S6.2
, we state and prove some auxiliary lemmas on the value function under Assumptions I-V, which will be used in the proofs of this section. Sections
S6.3
and
S6.4
provide the proofs of Theorem
4.1
and Proposition
1
, respectively.

S6.0.1
New terminologies

Notation and terminologies:

In what follows, we will denote
\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}
.
For the purpose of induction, we introduce some new notation.
Let us define the function
\mathbf{p}_{T}:\mathcal{H}_{T}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k_{T}}
so that

\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T})_{i}=\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}+\ldots+Y_{T}\mid H_{T},A_{T}=i]\text{ for }i\in[k_{T}],

and for
t=T-1,\ldots,1
, the functions
\mathbf{p}_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
are recursively defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t};f)_{i}=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});\mathbf{p}_{1+t}(H_{1+t};f))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]\text{ for all }i\in[k_{t}].

(S6.1)

When
f
is clear from the context, we will denote
\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t};f)
simply by
\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t})
.
We also define the functions
\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}

so that
\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}=\mathbf{p}_{T}
and for
t=T-1,\ldots,1
, the
\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}
’s are recursively defined as

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{i}=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i],\quad i\in[k_{t}].

(S6.2)

For a function
h:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto[k_{t}]
, expressions such as
\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t})_{h(H_{t})}
and
\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{h(H_{t})}
may also be denoted by
\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t},h(H_{t}))
and
\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t},h(H_{t}))
, respectively, especially when the expression of
h(H_{t})
is long or convoluted.
For the sake of notational simplicity, for any
\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}.

(S6.3)

S6.1
Lemmas on the properties of
\phi
under Conditions
N1
-
N2

This section is organized as follows.
First, we list some facts that we will repeatedly use in the proofs of Theorem
4.1
and
4.2
. In Section
S6.1.1
, we establish properties that
\phi
satisfies when Condition
N1
holds. In Section
S6.1.2
, we derive properties of
\phi
under both Conditions
N1
and
N2
.

Under Assumption I,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]=\Psi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})\text{ for all }t\in[T],

(S6.4)

and for any random variable
\mathbb{V}
and
i\in[k_{t}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{V}\frac{1[A_{t}=i]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]

(S6.5)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{V}\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]=\Psi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))

(S6.6)

where
\mathbf{p}(H_{t})_{j}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}\mid H_{t},A_{t}=j]
for all
j\in[k_{t}]
.
Therefore, for any
H_{t}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{V}\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))].

(S6.7)

Moreover, if
h(H_{t})
is any
\mathbb{P}
-measurable function of
H_{t}
, then

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[h(H_{t})\mathbb{V}\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}[h(H_{t})\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))]\text{ for all }t\in[T].

(S6.8)

Also, if Assumption I holds, with
\mathbf{p}(H_{t})_{j}=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}\mid H_{t},A_{t}=j]
, we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{1[A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))]-1[A_{t}=i]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}h(H_{t})\mathbb{V}\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E\bigg[\frac{1[A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))]-1[A_{t}=i]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}h(H_{t})\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}\mid H_{t},A_{t}]\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E\bigg[\frac{1[A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))]-1[A_{t}=i]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}h(H_{t})\mathbf{p}(H_{t})_{A_{t}}\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\max(\mathbf{p}(H_{t}))-\mathbf{p}(H_{t})_{i}\Big)h(H_{t})\bigg|H_{t}\bigg].

(S6.9)

We will also use the fact that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]=1\text{ for all }t\in[T].

(S6.10)

S6.1.1
Properties of
\phi
satisfying Condition
N1

Fact S6.1
.

Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies Condition
N1
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If
\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for some
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
,
then
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m
.

Proof of Fact
S6.1
.

If the assertion in Fact
S6.1
does not hold, we can extract a subsequence of
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
, if necessary, so that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\not\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
but

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to\Psi^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{p})
. We denote this subsequence by
\{\mathbf{x}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}
for the sake of notational simplicity. Then,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\sup_{m}\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}),

which would contradict (
4.4
).

∎

Lemma S6.1
.

Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies Condition
N1
, where
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Then the
\phi(\cdot;i)
’s are bounded above for each
i\in[k]
. Moreover, for each
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
such that
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.1
.

First we prove the boundedness of
\phi
.
Suppose there exists
i\in[k]
such that
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)=\infty
. In this case,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\infty
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
. Also there exists
\{\mathbf{x}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\to_{m}\infty
. Therefore,

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
. Fact
S6.1
implies that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consider
\mathbf{p}_{1},\ \mathbf{p}_{2}
in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
such that
\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{1})=1
and
\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{2})=2
.
For both
i\in[1,2]
, it holds that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{i})
for all sufficiently large
m
.
Then there exists
M\in\mathbb{N}
such that for all
m\geq M
,
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{1})\cap\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{2})=\emptyset
, which can not hold. Therefore,
\sup\phi(\cdot;i)
must be finite.

Now suppose
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})=0
for some
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
, but
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Let
\mathcal{C}=\{i\in[k]:\mathbf{p}^{\prime}_{i}>0\}
, which is non-empty because
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Since
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})=0
,
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}^{\prime})=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
if
i\in\mathcal{C}
. Therefore, if
i\in\mathcal{C}
,
\phi(\mathbf{x};i)=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then for any
\mathbf{p}
such that
\{i\in[k]:\mathbf{p}_{i}>0\}=\mathcal{C}
,

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=0
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
. We can choose
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
so that

\{i\in[k]:\mathbf{p}_{i}>0\}=\mathcal{C}
and the non-zero
\mathbf{p}_{i}
’s are all different so that
[k_{r}]\setminus\argmax(\mathbf{p})
is non-empty. Therefore, there exists
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfying
\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p})
. Therefore, the set
\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p})\}
is non-empty, which implies

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})>-\infty.

Since
0\leq\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
, it follows that the above supremum is
0
. Therefore, (
4.4
) is violated. Therefore,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})>0
for all non-zero
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
.
∎

Lemma S6.2
.

If
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]
satisfies Condition
N1
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, then
\Psi^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
is positively homogenous, convex, and a continuous function.

Proof of Lemma
S6.2
.

In Section
4
, it was mentioned that
\Psi^{*}
is the support function of the set
\mathcal{V}_{\phi}
. A support function of a nonempty set is sublinear
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Proposition 2.1.2, pp. 134 of )
. Sublinear functions are functions that are both convex and positively homogenous

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Definition 1.1.1, pp. 123 of )
.
By Lemma
S6.1
,
\phi(\cdot;i)
is bounded above for each
i
. Therefore,
|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})|<\infty
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Hence,
\text{dom}(\Psi^{*})=\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Since a convex function is continuous on the interior of its domain
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. pp. 104 of)
,
\Psi^{*}
is continuous.

∎

Lemma S6.3
.

Let
\mathfrak{B}
be any bounded set in
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
.
Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfies Condition
N1
. Then there exists a non-negative convex function
\varrho:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
, depending only on
\psi
and
\mathfrak{B}
, so that

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\varrho(\max(p)-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}))

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{B}
,
where
\kappa
is as in (
S6.3
). Moreover,
\varrho(0)=0
,
\varrho
is positive on
(0,\infty)
, and for any sequence
\{\epsilon_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
,
\lim_{m\to\infty}\varrho(\epsilon_{m})=0
if and only if
\lim_{m\to\infty}\epsilon_{m}=0
.

Proof.

The proof follows from
Zhang (2004)
but is given here for the sake of completeness. Let us denote

\Upsilon(\epsilon)=\inf_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{B},\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}):\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\epsilon\}

where the infimum is
\infty
the set is empty.

Then by Proposition 23 of
Zhang (2004)
,
\Upsilon
is non-ngeative,
\Upsilon(0)=0
, and non-decreasing on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Proposition 23 of
Zhang (2004)
further implies that for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{B}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\Upsilon(\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})).

We will now show that if
\epsilon>0
, then
\Upsilon(\epsilon)>0
. For fixed
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{B}
, if
\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon
, then
\kappa(\mathbf{x};p)<\max(\mathbf{p})
, indicating
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})
. Moreover,
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
since
\argmax(\mathbf{0}_{k})=[k]
. Therefore,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}:\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\epsilon}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{<}}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}),

where (a) follows because
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
.
Hence, for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathfrak{B}
,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})>0
if
\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\epsilon
.

Now suppose
\Upsilon(\epsilon)=0
. Then there exists a sequence
(\mathbf{x}^{(m)},\mathbf{p}^{(m)})_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathfrak{B}
such that
\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\geq\epsilon
for all
m\geq 1
but
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}0
. Since
\mathfrak{B}
is bounded,
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}
has a convergent subsequence that converges to
\mathbf{p}^{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{B}}
.
Then

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Psi^*(\mpm)-\Psi(\mxm;\mpm)-\slb\Psi^*(\mp^0)-\Psi(\mxm;\mp^0)\srb}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \absolutevalue{\Psi^*(\mpm)-\Psi^*(\mp^0)}+\absolutevalue{\Psi(\mxm;\mp^0)-\Psi(\mxm;\mpm)},

whose first term goes to zero as
m\to\infty
because
\Psi^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
by Lemma
S6.1
.
For the second term, note that since
\Psi
is linear in
\mathbf{p}
,

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Psi(\mxm;\mp^0)-\Psi(\mxm;\mpm)}=\absolutevalue{\Psi(\mxm;\mp^0-\mpm)}\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\|\mathbf{p}^{0}-\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}

which goes to zero as
m\to\infty
because
\phi
is bounded by Lemma
S6.1
and
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\to_{m}\mathbf{p}^{0}
. Hence, we have showed that

\absolutevalue{\Psi^*(\mpm)-\Psi(\mxm;\mpm)-\slb\Psi^*(\mp^0)-\Psi(\mxm;\mp^0)\srb}\to_{m}0,

which, combined with the fact that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)},\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}0
, implies that

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0}).

On the other hand,
\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\geq\epsilon
for all
m\geq 1
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-|\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})|

\displaystyle\ -|\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})|

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \epsilon-|\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})|-|\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})|.

Since
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\to_{m}\mathbf{p}^{0}
, we have
\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})
. Also

|\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})|=|(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}-(\mathbf{p}^{0})_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}|\leq\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}-\mathbf{p}^{0}\|_{2},

which converges to
0
as
m\to\infty
.
Hence,

\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})-\kappa(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})\geq\epsilon/2
for all sufficiently large
m
. Hence,
\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})
for all sufficiently large
m
. However,
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0})
.
Therefore,

\sup_{\mathbf{p}^{0}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}<\max(\mathbf{p}^{0})}\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{0})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{0}),

which is a contradiction since
\mathbf{p}^{0}\in\overline{\mathfrak{B}}\subset\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
.
Thus, the assumption that
\Upsilon(\epsilon)=0
was wrong. Hence,
\Upsilon(\epsilon)>0
.

Let us extend
\Upsilon
to
\mathbb{R}
by setting
\Upsilon
to be
0
on
(-\infty,0)
. Since
\Upsilon\geq 0
,
\Upsilon
is bounded below by the affine function
f(0)=0
. Then
Proposition 25 of
Zhang (2004)
applied to our case (we need to take
Q=\mathfrak{B}
,
l(\mathbf{p},d)=\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(d,\mathbf{p})
, and
v\equiv 1
in that proposition) implies there is a convex function
\varrho
such that
\varrho(x)\leq\Upsilon(x)
,
\varrho(\epsilon)>0
whenever
\epsilon>0
,
\varrho(0)=0
, and
\varrho
is non-decreasing. Since
\varrho\leq\Upsilon
and
\Upsilon<\infty
on
\mathbb{R}
,
\text{dom}(\varrho)=\mathbb{R}
.

It remains to show that
\varrho(\epsilon_{m})\to_{m}0
if and only if
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
. For the if part, note that
\varrho
is continuous at
0
because
\varrho
is convex and
0\in\text{int}(\text{dom}(\varrho))\equiv\mathbb{R}
. Thus
\lim_{\epsilon\to 0}\varrho(\epsilon)=0
.
Now we prove the only if part. Suppose
\{\epsilon_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
is a subsequence so that
\epsilon_{m}\geq 0
for all
m\geq 1
and
\varrho(\epsilon_{m})\to_{m}0
. Then we claim that
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
. We can replace
\{\epsilon_{m}\}_{m\geq 1}
, if necessary, with a subsequence so that
\epsilon_{m}\geq\epsilon>0
for all
m\geq 1
. Then
\varrho(\epsilon_{m})\geq\varrho(\epsilon)>0
for all
m
along that sequence since
\varrho
is non-decreasing. However, all subsequences of
\varrho(\epsilon_{m})
must go to zero, leading to a contradiction. Hence, we have shown that
\varrho(\epsilon_{m})\to_{m}0
only if
\epsilon_{m}\to 0
.

∎

Lemma S6.4
.

Suppose
m\in\mathbb{N}
and the single-stage surrogate
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]
satisfies Condition
N1
and
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
.
Let
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
satisfy
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Then there exists
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{p})>0
, depending only on
\mathbf{p}
and
\phi
, so that for any sequence
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
satisfying
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
,

\liminf_{m}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))>\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{p}).

Proof.

Had the
\phi(\cdot;i)
’s been zero for all
i\in[k]
, then
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=0
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
. This contradicts that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
. Thus, there exists a
i\in[k]
so that
\phi(\cdot;i)
is not identically zero.
Since
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
, the above implies
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq\max(\mathbf{p})\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)>0
.

Let us define the function

\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})=\sup_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})

for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
.
Since
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
, for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
, it holds that
\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{p})<\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Recall that we denote by
\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}
the unit vector of length one with one in the
i
th position.
For any
\mathbf{p}
, we denote the set

J(\mathbf{p})=\bigg\{\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}\mathbf{p}_{i}:i\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})\bigg\}.

Note that
|J(\mathbf{p})|=|\argmax(\mathbf{p})|
is a finite number.
However, for any
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p})
, since the
\phi
’s are non-negative,

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})=\sum_{i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\mathbf{q}_{i}\leq\sum_{i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}=\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Therefore, for any
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p})
,

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{q})=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}).

By definition of
\overline{\Omega}
,
\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})<\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{q})\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
for each
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p})
satisfying
\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Since
J(\mathbf{p})
is a finite set, it therefore follows that
\max_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})<\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Therefore

\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p}):=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\max_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})

(S6.11)

is positive.
Since
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
, there exists a
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p})
so that
\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Hence, the maximum
\max_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})>-\infty
.
Therefore,
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})\in\mathbb{R}_{>0}
.

Since
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, and
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
,
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
by Fact
S6.1
. Therefore, given any
\epsilon>0
,

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)},\mathbf{p})>\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon
for all sufficiently large
m
. Let us choose
\epsilon<\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})/2
. Note that

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}+\max(\mathbf{p})\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\max(\mathbf{p})\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))=

\displaystyle\ \Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})-\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon-\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}

for all sufficiently large
m
.
Let us define
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}
so that
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}_{i}=\mathbf{p}_{i}
if
i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})
and 0 otherwise. Since

\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}\in J(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m
. Then

\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}=\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{q}^{(m)}).

Since
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
,
\max(\mathbf{p})>0
. There can be two situations. Either
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
or
\max(\mathbf{q}^{(m)})>0
. In the first case,

\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\mathbf{p}_{i}=0
. Therefore,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\geq\frac{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon}{\max(\mathbf{p})}>\frac{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})}{2\max(\mathbf{p})}

because
\epsilon<\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})/2\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})/2
by (
S6.11
).
In the second case,
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{q}^{(m)})

because
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})}=0
.
Suppose
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
satisfies
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{q})
for some
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p})
such that
\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Then it holds that

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})\leq\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})\leq\max_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{p}),\mathbf{q}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p}).

Since
\mathbf{q}^{(m)}\in J(\mathbf{p})
for all sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
, it follows that

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{q}^{(m)})<\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})

given
m
is sufficiently large. Therefore, in the second case,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\geq\frac{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{q}^{(m)})}{\max(\mathbf{p})}\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})-\epsilon}{\max(\mathbf{p})}\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})}{2\max(\mathbf{p})}

since
\epsilon\leq\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})/2
.
Thus, we have shown that for
all sufficiently large
m
,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})}{2\max(\mathbf{p})}.

The definition of
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})
in (
S6.11
) implies that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Hence,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))\geq\frac{\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})}{2\max(\mathbf{p})}.

Since
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})>0
, the proof follows with
\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{p})=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})/2
.
∎

Lemma S6.5
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]
, which is not identiacally zero, satisfies Condition
N1
. Let
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
be such that there exists
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
and
\mathbf{p}\neq 0
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Then there exists a constant
c>0
, depending only on
\psi
, so that
\liminf_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))\geq c
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.5
.

Since
\phi
is not identically zero,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})>0
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
. If not, we can replace
\phi
by
\phi/\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})
. This ensures that we can apply Lemma
S6.4
, which implies

\liminf_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))\geq\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{p}).

Therefore, it suffices to show that
c=\inf_{\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0},\mathbf{p}\neq 0}\mathcal{Z}(\mathbf{p})>0
where
\mathcal{Z}
is as in Lemma
S6.4
.
Suppose, if possible,
c=0
. Then
there exist a sequence
\{\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
and sequences
\{\mathbf{x}^{m,r}\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\neq 0
,
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
but
\liminf_{r}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))\to_{m}0
.
Each
\{\mathbf{x}^{m,r}\}_{r\geq 1}
has a subsequence
\{\mathbf{x}^{m,r^{\prime}}\}_{r^{\prime}\geq 1}
so that

\lim_{r^{\prime}\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r^{\prime}};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r^{\prime}}))=\liminf_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})).

Replacing the original sequences by these subsequences, we obtain sequences
\{\mathbf{x}^{m,r}\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, but

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))=0.

Note that since
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
,
\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}=\sum_{l\in[k]}\mathbf{p}^{(m)}_{l}>0
. Therefore, we obtain that

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)}/\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})}{\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{\to_{r}}}\frac{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})}{\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)}/\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}),

where (a) follows because the map
\mathbf{p}\mapsto\Psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{p})
is linear in
\mathbf{p}
, (b) follows because
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})
, and (c) follows because
\Psi^{*}
is positively homogeneous by Lemma
S6.2
.
Therefore, without the loss of generality, we can write that there exists
\{\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
but

\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))=0.

Since
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
is closed and bounded, there is a subsequence
\{m_{l}\}_{l\geq 1}\subset\{m\}_{m\geq 1}
so that
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
. For the sake of notational simplicity, we will denote the sequence
\{m_{l}\}_{l\geq 1}
by
\{l\}_{l\geq 1}
. Hence
l\to\infty
and
\mathbf{p}^{(l)}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
as
l\to\infty
. We will next show that if
\mathbf{p}^{(l)}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
, then there exists a sequence
\mathbf{x}^{(l)}
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{l}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
but
\lim_{l\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))=0
, which will contradict Lemma
S6.4
, thus completing the proof.

Fix
l\geq 1
. Since
\lim_{m\to\infty}\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r},\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))=0
, there exists
M_{l}\in\mathbb{N}
so that for all
m\geq M_{l}
,

\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r},\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))<1/l.

This implies that for all
m\geq M_{l}
, there exists
R_{m}>0
so that for all
r\geq R_{m}
,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r},\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r}))<1/l.

In particular,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{M_{l},R_{M_{l}}},\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{M_{l},R_{M_{l}}}))<1/l.

Since
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})
for each
m\in\mathbb{N}
, given any
m
and
l
, there exists
\mathfrak{K}_{m,l}>0
so that if
r\geq\mathfrak{K}_{m,l}
, it holds that

|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})|<1/l
.

We choose
\mathbf{x}^{(1)}=\mathbf{x}^{1,1}
. For any
l\in\mathbb{N}
so that
l\geq 2
, we choose
\mathbf{x}^{(l)}=\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}}
so that
m_{l}=\max(m_{l-1}+1,M_{l})
, and
n_{l}=\max(n_{l-1},R_{m_{l}},\mathfrak{K}_{m_{l},l})
. We will now show that if
\mathbf{p}^{(i)}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
, then
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{l}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
.
For each
l\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\mathbf{p}^{*})\leq

\displaystyle|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})-\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})|+|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})|

\displaystyle\ +|\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{*})|.

Since
m_{l}>m_{l-1}
, we have
m_{l}\to\infty
. Thus
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
as
l\to\infty
. Since
\Psi^{*}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
by Lemma
S6.2
, the above implies
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})\to_{l}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
, indicating that the first term in the above bound approaches zero. Since
n_{l}\geq\mathfrak{K}_{m_{l},l}
,
it holds that

|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})|<1/l.

Therefore,

\lim_{l\to\infty}|\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})|=0.

On the other hand,

|\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\mathbf{p}^{*})|\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},l\in[k]}|\phi(\mathbf{x},l)|\|\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\|_{1},

which goes to zero as
l\to\infty
because
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{l})}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
and
\phi
is bounded by Lemma
S6.1
. Therefore, we have shown that if
\mathbf{p}^{(i)}\to_{l}\mathbf{p}^{*}
, then
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{l}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
. Therefore, it just remains to show that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))=0
. Since
m_{l}\geq M_{l}
and
n_{l}\geq R_{m_{l}}
,

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(l)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(l)}))=\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}}))<1/l.

Since
\phi
is non-negative, the above implies that

\lim_{l\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m_{l},n_{l}}))=0,

which proves the desired contradiction.

∎

Lemma S6.6
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
, which is not identiacally zero, satisfies Condition
N1
. The sequences
\{\mathbf{x}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\{\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
are such that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}0
. Moreover, the sequences
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}
satisfy
\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}>0
. Then there exists a constant
\mathcal{J}>0
, depending only on
\psi
, so that
\liminf_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))\geq\mathcal{J}
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.6
.

Since
\liminf_{m\to\infty}\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}>0
,
\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}
is bounded away from
0
. Therefore,

\mathbf{q}^{{(m)}}=\mathbf{p}^{(m)}/\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}
is well defined for all sufficiently large
m\in\mathbb{N}
and

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{q}^{{(m)}})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{q}^{{(m)}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\frac{\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})}{\|\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\|_{1}}\to_{m}0,

where (a) follows because
\Psi^{*}
is positively homogeneous by Lemma
S6.2
and
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\cdot)
is linear for each fixed
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
.
Therefore, we can replace
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}
with
\mathbf{q}^{{(m)}}
.
Note that
\mathbf{q}^{{(m)}}\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
. Hence, without loss of generality, we assume that
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
.

If possible, suppose the assertion in the statement of Lemma
S6.6
is false. This implies there exists
\epsilon>0
and sequences
\{\mathbf{x}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\{\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
such that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}0
but
\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)}))=\mathcal{J}-\epsilon
. Therefore, all subsequences
\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})}
of
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
must satisfy

\lim_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})}))=\mathcal{J}-\epsilon.

We will show that there exists a subsequence
\{m_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\{m\}
so that

\liminf_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})}))\geq\mathcal{J},

which will finish the proof by contradiction. We will consider the following subsequence. Since
\{\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
and
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
is closed, there exists a subsequence
\{m_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\{m\}
so that
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})}\to_{r}\mathbf{p}^{*}\in\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
. It is clear that
\mathbf{p}^{*}\neq 0
. If we can show
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
, then Lemma
S6.5
would imply
\liminf_{r\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})}))\geq\mathcal{J}
, leading to the desired contradiction. Therefore, it is enough to prove that
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{*})
.

To this end, note that

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})

\displaystyle\ +\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})+\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*}),

which converges to zero as
r\to\infty
because (a)
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})\to_{r}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
since
\Psi^{*}
is continuous by Lemma
S6.2
and
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})}\to_{r}\mathbf{p}^{*}
, (b)
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})\to_{r}0
because
\{m_{r}\}_{r\geq 1}\subset\{m\}
and
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\to_{m}0
, and (c)
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*})\to_{r}0
since

\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})};\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})})-\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m_{r})},\mathbf{p}^{*})\leq\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k},i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\|\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})}-\mathbf{p}^{*}\|_{1},

which converges to
0
as
\mathbf{p}^{(m_{r})}\to_{r}\mathbf{p}^{*}
. Hence, the proof follows.

∎

S6.1.2
Properties of
\phi
under both Conditions
N1
and
N2

Lemma S6.7
.

Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
with
C_{\phi}=1
.
Then there exists

\Omega<1
so that for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)<\Omega.

Proof of Lemma
S6.7
.

Fix
i\in[k]
. Let
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=i\}
. Recall that we denote by
\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}
the unit vector with one in the
i
th position. Consider
\mathbf{p}^{(i)}=\mathbf{1}_{k}-\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}
, whose
i
th element is zero but all other elements are one. Clearly, if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, then
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=i\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
. Let
\mathfrak{G}_{i}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
. Note that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})=\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
because
\phi
satisfies Condition
N2
with
C_{\phi}=1
. Thus,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})=1
. Consequently,
\mathfrak{G}_{i}<1
since

\mathfrak{G}_{i}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}^{(i)})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{<}}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})=1,

where step (a) follows from Condition
N1
.

Since
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}^{(i)})=\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]:j\neq i}\phi(\mathbf{x};j)
, we have shown that if
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{i}
, then

\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]:j\neq i}\phi(\mathbf{x};j)\leq\mathfrak{G}_{i}

for some
\mathfrak{G}_{i}<1
. We can show that above holds for all for all
i\in[k]
. Let
\Omega=\max_{i\in[k]}\mathfrak{G}_{i}
. Clearly,
\Omega<1
. Since any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
belongs to
\mathcal{C}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}
, we have

\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]:j\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};j)\leq\alpha_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\leq\Omega,

which completes the proof.
∎

Lemma S6.8
.

Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
. Then there exists
\chi_{\phi}\in(0,1]
, depending only on
\phi
, so that

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq C_{\phi}\chi_{\phi}(\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}))

for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
. Here
C_{\phi}>0
is as in Condition
N2
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.8
.

First, we will consider the case when
C_{\phi}=1
.
Recall from (
S6.3
) that
\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}
. Let us define
z=\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})
. Then

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-z+z\Big(1-\sum_{i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\Big)+\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}(z-\mathbf{p}_{i})\phi(\mathbf{x};i),

which is bounded below by

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-z+\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}(z-\mathbf{p}_{i})\phi(\mathbf{x};i)

since
z\geq 0
and

\sum_{i\in[k]}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\leq\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}C_{\phi}=1,

where (a) follows from Condition
N2
.
However, since
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
,
\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\geq 0
, and
z-\mathbf{p}_{i}\geq z-\max(\mathbf{p})
, the expression in the above display is bounded below by

\displaystyle\max(\mathbf{p})-z+\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}(z-\max(\mathbf{p}))\phi(\mathbf{x};i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ (\max(\mathbf{p})-z)\Big(1-\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\Big).

Lemma
S6.7
implies
\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\leq\Omega
where
\Omega<1
, and it depends only on
\phi
. Since
z=\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})
, it follows that

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq(1-\Omega)(\max(\mathbf{p})-\kappa(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})).

Since
\Omega\in[0,1)
,
1-\Omega\in(0,1]
. Hence, the proof follows for
C_{\phi}=1
. When
C_{\phi}\neq 1
, the proof follows taking
\tilde{\phi}
to be
\phi/C_{\phi}
and applying the above result to
\tilde{\phi}
.

∎

Lemma S6.9
.

Suppose the single-stage surrogate
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\times\mathbb{R}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. Further suppose, there exists
C>0
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{1}_{k})=C
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Then there exists
\mathcal{J}>0
so that
\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))>\mathcal{J}
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.9
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
C=1
. If not, we can replace
\phi
by
\phi/C
so that
\Psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
.
Hence, the conditions of Lemma
S6.4
are satisfied. We let
\overline{\Omega}
,
J
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}
be as in the proof of Lemma
S6.4
. We have shown in the proof of Lemma
S6.4
that
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})>0
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
such that
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
.
Since
\phi
satisfies
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
,

\displaystyle 1=\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1}_{k})=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)+\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})).

Hence,

\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))=1-\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]:i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\phi(\mathbf{x};i)=1-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q}),

where
\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{1}_{k}-\mathbf{e}^{(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))}_{k}
. Note that
\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{1}_{k})
. Moreover,
\argmax(\mathbf{q})=[k]\setminus\{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\}
, indicating
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{q})
.
Therefore,
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{q})\leq\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\geq 1-\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})\geq 1-\sup_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{1}_{k})}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})=\max(\mathbf{1}_{k})-\sup_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{1}_{k})}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})-\sup_{\mathbf{q}\in J(\mathbf{1}_{k})}\overline{\Omega}(\mathbf{q})=\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{1}_{k}).

The proof follows since
\tilde{\mathcal{Z}}(\mathbf{p})>0
for all
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
, as mentioned in the beginning of the proof.
∎

Lemma S6.10
.

Let
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Suppose
\phi:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times[k]\mapsto\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
. Given any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
, if
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle
for some
\mathbf{v}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, then
the vector
\mathbf{v}^{*}
satisfies

\mathbf{v}^{*}\in\text{conv}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{set}})
,
where

\mathcal{C}_{\text{set}}=\{\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}:i\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})\}.

Moreover, if
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, then

\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)\to_{m}0
if
i\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.10
.

The proof is trivial if
\mathbf{p}=\mathbf{0}_{k}
. Thus we assume
\mathbf{p}\neq\mathbf{0}_{k}
.
Without loss of generality, let us assume that
C_{\phi}=1
because otherwise we can replace
\phi
by
\phi/C_{\phi}
. Recall the image set
\mathcal{V}
defined in (
4.3
). Since
C_{\phi}=1
,
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1}_{k})\leq 1
, which implies
\phi(\mathbf{x};i)\leq 1
for each
i\in[k]
and

\mathcal{V}\subset\bigg\{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}:\mathbf{x}_{i}\in[0,1],\quad\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{x}_{i}\leq 1\bigg\}:=\mathcal{C}.

Since
\Phi^{*}
is the support function of
\mathcal{V}
(see the proof of Lemma
S6.2
), and
\Phi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle
, it follows that
\mathbf{v}^{*}
lies on a face of the closed convex hull of
\mathcal{V}

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cc. pp. 145 of)
. Since
\mathcal{V}\subset\mathcal{C}
, and
\mathcal{C}
is both closed and convex,
\overline{\text{conv}(\mathcal{V})}\subset\mathcal{C}
.
Therefore,
\mathbf{v}^{*}\in\mathcal{C}
, which implies
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}\in[0,1]
for each
i\in[k]
and
\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}\leq 1
. Therefore,

\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle\leq\max(\mathbf{p})\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}\leq\max(\mathbf{p}).

Since
\phi
satisfies Condition
N2
, it follows that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
, implying

\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle=\max(\mathbf{p})\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}=\max(\mathbf{p}),

implying
\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}=1
and

\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle=\max(\mathbf{p})\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}.

Therefore,

\sum_{i\in[k]}(\max(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p}_{i})\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}=0,

implying
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}>0
only if
\max(\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{i}
. Since
\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}^{*}_{i}=1
, it follows that
\mathbf{v}^{*}=\sum\limits_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}=\sum\limits_{i\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}\mathbf{e}^{(i)}_{k}\in\text{conv}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{set}})
.

Let
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}_{i}=\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)
. We will show that, if
i\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})
, given any subsequence of
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}_{i}
, we can find a further subsequence that converges to zero. Therefore, it will follow that
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}_{i}\to_{m}0
. Since
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
,
\phi
is bounded by Lemma
S6.1
. Therefore, given any subsequence of
\{\mathbf{x}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}
, we can find a further subsequence so that the
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}_{i}=\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)
’s converge (say to some
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
) for all
i\in[k]
along this subsequence. To simplify notation, we will denote the corresponding subsequences by
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
and
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}
. Hence,
\mathbf{y}^{(m)}\to_{m}\mathbf{v}^{*}
. Therefore, it suffices to show that
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}=0
unless
i\in\argmax(\mathbf{p})
.

Note that
\langle\mathbf{y}^{(m)},\mathbf{p}\rangle\to_{m}\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle
because the
l_{2}
inner product is a continuous function. Noting
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})=\langle\mathbf{y}^{(m)},\mathbf{p}\rangle
and
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
, we obtain that
\langle\mathbf{v}^{*},\mathbf{p}\rangle=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
. Therefore, from the first part, it follows that
\mathbf{v}^{*}\in\text{conv}(\mathcal{C}_{\text{set}})
, which implies
\mathbf{v}_{i}^{*}=0
if
i\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})
.

∎

S6.2
Preliminary auxlliary lemmas

Lemma S6.11
.

Suppose
T\geq 2
,
\mathbb{P}
satisfies Assumptions I-IV, and
(\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{T})
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for
t\in[2:T]
. Then

\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]

(S6.12)

and
\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\argmax(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
for
t\in[2:T]
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.11
.

We will show that

\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg]

for
t\in[2:T]
using induction. Our induction hypothesis is that the above and
\argmax(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\argmax(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
holds for
t\in[2:T]
.
Let
t=T
. Note that (
S6.2
) implies

\mathbf{p}^{*}_{T}(H_{T})_{i}=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\mid H_{T},A_{T}=i\Big]
. Since
Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T},i)=\mathbb{E}[Y_{T}\mid H_{T},A_{T}=i]
for all
i\in[k_{T}]
and
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=\max(\argmax(\mathbf{x}))
for any vector
\mathbf{x}
, it follows that

\begin{split}\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))=&\ \max(\argmax_{i\in[k_{T}]}\mathbb{E}[Y_{T}\mid H_{T},A_{T}=i])=\max(\argmax(Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))\\
=&\ \text{pred}(Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T}),\end{split}

where the last step follows because (
S3.1
) defines
d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
to be
\text{pred}(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
for all
t\in[T]
.
Since
\phi_{t}
’s satisfy Condition
N2
for
t\in[2:T]
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
,

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{t}))=\max(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T})_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\mid A_{T}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T})),H_{T}\bigg]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\mid A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T}),H_{T}\bigg],

where (a) follows because
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\in\argmax(\mathbf{x})
for any vector
\mathbf{x}
and (b) follows because we just showed that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})
.
Since
\mathbb{P}
satisfies Assumption I, (
S6.5
) yields

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\ \bigg|\ H_{T},A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\frac{1[A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})]}{\pi_{T}(A_{T}\mid H_{T})}\ \bigg|\ H_{T}\bigg],

which implies that the induction hypothesis holds when
t=T
.

Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for some
1+t\in[3:T]
. We will show that the induction hypothesis holds for
t
. Since
t\in[2:T]
,
\phi_{t}
satisfies Condition
N2
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
. Therefore,
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\max(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
where by (
S6.2
), for any
i\in[k_{t}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{i}

\displaystyle\ =\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{1+t}\bigg]\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

(S6.13)

since the Induction hypothesis holds for
1+t
.
Thus

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\frac{1[A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))]\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\prod_{j=t}^{T}\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t}\bigg],

where the last step follows from (
S6.5
). Thus to show that the induction hypothesis follows for
t
, it only remains to show that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
.
To show the latter, we will first prove a fact. Since
(Y_{1},\ldots,Y_{t-1})\subset H_{t}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=j\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \Big(\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}\Big)\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=j\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=j\bigg],

(S6.14)

where the last step follows from (
S6.10
).
Since
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\argmax(\mathbf{p}))
for any vector
\mathbf{p}
, (
S6.13
) implies
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
equals

\displaystyle\max\bigg\{\argmax_{j\in[k_{t}]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=j\bigg]\bigg\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \max\bigg\{\argmax_{j\in[k_{t}]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=j\bigg]\bigg\},

where (a) follows due to (
S6.14
).
However, Fact
S11.4
implies

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]=Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i),

implying
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=\text{pred}(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
.
Therefore, the proof follows.
∎

Lemma S6.12
.

Under the setup of Lemma
S6.11
, there exist
c_{1},c_{2}>0
, depending only on
\mathbb{P}
, so that the
\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
defined in (
S6.2
) satisfies
c_{1}<\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(h_{t})_{i}<c_{2}
for all
h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
,
i\in[k_{t}]
, and
t\in[T]
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.12
.

We will use (
S6.13
). By our assumption, there is a constant depending on
\mathbb{P}
, i.e.,
C_{min}(\mathbb{P})
, so that
Y_{t}>C_{min}(\mathbb{P})
for each
t\in[T]
. Hence, (
S6.13
) implies for each
i\in[k_{t}]
,

\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{i}\geq TC_{min}(\mathbb{P})\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg],

which equals
TC_{min}(\mathbb{P})
by
(
S6.10
). Moreover, Assumption IV implies that there exists a constant depending on
\mathbf{p}
, say
C_{max}(\mathbb{P})
, so that
Y_{j}\leq C_{max}(\mathbb{P})
for all
j\in[T]
. Hence,

\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{i}\leq TC_{max}(\mathbb{P})\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]=TC_{max}(\mathbb{P})

by the same argument. Hence, the proof follows.
∎

Lemma S6.13
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
satisfies Assumptions I-V, and if
T\geq 2
, then for
t\in[2:T]
,
\phi_{t}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
. Then
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1})=Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1})
and
V_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.13
.

If
T=1
, then the proof follows trivially because in this case,
p_{1}^{*}(H_{1})_{i}=p_{1}(H_{1})_{i}=E[Y_{1}\mid H_{1},A_{1}=i]=Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1},i)
for all
i\in[k_{1}]
. Hence, we assume that
T\geq 2
.
For
t\in[2:T]
,
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
implies
\Psi^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=1
, which indicates Lemmas
S6.3
–
S6.8
hold.
By (
S6.2
) and (
S6.12
), for any
i\in[k_{1}]
,

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1})_{i}=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}[\Psi_{2}^{*}(p_{2}^{*}(H_{2}))\mid H_{1},A_{1}=i]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=2}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{2}\bigg]\ \bigg|\ H_{1},A_{1}=i\bigg]

where the last step follows by Lemma
S6.11
.
Using Fact
S11.4
in step (a), we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1})_{i}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=2}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{1},A_{1}=i\bigg]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1},i).

That
V_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
follows noting
V_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\max_{i\in[k_{1}]}Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1},i)]
.
∎

Lemma S6.14
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
satisfies Assumptions I-V. Then
V^{\psi}_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
, and for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
,
V^{\psi}(f)=\mathbb{E}[\Psi(f_{1}(H_{1});\mathbf{p}_{1}(H_{1}))]
where
\mathbf{p}_{1}(H_{1})\equiv\mathbf{p}_{1}(H_{1};f)
. In the special case where
\phi_{1}
also satisfies Condition
N2
with
C_{\phi_{1}}=1
, then
V_{*}^{\psi}=V_{*}
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.14
.

We will prove
V^{\psi}_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
by induction. Since the proof of
V^{\psi}(f)=\mathbb{E}[\Psi(f_{1}(H_{1});\mathbf{p}_{1}(H_{1})]
is similar, it is skipped.

Induction hypothesis:
For
t\in[T]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{i}\in\mathcal{F}_{i}:i\in[t:T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{t})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\bigg],

where the product term is one when the range of the product is empty, which occurs only when
t=1
.
When
t=T
, taking
\mathbb{V}=\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{t}
and
h(H_{T})=\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}\{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})/\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})\}
in (
S6.8
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{T}\in\mathcal{F}_{T}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{T-1}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\Psi_{T}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))\bigg].

Thus the induction hypothesis holds for
t=T
. Suppose it holds for some
t\in[2:T]
. We will show that then the induction hypothesis holds for
t-1
. Note that
t-1\in[T-1]
and

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{i}:i\in[t-1:T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \sup_{f_{t-1}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{f_{t-1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{t-2}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{T}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg(\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\frac{\phi_{t-1}(f_{t-1}(H_{t-1});A_{t-1})}{\pi_{t-1}(A_{t-1}\mid H_{t-1})}\bigg)\bigg].

Now we apply (
S6.8
) on the right hand side of the above display at time stage
t-1
with
\mathbb{V}=\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))
and
h(H_{t})=\prod_{j=1}^{t-2}\{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})/\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})\}
. Noting that

\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{V}\mid H_{t-1},A_{t-1}=i]=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\mid H_{t-1},A_{t-1}=i]=\mathbf{p}_{t-1}^{*}(H_{t-1})_{i}

by (
S6.2
), and applying (
S6.8
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\sup_{f_{t-1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{t-2}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{T}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg(\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\frac{\phi_{t-1}(f_{t-1}(H_{t-1});A_{t-1})}{\pi_{t-1}(A_{t-1}\mid H_{t-1})}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle\ =\sup_{f_{t-1}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1}^{t-2}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{T}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\Psi_{t-1}^{*}(p_{t-1}^{*}(H_{t-1}))\bigg],

which implies that the induction hypothesis holds for
t-1
. Therefore, the induction hypothesis holds for all
t\in[1:T]
. Thus, setting
t=1
, we obtain that

V_{*}^{\psi}=\sup_{f_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{t}:t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{\phi_{j}(f_{j}(H_{j});A_{j})}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))].

Finally, in the special case,
\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))=\max(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))
by Assumption
N2
. Hence,
V_{*}^{\psi}=\mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
, which is
V_{*}
by Lemma
S6.13
.
∎

Auxiliary lemmas for induction steps

Lemmas
S6.15
and
S6.16
are used during the induction steps in the proofs of Theorem
4.1
and
4.2
, respectively.

Lemma S6.15
.

Suppose
\mathbb{P}
satisfies Assumptions I-V,
\phi_{1}
satisfies Condition
N1
and for
t\in[2:T]
,
\phi_{t}
satisfies Conditions
N1
and
N2
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
. Let

\displaystyle Z_{\text{diff,t}}=

\displaystyle\ \frac{\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)1[A_{t}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]}{\prod_{r=t}^{T}\pi_{r}(A_{r}\mid H_{r})}\prod_{r=t+1}^{T-1}\phi_{r}\Big(f_{r}(H_{r});\text{pred}(f_{r}(H_{r}))\Big)

\displaystyle\ \times\left\{\prod_{r=1+t}^{T}1[A_{r}=d_{r}^{*}(H_{r})]-\prod_{r=1+t}^{T}1[A_{r}=\text{pred}(f_{r}(H_{r}))]\right\},

where the product terms in the expectation are one if the range is empty.
Then
for
t=1,\ldots,T
, for all
f\in\mathcal{F}
, and for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
,
\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\text{diff,t}}\mid\ H_{t}\right]
is non-negative and

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t}))\geq\Big(\min_{1+t\leq i\leq T}\chi_{\phi_{i}}\Big)\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbb{E}\left[Z_{\text{diff,t}}\mid\ H_{t}\right],

(S6.15)

where the
\chi_{\phi_{t}}
’s are as in Lemma
S6.8
and
p_{t}(H_{t})\equiv p_{t}(H_{t};f)
.

Proof of Lemma
S6.15
.

First of all, the assumptions imply Lemma
S6.8
is applicable to
\phi_{2},\ldots,\phi_{T}
.
When
t=T
, we can show that the LHS of (
S6.15
) is zero for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
because
\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(h_{T})=\mathbf{p}_{T}(h_{T})
for all
h_{T}\in\mathcal{H}_{T}
by definition. Also, the RHS of (
S6.15
) is zero in this case because the range
[1+T,T]
is empty. Thus,(
S6.15
) trivially follows for
t=T
. Therefore, it suffices to consider the case
t\in[T-1]
, which implies we also consider
T\geq 2
.

We will prove the lemma by Induction. Our induction hypothesis is that (
S6.15
) holds and the RHS is non-negative for
t\in[T-1]
. First, we will show that (
S6.15
) holds for
t=T-1
. Then we will show that if (
S6.15
) holds for
t+1\in[2:T-1]
, then it holds for
t
as well. When
t=T-1
, the LHS of (
S6.15
) equals

\displaystyle\Psi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{T-1}^{*}(H_{T-1}))-\Psi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{T-1}(H_{T-1}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{T-1}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{T-1}(H_{T-1})_{i}-\mathbf{p}_{T-1}(H_{T-1})_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{T-1}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{T}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-\Psi_{T}(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T}))\mid H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=i]\Big)

by (
S6.2
).
Since
\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}=\mathbf{p}_{T}
, it follows that the above is non-negative. Lemma
S6.8
a applies to
\phi_{T}
because
T\geq 2
. Applying Lemma
S6.8
, we obtain that there exists
\chi_{\phi_{T}}>0
, depending only on
\phi_{t}
, so that

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{T-1}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-\Psi_{t}(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))\mid H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=i]\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \chi_{\phi_{T}}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{T-1}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};i)\mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-\kappa(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T}))\mid H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=i]

which is bounded below by

\chi_{\phi_{T}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-\kappa(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T}))\mid H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})],

which is non-negative because
\mathbf{p}_{T}=\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}
. However,

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}[\max(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-\kappa(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T}))\mid H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]

(S6.16)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\frac{1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))]-1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))]}{\pi_{T}(A_{T}\mid H_{T})}\bigg|H_{T-1},A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)1[A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]\frac{1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))]-1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))]}{\prod_{t=T-1}^{T}\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg|H_{T-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)1[A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]\frac{1[A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})]-1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))]}{\prod_{t=T-1}^{T}\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg|H_{T-1}\bigg]

where (a) follows from (
S6.9
) with
i=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))
, (b) follows from an application of (
S6.5
), and (c) follows because
\text{pred}(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})
by Lemma
S6.13
.
Since

\max(\mathbf{p}_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))\geq\kappa(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}^{*}_{T}(H_{T}))=\kappa(f_{T}(H_{T});\mathbf{p}_{T}(H_{T})),

(
S6.16
) also implies that

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)1[A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]\times\frac{1[A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})]-1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))]}{\prod_{t=T-1}^{T}\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\ \bigg|\ H_{T-1}\bigg],

which is non-negative.
Since
\chi_{\phi_{T}},\phi_{T-1}\geq 0
, it also follows that

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{T-1}^{*}(H_{T-1}))-\Psi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{T-1}(H_{T-1}))\geq\chi_{\phi_{T}}\phi_{T-1}(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)1[A_{T-1}=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})]\frac{1[A_{T}=d_{T}^{*}(H_{T})]-1[A_{T}=\text{pred}(f_{T}(H_{T}))]}{\prod_{t=T-1}^{T}\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg|H_{T-1}\bigg],

which is non-negative, implying that the induction hypothesis holds for
t=T-1
. If
T=2
, then there is nothing to prove. Suppose
T\geq 3
and that the induction hypothesis is true for
t+1
where
t\in[1,T-2]
. We will show that the induction hypothesis is true for
t
, which will complete the proof of the current lemma.
Note that

\displaystyle\ \Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbf{p}^{*}_{t}(H_{t})_{i}-\mathbf{p}_{t}(H_{t})_{i}\Big)

(S6.17)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});\mathbf{p}_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]\Big)

by (
S6.2
) since
t\in[T-2]
. However, the above equals

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});\mathbf{p}^{*}_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]\Big)}_{S_{1}}

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\underbrace{\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)\Big(\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});\mathbf{p}^{*}_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});\mathbf{p}_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]\Big)}_{S_{2}}.

(S6.18)

Since
1+t\in[2:T-1]
, Lemma
S6.8
can be applied to
\phi_{1+t}
, yielding

\displaystyle S_{1}\geq
