(S7.18)

and the products are one if the ranges are empty.
Since
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{m,t};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})\to_{m}\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})
for all
t\notin\{r,1+r\}
by construction, to show
V^{\psi}(f^{(m)})\to_{m}V^{\psi}_{*}
, it suffices to show that
\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\gamma^{(m)})\to_{m}\Psi^{*}_{r}(\nu)
where
\nu\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{r}}
is as in (
S7.10
).
To this end, we note that

\displaystyle\absolutevalue{\Psi_r(\xpmr;\gamma^\upm)-\Psi_r^*(\nu)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \bigg|\sum_{i=1}^{k_{r}}\Big(\phi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};i)\gamma^{(m)}_{i}-\phi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};i)\nu_{i}\Big)\bigg|+\absolutevalue{\sum_{i=1}^{k_r}\phi_r(\xpmr;i)\nu_i-\Psi_r^*(\nu)}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{r}},i\in[k_{r}]}\phi_{r}(\mathbf{x};i)\sum_{i=1}^{k_{r}}\absolutevalue{\gamma^\upm_i-\nu_i}+\absolutevalue{\Psi_r(\xpmr;\nu)-\Psi_r^*(\nu)},

where we used the non-negativity of
\phi_{r}
.
By Lemma
S6.1
,
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{r}},i\in[k_{r}]}\phi_{r}(\mathbf{x};i)
is finite. For each
i\in[k_{r}]
,

\displaystyle|\gamma^{(m)}_{i}-\nu_{i}|=|\Psi_{1+r}(\mathcal{M}^{m,i};\mathbf{p}^{(i)})-\Psi^{*}_{1+r}(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})|

which goes to
0
as
m\to\infty
by the definition of
\mathcal{M}^{m,i}
in (
S7.15
). On the other hand,
\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\nu)\to_{m}\Psi_{r}^{*}(\nu)
by (
S7.16
). Therefore, we have shown that
\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\gamma^{(m)})\to_{m}\Psi_{r}^{*}(\nu)
, which completes the proof of this step.

Step 1e. Calculating
V(f^{(m)})

We will apply Lemma
S7.4
with
\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=\phi_{\text{dis}}(\mathbf{x};i)=1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=i]
for
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
and
t\in[T]
.
Equation
S7.17
yields that

\displaystyle V(f^{(m)})=\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\gamma^{(m)})\prod_{t=1}^{r-1}\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{m,t};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})\prod_{t=r+2}^{T}\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{m,t};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}}),

where
\phi_{t}\equiv\phi_{\text{dis}}
and
\gamma^{(m)}
is as in (
S7.18
).
For
\phi_{\text{dis}}
, we have already shown that
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}
for any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k_{t}}
. Therefore,
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x}^{m,t};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=1
for each
t\notin\{r,1+r\}
and
\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\gamma^{(m)})=\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}
.
Therefore,

V(f^{(m)})=\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}.

Step 1f. Implication of Fisher consistency

Suppose
\phi_{t}
’s are Fisher consistent. Since
V^{\psi}(f^{(m)})\to_{m}V^{\psi}_{*}
by Step 1d, Fisher consistency implies
V(f^{(m)})\to_{m}V_{*}
. Step 1e shows that that
V(f^{(m)})=\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}
and Step 1c shows that
V_{*}=\max_{i\in[k_{r}]}\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
. Therefore, Fisher consistency yields that
\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\to_{m}\max_{i\in[k_{r}]}\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
. We will use use this convergence to show that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})=1
for all sufficiently large
m
. The definition of
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}
in (
S7.16
) implies that
\Psi_{r}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r};\nu)\to_{m}\Psi_{r}^{*}(\nu)
. Since we proved that
\phi_{r}
satisfies Condition
N1
, Fact
S6.1
applies. Hence,

\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})\in\argmax(\nu)
for all sufficiently large
m
. Therefore, if
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})=1
for all sufficiently large
m
, it will follow that
1\in\argmax(\nu)
. Then the definition of
\nu
in (
S7.10
) would imply that
\Psi_{1+r}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq\Psi_{1+r}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
, completing the proof of Step 1. Thus it suffices to show that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})=1
for all sufficiently large
m
.

To this end, note that (
S7.13
) implies

\gamma^{(m)}_{i}=(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})_{\text{pred}(\mathcal{M}^{m,i})}\leq\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
for each
i\in[k_{r}]
. Let us denote the vector
v^{0}=(\max(\mathbf{p}^{(1)}),\ldots,\max(\mathbf{p}^{(k_{r})}))
. Therefore,
\gamma^{(m)}_{i}\leq\mathbf{v}^{0}_{i}
for each
i\in[k_{r}]
. Hence, it holds that

\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\leq v^{0}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\leq\max(v^{0}).

Since
\max(v^{0})=\max_{i\in[k_{r}]}\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
and Fisher consistency implies
\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\to_{m}\max_{i\in[k_{r}]}\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})
, it follows that
\gamma^{(m)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\to_{m}\max(v^{0})
. Therefore, it follows that
v^{0}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})}\to_{m}\max(v^{0})
. Since
\argmax(v^{0})=\argmax_{i\in[k_{r}]}\max(\mathbf{p}^{(i)})=1
for
\mathbb{P}\in\mathcal{P}_{\text{small}}
, it follows that
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{m,r})=1
for all sufficiently large
m
.

S7.3.2
Step 2: Proving
\max(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{q})

implies
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
for all
\mathbf{p},\mathbf{q}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k_{t}}
and
t>1

Suppose
t\in[2:T]
.
Consider
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}=\mathbf{p}+(1/m)\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}}
. Then
\max(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})=\max(\mathbf{p})+1/m>\max(\mathbf{q})
. Taking
r=t-1
in the previous step, we obtain that

\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\geq\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
. Therefore,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p}^{(m)})\geq\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
. Since
\mathbf{p}^{(m)}\to_{m}\mathbf{p}
and
\Psi_{t}^{*}
is continuous for each
t\in[T]
by Lemma
S6.2
, it follows that
\Psi^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{p})\geq\Psi^{*}_{t}(\mathbf{q})
. Switching the role between
\mathbf{p}
and
\mathbf{q}
, we can similarly prove that
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
, which implies
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{q})
. Therefore, the function
\Psi_{t}^{*}:\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k_{t}}\mapsto\mathbb{R}
satisfies
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=G_{t}(\max(\mathbf{p}))
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k_{t}}
for some
G_{t}:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
.

S7.3.3
Step 3: Showing that
G_{t}
is linear on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
for
t\in[2:T]

Now, we will show that
G_{t}
is positively homogenous on
\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
in that
G_{t}(x)=C_{t}x
for some
C_{t}>0
for all
x\geq 0
. Note that if
x\geq 0
, then

G_{t}(x)=G_{t}(\max(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}}))=\Psi_{t}^{*}(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}}).

However,

\Psi_{t}^{*}(x\mathbf{1}_{k_{2}})=x\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}}\sum_{i=1}^{k_{t}}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=x\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}}).

Hence, we have shown that
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{x})=\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})\max(\mathbf{x})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k_{t}}
. The proof follows noting
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})>0
by Lemma
S6.1
.

S8
Proofs of the lemmas from Section
4

S8.1
Single-stage case: proof of Lemma
4.1

Proof of Lemma
4.1
.

The sufficiency part follows directly from Theorem
4.1
. We will only consider the proof of the necessity of Condition
N1
.
In this case
T=1
and
f=f_{1}
.
Note that given any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>0}
, we can find a
\mathbb{P}
satisfying Assumptions I-V so that
\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}\mid H_{1},A_{1}=i]=\mathbf{p}_{i}
for all
i\in[k]
. In this case,

Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1},A_{1})=\mathbb{E}[Y_{1}\mid H_{1},A_{1}]=\mathbf{p}_{A_{1}}
.
From Lemma
S7.2
, it follows that

V^{\psi}(f)=\sup_{f_{1}\in\mathcal{F}_{1}}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});A_{1})}{\pi_{1}(A_{1}\mid H_{1})}Y_{1}\bigg]=\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}).

Equation
S7.7
implies that
V^{\psi}(f)=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});\mathbf{p})]
.
The rest of the proof follows similarly to Section
S7.2
, and hence skipped.
∎

S8.2
Proof of Lemma
4.2

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

In this proof, we will use that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})>0
, which follows from Lemma
S6.1
since
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
.

Only if part:
Suppose Condition
N2
holds. Without loss of generality, let us assume that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
. Otherwise, we can replace
\phi
by
\phi/\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})
. Since
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
,
C_{\phi}
is one. Thus, we need to show that
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\subset\text{conv}(\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
. Note that it suffices to show

\displaystyle\{\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(k)}\}\subset\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi}

(S8.1)

because
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\subset\text{conv}(\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
would follow taking convex hull on both sides of (
S8.1
). Convex hull of a set in
\mathbb{R}^{k}
is same as that of its closure, cf. page 31 of
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal (2004)
. Therefore,
\text{conv}(\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})=\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
, which would complete the proof of
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\subset\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
.
To prove (
S8.1
), will show that
\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(1)}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi}
because the proof will follow similarly for the other
\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(i)}
’s. Consider
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>0}
such that
\argmax(\mathbf{p})=1
. Then from Lemma
S6.10
, it holds that there exists a sequence
\{\mathbf{v}^{(m)}\}_{m\geq 1}\subset\mathcal{V}_{\phi}
such
that
\mathbf{v}^{(m)}_{i}\to_{m}0
if
i\neq 1
and
\langle\mathbf{v}^{(m)},\mathbf{p}\rangle\to_{m}\mathbf{p}_{1}
. Since
\mathbf{p}_{1}>0
,
\mathbf{v}^{(m)}_{1}\to_{m}1
. Here Lemma
S6.10
applies because both Conditions
N1
and
N2
hold with
C^{\phi}=1
. Therefore,
\mathbf{v}^{(m)}\to_{m}\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(1)}
. Hence, it follows that
\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(1)}\in\overline{\mathcal{V}}_{\phi}
. Therefore, the proof of (
S8.1
) follows.

If part:
Suppose
C_{\phi}\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\subset\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
where
C_{\phi}=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1})=1
. Otherwise, we can replace
\phi
by
\phi/\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})
. Thus, we have
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}\subset\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})
. Hence,

\varsigma_{\mathcal{S}^{k-1}}\leq\varsigma_{\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})}

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Theorem 3.3.1, p. 151,)
. Also, the support function of
\mathcal{S}^{k-1}
equals the support function of
\{\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(1)},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{k}^{(k)}\}

(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, cf. Prop 2.2.1, p. 137,)
. Hence, for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}}
,

\varsigma_{\mathcal{S}^{k-1}}(\mathbf{p})=\max\{\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{k}\}=\max(\mathbf{p}).

Therefore, we have obtained that for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
,

\max(\mathbf{p})\leq\varsigma_{\text{conv}({\mathcal{V}}_{\phi})}(\mathbf{p})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})
.
If
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{\geq 0}
, then

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=\max(\mathbf{p})

because we assumed
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
. Therefore, for
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}_{>0}
,
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
. Hence, Condition
N2
holds.
∎

S8.3
Proof of Lemma
4.3

Proof of Lemma
4.3
.

Note that for any
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi_{a,b}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=b\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi(a\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=b\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=bC^{\phi}\max(\mathbf{p})

because
\phi
satisfies Condition
N2
.
Hence,
\phi_{a,b}
satisfies Condition
N2
.
Also,

\sup_{\mathbf{x}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi_{a,b}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=b\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(a\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}).

Note that
\text{pred}(a\mathbf{x})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})
since
a>0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(a\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}:\text{pred}(a\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(a\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})<\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})

since
\phi
satisfies (
4.4
). Thus it follows that

b\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi_{a,b}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})<b\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi_{a,b}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p}),

indicating that
\phi_{a,b}
satisfies Condition
N1
. Hence, we showed that
\phi_{a,b}
satisfies Condition
N1
and Condition
N2
.

With an abuse of notation, we denote the surrogate
\phi+c
by
\phi_{c}
and the corresponding
\Psi
and
\Psi^{*}
transforms as
\Psi_{c}
and
\Psi^{*}_{c}
, respectively. Observe that

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi_{c}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})+c\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{<}}

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})+c\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{i}=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Psi_{c}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\Psi_{c}^{*}(\mathbf{p}),

where (a) follows because
\phi
satisfies Condition
N1
.
Therefore,
\phi_{c}
satisfies Condition
N1
.
However,

\Psi_{c}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\Psi_{c}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})+c\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{i}=C^{\phi}\max(\mathbf{p})+c\sum_{i\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{i}

because
\phi
satisfies Condition
N2
. Therefore,
\phi_{c}
does not satisfy Condition
N2
unless
c=0
.
∎

S8.4
Restricted class: Proof of Lemma
4.4

As in Proposition
1
, it suffices to prove the result for the case where
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for all
t\in[T]
, since the general case then follows by replacing
\phi_{t}
with
\phi_{t}/C_{\phi_{t}}
.
Note that since
af\in\mathcal{L}
for all
a>0
and
f\in\mathcal{L}
, if we can prove that
\lim_{a\to\infty}V^{\psi}(af^{*})=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}V^{\psi}(f)
, then

\sup_{f\in\mathcal{F}}V^{\psi}(f)=\sup_{f\in\mathcal{L}}V^{\psi}(f)
, which, combined with Proposition
1
, completes the proof. Hence, it suffices to show that
\lim_{a\to\infty}V^{\psi}(af^{*})=V^{\psi}_{*}
. Lemma
S6.14
implies that under Conditions
N1
and
N2
,
V^{\psi}_{*}=\mathbb{E}[\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]
where
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1})
is as in (
S6.2
). However, Condition
N2
implies
\Psi_{1}^{*}(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))=\max(\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))
. Lemma
S6.13
implies
\mathbf{p}_{1}^{*}(H_{1})=Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1})
. Thus
V^{\psi}_{*}=\mathbb{E}[Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1})]
. However, Fact
S11.4
implies
V_{*}=\mathbb{E}[Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1})]
. Therefore,
V^{\psi}_{*}=V_{*}
.
Hence, it suffices to show that
\lim_{a\to\infty}V^{\psi}(af^{*})=V_{*}
.

If
\argmax(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t}))
is singleton, then Condition
N3
implies

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to\infty}\phi(af^{*}_{t}(H_{t});j)=1[j=\argmax(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t}))].

Thus

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\lim_{a\to\infty}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\phi(af^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1[j=\argmax(f_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg)=1.

Under the setup of the current lemma,
\argmax(f_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
for some version of
d_{t}^{*}
with
\mathbb{P}
-probability one. Therefore, the above implies

\mathbb{P}\bigg(\lim_{a\to\infty}\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\phi(af^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}=\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1[j=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg)=1.

Since Condition
N1
is satisfied,
Lemma
S6.1
implies that the
\phi
’s are all bounded. Assumption I implies that the
\pi_{t}
’s are all bounded below and Assumption IV implies that the
Y_{t}
’s are bounded above. Therefore,

\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\phi(af^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}<C

for some constant
C>0
potentially depending on
\mathbb{P}
.
Hence, the dominated convergence theorem implies that

\displaystyle\lim_{a\to\infty}V^{\psi}(af^{*})=

\displaystyle\ \lim_{a\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{\phi(af^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{t=1}^{T}\frac{1\Big[A_{t}=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})\Big]}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg],

which equals
V_{*}
, thus completing the proof.

S8.5
Proof of the results for the kernel-based surrogates in Section
4.2.1

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Without loss of generality, let us assume
C=1
in (
4.10
).
Since
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=\max(\argmax(\mathbf{x}))
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, (
4.10
) implies

\displaystyle\phi(\mathbf{x},j)=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})=j]K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\prod_{i=j+1}^{k}1[\mathbf{x}_{j}-Z_{j}>\mathbf{x}_{i}-Z_{i}]\prod_{i=1}^{j-1}1[\mathbf{x}_{j}-Z_{j}\geq\mathbf{x}_{i}-Z_{i}]dP_{Z}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ P_{Z}\bigg(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}+\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}\text{ for all }i\neq j,\ i\in[k]\bigg)

(S8.2)

where
(a)
uses the fact that
P_{Z}(Z_{j}=Z_{i}+\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i})=0
for all
i
and
j
in
[k]
, which follows because the
Z_{i}
’s have density. Now

\displaystyle P_{Z}\bigg(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}+\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}\text{ for all }i\neq j,\ i\in[k]\bigg)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[P_{Z}\bigg(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}+\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}\text{ for all }i\neq j,\ i\in[k]\bigg|Z_{j}\bigg)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\neq j}P_{Z}\bigg(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}+\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}\bigg|Z_{j}\bigg)\bigg]

because
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k}
are i.i.d. given
Z_{j}
.
Since they are independent of
Z_{j}
as well, the above equals

\displaystyle E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z_{j}+\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg]=E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg],

where
Z
is a random variable with distribution
F_{\mathbb{K}}
. Thus the first part of the lemma follows.
Suppose
j\in\argmax(\mathbf{x})
. Then
\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}\geq 0
for all
i\in[k]
. Thus,

1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j})\geq 1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z).

Therefore,
\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\geq E_{\mathbb{K}}[(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z))^{k-1}]
.
Now note that since
Z\sim F_{\mathbb{K}}
,
F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z)\sim\mathbb{U}(0,1)
. Hence,
1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z)\equiv U\sim\mathbb{U}(0,1)
as well. Thus
\phi(\mathbf{x},\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\geq\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{U}^{k-1}]=1/k
. Hence, the second part of the proof follows.

∎

S8.5.1
Forms of the kernel-based
\phi
in special cases

Lemma
4.5
implies

\phi(\mathbf{x};j)=CE_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\neq j}\overline{F}_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{x}_{j})\bigg]\quad\text{ for all }j\in[k],

where
\overline{F}_{\mathbb{K}}=1-F_{\mathbb{K}}
.
If
\mathbb{K}
is symmetric about 0, then

\phi(\mathbf{x};j)=CE_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\neq j}F_{\mathbb{K}}(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}-Z)\bigg]\quad\text{ for all }j\in[k].

Suppose
k=3
and
C=1
. Then it follows that

\phi(\mathbf{x};i)=\begin{cases}\eta_{s}(\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{2},\mathbf{x}_{1}-\mathbf{x}_{3})&\text{ if }i=1\\
\eta_{s}(\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{x}_{3},\mathbf{x}_{2}-\mathbf{x}_{1})&\text{ if }i=2\\
\eta_{s}(\mathbf{x}_{3}-\mathbf{x}_{1},\mathbf{x}_{3}-\mathbf{x}_{2})&\text{ if }i=3\end{cases}

where for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
,

\eta_{s}(x,y)=\begin{cases}E_{\mathbb{K}}[F_{\mathbb{K}}(x-Z)F_{\mathbb{K}}(y-Z)]&\text{ if }\mathbb{K}\text{ is symmetric about }0\\
E_{\mathbb{K}}[\overline{F}_{\mathbb{K}}(Z-x)\overline{F}_{\mathbb{K}}(Z-y)]&\text{ otherwise. }\end{cases}

(S8.3)

In Section
3
, we used
\eta
to denote the template for two-stage PERM losses, so
\eta_{s}
may appear to be an overloaded notation. However, the expressions above show that
\phi
is a single-stage PERM loss with template
\eta_{s}

(see also
Wang and Scott, 2023b
, for the single-stage definition of PERM losses)
. Here, the subscript
s
indicates ”single stage.”

Since logistic is a symmetric (w.r.t. 0) distribution, when
\mathbb{K}
is the standard logistic density, straightforward algebra yields

\eta_{s}(a,b)=\frac{e^{a+b}\left(a\left(e^{b}-1\right)^{2}+\left(e^{a}-1\right)\left(-e^{a}b+\left(e^{b}-1\right)\left(e^{a}-e^{b}\right)+b\right)\right)}{\left(e^{a}-1\right)^{2}\left(e^{b}-1\right)^{2}\left(e^{a}-e^{b}\right)}

for all
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
When
\mathbb{K}
is the standard Gumbel density,
straightforward algebra yields

\eta_{s}(a,b)=\frac{e^{a+b}-1}{(1+e^{a})(1+e^{b})}+\frac{1}{1+e^{a}+e^{b}}\text{ for all }a,b\in\mathbb{R}.

S8.5.2
Proof of Lemma
4.6

Proof of Lemma
4.6
.

Without loss of generality, we assume
C=1
. Therefore, to show
C_{\phi}=C
, it suffices to show that
C_{\phi}=1
.

First we will show that

\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
, which would imply that
\phi
satisfies Condition
N2
with
C_{\phi}=1
. Note that

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=

\displaystyle\ \sum_{j\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{j}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})=j]K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\ =\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Big(\sum_{j\in[k]}\mathbf{p}_{j}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})=j]\Big)K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\ =\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}\leq\max(\mathbf{p})\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}.

Since
\int K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}=1
, we have
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})
. Suppose
j\in\argmax(\mathbf{x})
. Let
x_{n}=n\mathbf{e}^{(k)}_{j}
. Then

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi(x_{n};\mathbf{p})=\lim_{n\to\infty}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

by the dominated convergence theorem since
\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{u})}\leq\max(\mathbf{p})
and
\int K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}=1
. However,

\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}_{n}-\mathbf{u})}=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}_{n})}=\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{e}^{(k)}_{j})}=\mathbf{p}_{j}.

Therefore,
\lim_{n\to\infty}\Psi(x_{n};\mathbf{p})=\mathbf{p}_{j}=\max(\mathbf{p})
. Hence, we have proved that
\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
, which implies Condition
N2
holds with
C_{\phi}=1
.

Now we will show that Condition
N1
holds. To this end, it suffices to show that (
4.7
) holds.
Suppose
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\notin\argmax(\mathbf{p})
.
Then
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})
equals

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{u}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}+\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{u}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{u}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}+\max(\mathbf{p})\displaystyle\int_{\mathbf{u}:\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{u})\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}K(\mathbf{u})d\mathbf{u}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)+\max(\mathbf{p})P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)

where
\vec{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{k})
with the
Z_{i}
’s being i.i.d. with common density
\mathbb{K}
, and
P_{K}
is the probability measure induced by
K
, the joint density of the random vector
\vec{Z}
.
Therefore, we have obtained that

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})-P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\Big(\max(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\Big),

which, noting
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
, implies

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\Big(\max(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}\Big).

(S8.4)

Observe that if
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=\text{pred}(-\vec{Z})
, then
\argmax(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})\ni\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})
.
Letting
P_{Z}
denote the probability measure corresponding to the distribution of the
Z_{i}
’s, we obtain that

\displaystyle P_{K}\Big(\argmax(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})\ni\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\geq

\displaystyle\ P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ P_{Z}\Big(-Z_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}>-Z_{i},i\neq\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ P_{Z}\Big(-Z_{1}>-Z_{i},i\in[2:k]\Big),

where the last step follows because
-Z_{1},\ldots,-Z_{k}
are i.i.d., and hence, exchangeable, and
\mathbf{x}
is non-stochastic. The RHS of the above display is
2^{-(k-1)}
because the
Z_{i}
’s have a density.
Now note that

\displaystyle P_{K}\Big(\argmax(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})\ni\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\leq

\displaystyle\ P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)

\displaystyle\ +P_{K}\Big(\argmax(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})\text{ is not singleton}\Big),

whose second term vanishes because
\vec{Z}
has a density. Thus we have shown that

\displaystyle P_{K}\Big(\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}-\vec{Z})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})\Big)\geq 2^{-(k-1)}.

Therefore, (
S8.4
) implies (
4.7
) holds with
\chi_{\phi}=1
.

It remains to show that Condition
N3
holds. To this end, it suffices to show that if
\argmax(\mathbf{x})=j
, then
\lim_{a_{n}\to\infty}\phi(a_{n}\mathbf{x},j)=1
. By (
4.10
),

\displaystyle\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}\phi(a_{n}\mathbf{x},j)=

\displaystyle\ \liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}(a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j}))\Big)\bigg],

where (a) follows by Fatou’s lemma since
0\leq F_{\mathbb{K}}\leq 1
and (b) follows because
F_{\mathbb{K}}
is continuous on
\mathbb{R}
since it has a density. Since
\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}>0
for all
i\in[k]
such that
i\neq j
,

a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j}\to_{n}-\infty
. Thus, the RHS on the above display is one.
Thus we have shown that
\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}\phi(a_{n}\mathbf{x},j)\geq 1
. However,
\phi(\mathbf{x};j)\leq\Psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
since
\Psi(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{1}_{k})=1
for the kernel-based surrogate. Therefore,
\lim_{a_{n}\to\infty}\phi(a_{n}\mathbf{x},j)=1
in this case.

Now suppose
\argmax(\mathbf{x})
is singleton and
j\notin\argmax(\mathbf{x})
. Then

1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\to_{a.s.}0.

Therefore,

\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\to_{a.s.}0.

Therefore, by the dominated convergence theorem, it follows that

\displaystyle\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg],

which equals zero. The proof follows since

\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}\phi(a_{n}\mathbf{x},j)=\liminf_{a_{n}\to\infty}E_{\mathbb{K}}\bigg[\prod_{i\in[k]:i\neq j}\Big(1-F_{\mathbb{K}}(Z+a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}-a_{n}\mathbf{x}_{j})\Big)\bigg].

∎

S8.6
Proof of Lemma
4.7
for the product-based losses

Proof of Lemma
4.7
.

Without loss of generality, let us assume that
C=1
, which implies

\tau=1-F_{\mathbb{K}}
. Because
\mathbb{K}
is symmetric about zero,
1-F_{\mathbb{K}}=F_{\mathbb{K}}
, implying
\tau=F_{\mathbb{K}}
in this case. Since
\tau
is a distribution function in this case, it is non-decreasing. Hence, for the surrogate in (
4.11
),
\phi(\mathbf{x};\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}))\geq\tau(0)^{k-1}
. If
\mathbb{K}
is symmetric about zero, then
\tau(0)=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}1[y\leq 0]\mathbb{K}(y)dy=1/2
. Hence,
\mathcal{J}=2^{-(k-1)}
for such
\phi
’s.

Proving Conditions
N1
,
N2
, and (
4.7
):

Since we assumed
C=1
, it suffices to show that Conditions
N1
,
N2
, and (
4.7
) hold with
C_{\phi}=1
.
If we can show that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
, and

\displaystyle\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})-\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq(\max\mathbf{p}-\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})/2,

(S8.5)

then it will follow that Condition
N2
holds with
C_{\phi}=1
and (
4.7
) holds with
\chi_{\phi}=1/2
. The latter would imply that Condition
N1
holds. Thus it suffices to show (
S8.5
).

First, we will show that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq\max(\mathbf{p})
. To see this, consider
\mathbf{x}^{(m)}=m\mathbf{e}^{(k)}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{p})}
. In this case,
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x}^{(m)})=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p})
. Since
\tau
is a distribution function, it follows that
\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)=1
if
i=\text{pred}(\mathbf{p})
and
\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};i)=0
for all other
i
’s in
[k]
. Therefore,
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})\to_{m}\max(\mathbf{p})
. Since
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})
, it follows that

\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq\lim_{m\to\infty}\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{(m)};\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p}).

Therefore, to show that Condition
N2
holds, it suffices to show that
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})
.

We will show that given any
k\in\mathbb{N}
,

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq(\max(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})/2
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Note that the above implies

\displaystyle 2\max(\mathbf{p})-2\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\geq\max(\mathbf{p})-\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})},

(S8.6)

which, combined with
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\geq\max(\mathbf{p})
, leads to (
S8.5
) with
\chi_{\phi}=1/2
.
Also,
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq(\max(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})/2
implies
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})\leq\max(\mathbf{p})
, from which Condition
N2
follows. Therefore, it suffices to prove (
S8.6
). We will use mathematical induction to prove this. Our induction hypothesis is that (
S8.6
) holds for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. We will first show that (
S8.6
) holds for
k=1
. Next we will show that if (
S8.6
) hold for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
, it holds for
k+1
as well, which will complete the proof.

When
k=1
,
\mathbf{x}=x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mathbf{p}=p\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}
. Note that
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=p\tau(x)
. Since
\tau(x)
is a distribution function,
\sup_{x\in\infty}\tau(x)=1
and
\Psi^{*}(\mathbf{p})=p=\max(\mathbf{p})
. Also
\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})}=p
. Since
\tau
is a distribution function,
\tau(x)\leq 1
. Thus

\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})=p\tau(x)\leq p=(\max(\mathbf{p})+\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})/2=(p+\mathbf{p}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})/2,

implying (
S8.6
) holds for
k=1
.

Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
.
Consider
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+1}
and
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k+1}
. Let
\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})=i^{*}
. Suppose
r\in[k]
is such that
r=\max\{\argmax_{i\in[k]:i\neq i^{*}}\mathbf{x}_{i}\}
. Note that we allow the case
\mathbf{x}_{r}=\max(\mathbf{x})
, which can happen if
\argmax(\mathbf{x})
is not singleton. Also,
\mathbf{x}_{r}\leq\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}
and
\mathbf{x}_{r}\geq\mathbf{x}_{i}
for all
i\in[k]\setminus\{r,i^{*}\}
.
Note that
\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})
equals

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\phi(\mathbf{x};i^{*})+\sum_{j\in[k]:j\neq i^{*}}\mathbf{p}_{j}\phi(\mathbf{x};j)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{r})\prod_{i\notin\{r,i^{*}\}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{i})+\sum_{j\neq i^{*}}\mathbf{p}_{j}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\prod_{i\notin\{i^{*},j\}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}).

Here, the products will be one if the range is empty, which is the case when
k=2
since
r\neq i^{*}
.
Since
\tau
is a distribution function,
\tau\leq 1
. Also, since
\tau
is a distribution function, it is non-decreasing, implying
\tau(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\leq\tau(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})
for all
j\neq i^{*}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{r})+\tau(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\sum_{j\in[k_{2}]:j\neq i^{*}}\mathbf{p}_{j}\prod_{i\notin\{i^{*},j\}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}).

Let us denote

\mathbf{p}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{p}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}-1},\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}+1},\ldots,\mathbf{p}_{k+1})\text{ and
}\mathbf{x}^{\prime}=(\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}-1},\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}+1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{k+1}).

Then
\mathbf{p}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}_{\geq 0}^{k}
and
\mathbf{x}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Let us also denote by
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime};\mathbf{p}^{\prime})
the
k
-dimesnional version of
\phi
. Since the induction hypothesis holds for
k
,
\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime};\mathbf{p}^{\prime})\leq\max(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})
.

Note that the last display can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\Psi(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{p})\leq

\displaystyle\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{r})+\tau(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\Psi(\mathbf{x}^{\prime};\mathbf{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{r})+\tau(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\max(\mathbf{p}^{\prime})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}-\mathbf{x}_{r})+\tau(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}})\max(\mathbf{p})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{x\geq 0}\Big(\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(x)+\tau(-x)\max(\mathbf{p})\Big),

where we used the fact that
\mathbf{x}_{i^{*}}\geq\mathbf{x}_{r}
.
Since
\mathbb{K}
is symmetric about zero,
\tau(-x)=1-\tau(x)
. Therefore,
The derivative of the function
x\mapsto\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(x)+\max(\mathbf{p})\tau(-x)
is
\mathbb{K}(x)(\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}-\max(\mathbf{p}))
, which is non-positive because
\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\leq\max(\mathbf{p})
and
\mathbb{K}(x)\geq 0
as
\mathbb{K}
is a density. Thus on the positive half-line,
\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}\tau(x)+\max(\mathbf{p})\tau(-x)
is maximized at
x=0
. However, since
\mathbb{K}
is symmetric about zero,
\tau(0)=1/2
, which implies
\Psi(x;\mathbf{p})\leq(\mathbf{p}_{i^{*}}+\max(\mathbf{p}))/2
. Thus, we have shown that the induction hypothesis holds for
k+1
. Hence, the induction hypothesis holds for all
k\in\mathbb{N}
, which completes the proof.

Proving Condition
N3
:

Since
\tau
is a distribution function for
C=1
,
\tau(\infty)=1
and
\tau(-\infty)=0
. Note also that we have shown that
C_{\phi}=1
. If
\argmax(\mathbf{x})=j
, then

\lim_{a\to\infty}\phi(a\mathbf{x};j)=\prod_{i\neq j}\lim_{a\to\infty}\tau(a(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}))=1.

If
j\notin\argmax(\mathbf{x})
, and
\argmax(\mathbf{x})
is singleton,
a(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{\text{pred}(\mathbf{x})})\to-\infty
as
a\to\infty.
The proof follows noting that, in this case,

\lim_{a\to\infty}\phi(a\mathbf{x};j)=\prod_{i\neq j}\lim_{a\to\infty}\tau(a(\mathbf{x}_{j}-\mathbf{x}_{i}))=0.

∎

S9
Proofs for Section
6.2

S9.0.1
Proof of Lemma
6.1

Proof.

Proposition
1
implies

C_{*}(V_{*}-V(\widehat{f}))\leq V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f}).

Since
\mathcal{J}_{t}>0
for all
t\in[T]
, it follows that
C_{*}>0
.
Recall from Algorithm
1
that
\widehat{f}=\text{tran}(\widehat{g})
where the
tran
operator is as in (
5.2
). Since we defined
V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)
to be
V^{\psi}(\text{tran}(g))
,
V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})=V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\widehat{g})
.
From Section
5
, it follows that for relative-margin-based surrogates,
V^{\psi}_{*}=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{W}}V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)
. Thus
V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})=\sup_{g\in\mathcal{W}}V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)-V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\widehat{g})
,
implying the following decomposition of the
\psi
-regret
V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})
:

\displaystyle\sup_{g\in\mathcal{W}}V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)-V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\tilde{g})+(V^{\psi,\text{rel}}-\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}})(\tilde{g})-(V^{\psi,\text{rel}}-\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}})(\widehat{g})

\displaystyle\ +\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\tilde{g})-\sup_{g\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)+\underbrace{\sup_{g\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)-\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\widehat{g})}_{\text{Optimization error: }\texttt{Opt}_{n}}.

The proof follows noting
\widehat{V}^{\psi}(\tilde{g})\leq\sup_{g\in\mathcal{U}_{n}}\widehat{V}^{\psi}(g)\leq 0
because
\mathcal{U}_{n}\ni\tilde{g}
.
∎

S9.1
Proof of Result
4

Proof of Result
4
.

Proof follows trivially if
j\in\argmax(\mathbf{x})
. Suppose
j\notin\argmax(\mathbf{x})
. Let
\text{pred}(x)=i
. Then

\displaystyle\psi(x;j)=

\displaystyle\ P_{\mathbb{K}}\Big(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i^{\prime}}+x_{j}-x_{i^{\prime}}\quad\text{ for all }i^{\prime}\neq j,i^{\prime}\in[k]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ P_{\mathbb{K}}\Big(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}+x_{j}-x_{i}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ P_{\mathbb{K}}\Big(Z_{j}\leq Z_{i}-(x_{i}-x_{j})\Big)

However, since
x_{i}-x_{j}>0
,

P_{\mathbb{K}}(Z_{i}-Z_{j}\geq x_{i}-x_{j})=P_{\mathbb{K}}\Big((Z_{i}-Z_{j})^{2}>(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}\Big)\leq\frac{E_{\mathbb{K}}[(Z_{1}-Z_{2})^{2}]}{(x_{i}-x_{j})^{2}}

by Markov’s inequality since
Z_{1}-Z_{2}
has finite second moment. Moreover,

C_{a}=E_{\mathbb{K}}[(Z_{1}-Z_{2})^{2}]=E_{\mathbb{K}}[Z_{1}^{2}]+E_{\mathbb{K}}[Z_{2}^{2}]=2E_{\mathbb{K}}[Z_{1}^{2}]>0
because
E_{\mathbb{K}}[Z_{1}^{2}]>0
.
∎

S9.2
Proof of Theorem
6.1

Without loss of generality, we assume that
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for all
t\in[T]
. If that is not the case, we can replace the
\phi_{t}
’s by
\phi_{t}/C_{\phi_{t}}
.
Our first step is to express
V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(f)
in terms of the optimal Q-functions. To this end, we will need the following lemma.

Lemma S9.1
.

Under the setup of Theorem
6.1
, for any
f=(f_{1},\ldots,f_{T})\in\mathcal{F}
,

\displaystyle V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(f)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=1}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg].

Lemma
S9.1
is proved in
Section
S9.3
.
Since
\phi_{t}\geq 0
, and
\sum_{j\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};j)\leq\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=1
, if follows that
\phi_{t}\leq 1
for all
t\in[T]
. Moreover, since
\pi_{t}>C_{\pi}
for all
t\in[T]
, using Lemma
S9.1
and (
S6.6
), we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(f)

\displaystyle\ \leq\frac{\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big]}{C_{\pi}^{T}}.

(S9.1)

For fixed
i\in[k_{t}]
,

\displaystyle\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)}_{S_{1}}

\displaystyle\ -\underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)}_{S_{2}}.

Defining
\tilde{h}=\text{tran}(\tilde{g})
, we will now show that when
f=b_{n}\tilde{h}
, then

S_{1}\lesssim C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}z^{-1}+z1[H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{ti}]

(S9.2)

and

S_{2}\lesssim C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}\delta_{n}^{-1}+\delta_{n}1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{\prime}}.

(S9.3)

S9.2.1
Bound on
S_{1}

For fixed
t\in[T]
,
i\in[k_{t}]
, and
z>0
, let us denote
\mathcal{E}_{ti}=\{h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}:Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};i)\leq z\}
. Ideally,
\mathcal{E}_{ti}
should depend on
z
, but we omit the dependence for notational simplicity. Let us denote the random variables
1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{c}}
and
1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{c}}
by
1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}}
and
1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{c}}
, respectively.
Then

\displaystyle S_{1}=

\displaystyle\ \underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{c}}}_{S_{11}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}}}_{S_{12}}.

We apply Condition
N3.s
on the first term to obtain

\psi(a_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)\leq C_{a}\Big(b_{n}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)\Big)\Big)^{-2}.

Thus

S_{11}\lesssim C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i))\Big)^{-1}1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{c}}\leq C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}z^{-1}

because for
H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}^{c}_{t}
,
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)>z>0
.
Finally, (
S9.2
) follows, noting, since
\phi_{t}\leq 1
for all
t\in[T]
, we have
S_{12}\leq z1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}}
.

S9.2.2
Bound on
S_{2}

Now for fixed
t\in[T]
,
i\in[k_{t}]
, and for any
\delta_{n}>0
, we denote
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti}=\{h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}:Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};i)\leq 3\delta_{n}\}
. Here we suppress the dependence of
\mathcal{E}
on
n
for the sake of simplicity. Now notice that
S_{2}
is bounded above by

\displaystyle\underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(b_{n}\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)1_{(\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{\prime})^{c}}}_{S_{21}}

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(b_{n}\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)1_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti}}}_{S_{22}}.

We will analyze
S_{21}
first, for which, we use Lemma
S9.2
below. This lemma is proved in Supplement
S9.3.1
.

Lemma S9.2
.

Under the setup of Theorem
6.1
, for all
t\in[T]
,
h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
, and
i\in[k_{t}]
, it holds that,

\displaystyle\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}(h_{t})-\tilde{h}_{ti}(h_{t})\geq Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t};i)-2\delta_{n}\text{ where }\tilde{h}_{t}=\text{tran}(\tilde{g}_{t}).

(S9.4)

Since
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}

implies
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)>3\delta_{n}
, Lemma
S9.2
implies the following for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
:

\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}(H_{t})-\tilde{h}_{ti}(H_{t})>\delta_{n}>0\quad\text{for all }i\neq{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}.

Therefore,
\argmax(\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t}))=\{{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\}
for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
. On the other hand, Condition
N3.s
implies

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(b_{n}\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t});i)\leq

\displaystyle\ b_{n}^{-2}C_{a}\Big(\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}(H_{t})-\tilde{h}_{ti}(H_{t})\Big)^{-2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ b_{n}^{-2}C_{a}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)-2\delta_{n}\Big)^{-2},

where the last step follows from Lemma
S9.2
and the fact that
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)-2\delta_{n}>\delta_{n}>0
for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
.
Moreover, if
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)>3\delta_{n}
, then

2\delta_{n}<\frac{2}{3}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big),

which implies, for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
,

Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)-2\delta_{n}\geq\frac{1}{3}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big).

Therefore, for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
,

\phi_{t}(b_{n}\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t});i)\leq 9a_{n}^{-2}C_{a}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)^{-2}.

On the other hand, Condition
N3.s
applied on
\phi_{t}(a_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)
implies that

\phi_{t}(a_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)\leq C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)^{-2}.

Therefore, we have shown that for
H_{t}\in(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}
,

\displaystyle\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(b_{n}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(b_{n}\tilde{h}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ 10C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};{d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})})-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)^{-1},

which implies
S_{21}\leq 10C_{a}b_{n}^{-2}\delta_{n}^{-1}1_{(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti})^{c}}
. On the other hand, from the definition of
\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{\prime}
, and using
\phi_{t}\leq 1
,
it is not hard to see that

S_{22}\leq 6\delta_{n}1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}^{\prime}}
.
Thus, (
S9.3
) is proved.

Combining (
S9.1
), (
S9.2
), and (
S9.3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(b_{n}\tilde{h})\lesssim

\displaystyle\ \frac{1}{C_{\pi}^{T}}\bigg(Tb_{n}^{-2}C_{a}(z^{-1}+\delta_{n}^{-1})+\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}(z1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}}+\delta_{n}1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti^{\prime}}})\Big]\bigg).

Let
\mathcal{E}_{t}=\{h_{t}:\mu(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))\leq z\}
and
\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}=\{h_{t}:\mu(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))\leq 3\delta_{n}\}
.
Then

\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}1_{\mathcal{E}_{ti}}\leq(k_{t}-1)1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\text{ and }\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}1_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{ti}}\leq(k_{t}-1)1_{\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t}}.

By the small noise assumption,
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{t})\leq z^{\alpha}
and
\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}^{\prime}_{t})\lesssim\delta_{n}^{\alpha}
.
Thus

V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(b_{n}\tilde{h})\lesssim\frac{1}{C_{\pi}^{T}}\bigg(b_{n}^{-2}z^{-1}C_{a}+b_{n}^{-2}\delta_{n}^{-1}C_{a}+z^{1+\alpha}+\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}\bigg).

We want the
z^{1+\alpha}
term to match
\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}
. Thus, letting
z=\delta_{n}

V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(b_{n}\tilde{h})\lesssim\frac{1}{C_{\pi}^{T}}\bigg(b_{n}^{-2}\delta_{n}^{-1}C_{a}+\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}\bigg).

Since
b_{n}>\delta_{n}^{-(1+\alpha/2)}
, it follows that
\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}>b_{n}^{-2}\delta_{n}^{-1}
. Thus

V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(b_{n}\tilde{h})\lesssim\frac{1}{C_{\pi}^{T}}(1+C_{a})\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}.

Since
\sup_{g\in\mathcal{W}}V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)=V^{\psi}_{*}
for relative-margin-based surrogates, and
V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(b_{n}\tilde{g})=V^{\psi}(b_{n}\text{tran}(\tilde{g}))=V^{\psi}(b_{n}\tilde{h})
, the proof follows.

S9.3
Proof of the auxiliary lemmas for proving Theorem
6.1

Lemma S9.3
.

Under Assumptions I-V, for all
t\in[T]
and
i\in[k-t]
,

\max(p_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-p_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)=Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i).

(S9.5)

Proof of Lemma
S9.3
.

Note that for
j\in[k_{T}]
, (
S6.2
) implies

\displaystyle p_{T}^{*}(H_{T};j)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\mid H_{T},A_{T},A_{T}=j\Big]=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}[Y_{T}\mid H_{T},A_{T}=j]+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T-1}Y_{i},

which equals
Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T},j)+\sum_{i=1}^{T-1}Y_{i}
.
Therefore,

\max(p_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-p_{T}^{*}(H_{T};i)=\max(Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T};i).

Hence, the proof follows for
t=T
. For
t=1
, the proof follows from Lemma
S6.13
. Suppose
T>2
.
For
t\in[2:T-1]
, using (
S6.13
) we obtain that for
i\in[k_{t}]
,

\displaystyle p_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum\limits_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg],

whose last term is
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)
by Fact
S11.4
and the first term

\displaystyle\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}\Big)\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}

because

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]=1

by (
S6.10
).
Therefore, for
1<t<T
and
i\in[k_{t}]
,

p_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)=Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)+\sum\limits_{i=1}^{t-1}Y_{i}.

Hence, the lemma follows for
t\in[2:T-1]
.
∎

Proof of Lemma
S9.1
.

Without loss of generality, we assume that
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for each
t\in[T]
. Otherwise, we can replace
\phi_{t}
by
\phi_{t}/C_{\phi_{t}}
for each
t\in[T]
.
Since
\phi_{t}
is symmetric for each
t\in[T]
, it holds that
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})
for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
. Since
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=C_{\phi_{t}}=1
, it follows that
\Psi_{t}(\mathbf{x};\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=\sum_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
. This fact will be used repeatedly in the proof.
We will also use the notation introduced in Section
S6.0.1
for the proof of Theorem
4.1
.

For
t\in[T]
, let us define

\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Psi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});p_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-\Psi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});p_{t}(H_{t}))\mid H_{t}\bigg].

Since
p_{T}^{*}(H_{T})=p_{T}(H_{T})
, we obtain that
\mathcal{T}_{T}=0
.
For
t\in[T-1]
, we obtain that
\mathcal{T}_{t}
equals

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big(p_{t}^{*}(H_{t})_{i}-p_{t}(H_{t})_{i}\Big)\mid H_{t}\Big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\Big(p_{t}^{*}(H_{t};A_{t})-p_{t}(H_{t};A_{t})\Big)\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}\Big]\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1+t}^{*}(p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}\Big]\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))

\displaystyle\ -\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}\Big]\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1+t}]}\begin{matrix}\Big(\max(p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t};i)\Big)\\
\times\phi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});i)\end{matrix}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}\right]\bigg|H_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathbb{E}\left[\begin{matrix}\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))\\
-\Psi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});p_{1+t}(H_{1+t}))\end{matrix}\bigg|H_{1+t}\right]\bigg|H_{t}\right],

where (a) uses (
S6.6
) and (b) uses the facts: (i)
\Psi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{p})=\max(\mathbf{p})
for all
\mathbf{p}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+t}_{\geq 0}
since
\phi_{t}
satisfies Condition
N2
with
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
, (ii)
\sum_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
for each
t\in[T]
and
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
, as shown previously, and (ii)
H_{1+t}\supset\{H_{t},A_{t}\}
. The second term on the RHS of the above display equals

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mathcal{T}_{1+t}\bigg|H_{t}\bigg].

Note that Lemma
S9.3
implies

\max(p_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-p_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)=Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i).

Then it follows that for
t\in[T-1]
,
\mathcal{T}_{t}
equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg(\mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{1+t}]}\Big(\max(Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t};i)\Big)

\displaystyle\ \times\phi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});i)\mid H_{t},A_{t}\Big]+\mathcal{T}_{1+t}\bigg)\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg\{\frac{\phi_{1+t}(f_{1+t}(H_{1+t});A_{1+t})}{\pi_{1+t}(A_{1+t}\mid H_{1+t})}

\displaystyle\ \times\bigg(\max(Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))-Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t};A_{1+t})\Big)+\mathcal{T}_{1+t}\bigg\}\bigg|H_{t}\bigg]

(S9.6)

by (
S6.6
).
We claim that for any
t\in[T-1]
,

\mathcal{T}_{t}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=t+1}^{T}\prod_{i=t}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg|H_{t}\bigg].

(S9.7)

Note that since
\mathcal{T}_{T}=0
, (
S9.6
) implies

\mathcal{T}_{T-1}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\begin{multlined}\phi_{T-1}(f_{T-1}(H_{T-1});A_{T-1})\\
\\
\times\ \phi_{T}(f_{T}(H_{T});A_{T})\end{multlined}}{\begin{multlined}\pi_{T-1}(A_{T-1}\mid H_{T-1})\\
\\
\times\ \pi_{T}(A_{T}\mid H_{T})\end{multlined}}\Big(\max(Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T}))-Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T};A_{T})\Big)\bigg|H_{T-1}\right].

Hence, (
S9.7
) holds for
t=T-1
. Suppose (
S9.7
) holds for
t\in[2:T-1]
. Then (
S9.6
) implies

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{t-1}=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=t+1}^{T}\prod_{i=t-1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg|H_{t-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{i=t-1}^{t}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};A_{t})\Big)\bigg|H_{t-1}\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=t}^{T}\prod_{i=t-1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg|H_{t-1}\bigg].

Hence, (
S9.7
) holds for
t-1
as well. Therefore, by induction, (
S9.7
) holds for
t=1
and

\mathcal{T}_{1}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg|H_{1}\bigg].

By the definition of
\mathcal{T}_{t}
,

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{T}_{1}]=\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}(H_{1}))\Big].

Hence, it follows that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}(H_{1}))\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg].

Using Lemma
S6.14
, we can show that
V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(f)
equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1}^{*}(p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))\Big]+\mathbb{E}\Big[\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}(H_{1}))\Big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}[\max(p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-\Psi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\sum\limits_{j=1}^{k_{1}}\phi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});i)\Big(\max(p_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-p_{1}^{*}(H_{1})_{i}\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(c)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\phi_{1}(f_{1}(H_{1});A_{1})}{\pi_{1}(A_{1}\mid H_{1})}\Big(\max(Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1}))-Q_{1}^{*}(H_{1};A_{1})\Big)\Big]

\displaystyle\ +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\sum\limits_{j=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{j}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\Big(\max(Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j}))-Q_{j}^{*}(H_{j};A_{j})\Big)\bigg],

where (a) follows since
\phi_{1}
satisfies Condition
N1
, (b) follows because
\phi_{1}
satisfies
\sum_{i\in[k_{1}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
, and (c) follows from Lemma
S9.3
and (
S6.6
). Therefore, the proof follows.

∎

S9.3.1
Proof of Lemma
S9.2

Proof of Lemma
S9.2
.

We consider three cases: (i)
d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})\neq 1
,
i\neq 1
(ii)
d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})=1
,
i\neq 1
, and (iii)
d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})\neq 1
,
i=1
. We will show that (
S9.4
) holds in case (i) and (ii). The proof for case (iii) follows similarly to that of case (ii), and hence skipped.

Case (i)

Since
\tilde{h}_{t}=\text{tran}(\tilde{g}_{t})
, for
i\geq 2
,
\tilde{h}_{ti}(h_{t})=-\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})
. Thus,

\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}(h_{t})-\tilde{h}_{ti}(h_{t})=\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})-\tilde{g}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})-1}(h_{t}),

which equals

\displaystyle\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})-(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},1)-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i))-\Big(\tilde{g}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})-1}(h_{t})-\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},1)

\displaystyle\ -Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))\Big)\Big)+Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i)-2\sup_{j\in[k_{t}-1]}\|\tilde{g}_{t,j}-(Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,1)-Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,1+j)\|_{\infty}.

Thus (
S9.4
) follows noting that our assumption on
\tilde{g}
and (
6.4
) imply

\sup_{j\in[k_{t}-1]}\|\tilde{g}_{tj}-(Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,1)-Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,1+j))\|_{\infty}=\|\tilde{g}_{tj}-\texttt{blip}_{tj}\|_{\infty}\leq\delta_{n}.

(S9.14)

Case (ii)

Since
d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})=1
and
\tilde{h}_{t}=\text{tran}(\tilde{g}_{t})
, it follows that
\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}(h_{t})=0
. However, since
i\geq 2
,
\tilde{h}_{ti}(h_{t})=-\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})
.
Thus

\displaystyle\ \tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}(h_{t})-\tilde{h}_{t,i}(h_{t})=\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})

\displaystyle\ =\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})-(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},1)-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i))+Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i)

where we used
d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})=1
. However,
|\tilde{g}_{t,i-1}(h_{t})-(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},1)-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i))|\leq\delta_{n}
by (
6.4
) and (
S9.14
).
Thus

\tilde{h}_{t,d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}(h_{t})-\tilde{h}_{t,i}(h_{t})\geq Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t},i)-\delta_{n}.

∎

S10
Proofs for Section
6.3
: combined regret decay rate

S10.1
Proof of Theorem
6.2

Without loss of generality, we assume that
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for each
t\in[T]
. Otherwise, we can replace each
\phi_{t}
by
\phi_{t}/C_{\phi_{t}}
. As shown in the proof of Lemma Proof of Lemma
S9.1
,
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
implies
\sum_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
. Also, as shown in the proof of Theorem
6.1
,
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
implies
\Phi_{t}^{*}(\mathbf{1}_{k_{t}})=1
, which indicates
\phi_{t}\leq 1
for all
t\in[T]
. These facts will be used repeatedly in our proof. Throughout this proof, we will denote
\text{tran}(\tilde{g})
by
\tilde{f}
. We also remind the readers that
\widehat{f}=\text{tran}(\widehat{g})
. Since the
\phi_{t}
’s satisfy the conditions of Proposition
1
with positive
\mathcal{J}
, the regret of
\widehat{f}
is bounded by a constant multiple of the
\psi
-regret
V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})
, which we will denote by
r_{n}
from now on. Therefore, it suffices to prove the concentration bound on
r_{n}
. Since
\widehat{f}
is stochastic,
r_{n}
is also a random variable. Parts of the proof of Theorem
6.2
follow similar arguments as Theorem 5 in
Laha et al. (2024b)
. Therefore, we focus on the parts that differ from that proof. First, we introduce some new functions.

It will be convenient for us to write
V^{\psi}_{*}
as
V^{\psi}(f)
for some
f
. There are extended-valued scores for which the above is possible.
Let us define

f^{*}=(f^{*}_{1},\ldots,f^{*}_{T})
with
f^{*}_{t}:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto\overline{\mathbb{R}}^{k_{t}}
so that

\displaystyle f^{*}_{ti}(H_{t})=\begin{cases}\infty&\text{ if }i=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})\\
0&\text{otherwise,}\end{cases}

(S10.1)

where
d^{*}
is any version of the optimal DTR.
Note that we can write
f^{*}_{t}(H_{t})=\infty\times\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))}_{k_{t}}
.
We define
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});j):=\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))}_{k_{t}};j)
. Let us fix
h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}
. Condition
N3.s
implies that for
j\neq d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})

\limsup_{m\to\infty}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};j)\leq\lim_{m\to\infty}C_{a}m^{-2}=0.

Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};j)=0\text{ for all }j\neq d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}).

(S10.2)

Since we showed that under our assumptions,
\sum_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
, it follows that

\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1-\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{t}]:j\neq i}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};j)\text{ for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}},

implying that

\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))=1-\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{t}]:j\neq d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};j).

Hence,

\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))=1-\sum\limits_{j\in[k_{t}]:j\neq d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})}\lim_{m\to\infty}\phi_{t}(m\mathbf{e}^{(d_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))}_{k_{t}};j)=1

by (
S10.2
). Therefore, by our definition,

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(h_{t});j)=1[j=d_{t}^{*}(h_{t})],\quad j\in[k_{t}],t\in[T].

(S10.3)

With the above definition of
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});j)
’s,
V^{\psi}(f^{*})
is well-defined.

Lemma S10.1
.

Let
f^{*}
is as defined in (
S10.1
).
Then under the conditions of Theorem
6.2
,
V^{\psi}(f^{*})=V^{\psi}_{*}=V_{*}
when
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for all
t\in[T]
.

Proof of Lemma
S10.1
.

When
C_{\phi_{t}}=1
for all
t\in[T]

Lemma
S6.14
implies

V^{\psi}_{*}=V_{*}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1}^{T}\frac{\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});j)}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg],

which equals
V^{\psi}(f^{*})
.
Here the last step follows from (
S10.3
).

∎

For any
u,u^{\prime}\in\mathcal{F}
, define

\mathfrak{U}_{u,u^{\prime}}(\mathcal{D}):=\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\frac{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(u_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(u^{\prime}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})}{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}.

(S10.4)

The proof of Lemma
6.1
shows that

r_{n}=V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})\leq\text{Approximation error}+\text{ Estimation error }+\texttt{Opt}_{n},

where the approximation error is

\sup_{g\in\mathcal{W}}V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(g)-V^{\psi,\text{rel}}(\tilde{g})=V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\text{tran}(\tilde{g}))=V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\tilde{f}),

the estimation error is

(V^{\psi,\text{rel}}-\widehat{V}^{\psi,\text{rel}})(\tilde{g}-\widehat{g})=(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})[\L(\mathcal{D};\widehat{g})-\L(\mathcal{D};\tilde{g})],

and
\texttt{Opt}_{n}
is the optimization error.
Let us denote the approximation error by
\texttt{App}_{n}
.
We denote the absolute value of the estimation error is by
\texttt{Est}_{n}
, i.e.,
\texttt{Est}_{n}=|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})[\L(\mathcal{D};\widehat{g})-\L(\mathcal{D};\tilde{g})]|
.
Thus

r_{n}\leq\texttt{App}_{n}+\texttt{Est}_{n}+\texttt{Opt}_{n}.

Since for any

i\in[k_{t}]
,

\Gamma_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=\phi_{t}(\text{tran}(\mathbf{x});i)
by Definition
4.1
, it is not hard to see that

\L(\mathcal{D};\widehat{g})-\L(\mathcal{D};\tilde{g})=\mathfrak{U}_{\text{tran}(\widehat{g}),\text{tran}(\tilde{g})}(\mathcal{D})=\mathfrak{U}_{\widehat{f},\tilde{f}}(\mathcal{D})

because
\tilde{f}=\text{tran}(\tilde{g})
and
\widehat{f}=\text{tran}(\widehat{g})
. Thus
\texttt{Est}_{n}=|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})\mathfrak{U}_{\widehat{f},\tilde{f}}|
.

Note that
\delta_{n}^{-(1+\alpha/2)}=n^{1/2}(\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n})^{-1/2}
, which is less than
b_{n}
by our assumption on
b_{n}
. Therefore,
\texttt{App}_{n}
can be bounded directly by applying Theorem
6.1
, which yields
\texttt{App}_{n}\lesssim\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha}
. The non-trivial step is bounding
\texttt{Est}_{n}
. To this end, first, we need a bound on
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}
, which can be bounded using
r_{n}
and
\texttt{App}_{n}
.

Lemma S10.2
.

Under the setup of Theorem
6.2
, there exists a constant
C>0
depending only on
\mathbb{P}
, so that

\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}\leq C\bigg(V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}

where
\alpha
is as in Assumption
1
.

The above lemma is proved in Section
S10.2.1
. It implies

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\tilde{f}}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq C\bigg(V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(\tilde{f})\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}=C\bigg(V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\tilde{f})\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}

because
V^{\psi}(f^{*})=V^{\psi}_{*}
by Lemma
S10.1
. However, since

V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\tilde{f})=\texttt{App}_{n}\lesssim\delta_{n}^{1+\alpha},

we have
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\tilde{f}}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P},2}\lesssim C\delta_{n}^{\alpha}
. Thus

\displaystyle[\|\mathfrak{U}_{\widehat{f},\tilde{f}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\tilde{f}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}+\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\widehat{f}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\lesssim}}\delta_{n}^{\alpha/2}+r_{n}^{\alpha/\{2(1+\alpha)\}}:=\epsilon_{n},

(S10.5)

where (a) follows since
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\tilde{f}}\|^{2}_{\mathbb{P},2}\lesssim C\delta_{n}^{\alpha}
and by Lemma
S10.2
,

\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},\widehat{f}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}^{2}\leq C\bigg(V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}=C\bigg(V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(\widehat{f})\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}\lesssim r_{n}^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}.

Next, define the set

\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon)=\bigg\{\mathfrak{U}_{f,\tilde{f}}:f=\text{tran}(g),g\in\mathcal{U}_{n},\|\mathfrak{U}_{f,\tilde{f}}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq\epsilon\bigg\}.

Note that (
S10.5
) implies

\mathfrak{U}_{\widehat{f},\tilde{f}}\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})
. Therefore, we can bound
\texttt{Est}_{n}
by

\displaystyle\texttt{Est}_{n}\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})u|=\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})u|]

\displaystyle\ +\underbrace{\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})u|-\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})u|]}_{\text{deviation term}}

Bounding the deviation term requires Talagrand’s inequality, and this part is exactly similar to
Laha et al. (2024b)
if we plug in our
\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})
in the proof of step 3 of Theorem 5 therein.
From the symmetrization inequality
(
Koltchinskii, 2009
, cf. Theorem 2.1 of)
, it follows that

\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|(\mathbb{P}_{n}-\mathbb{P})u|]\lesssim\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|\mathfrak{R}_{n}(u)|]

where
\mathfrak{R}_{n}
is the Rademacher complexity. The number
\mathbb{E}[\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})}|\mathfrak{R}_{n}(u)|]
is called
the Rademacher complexity of the class
\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon)
. Our next step is to show that the bracketing entropy
N_{[]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))
of
\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})
is of the order
(\mathcal{I}_{n}/\epsilon)^{\rho_{n}^{\prime}}
. It can be then used to derive an upper bound on the Rademacher complexity of the class
\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})
using the following fact given by (3.12), pp.40, of
Koltchinskii (2009)
.

Fact S10.1

(Fact from
Koltchinskii (2009)
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{G}
is a function-class with envelope
F
such that
\|F\|_{\infty}\leq C
, where
C>0
. Further suppose there exists
\rho_{n}>0
such that

N_{[]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho_{n}}.

Let
\sigma^{2}=\sup_{u\in\mathcal{G}}Ph^{2}
. Suppose there exists
c>0
such that
n\sigma^{2}>c
.
Then

\mathbb{E}[\|\mathfrak{R}_{n}\|_{\mathcal{G}}]\lesssim\max\bigg\{\sigma\bigg(\frac{\rho_{n}\log(\I_{\mathcal G}/\sigma)}{n}\bigg)^{1/2},\frac{\rho_{n}\log(\I_{\mathcal G}/\sigma)}{n}\bigg\}.

By Lemma 9.1 of
Wellner (2005)
,
N_{[]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),L_{2}(P))\lesssim N(\epsilon/2,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
for any measure
P
.
On the other hand, since
\tilde{f}
is fixed, for any
\epsilon>0
, the covering number
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
is not larger than the covering number of the function-class

\mathcal{C}_{1}=\bigg\{\mathcal{D}\mapsto\frac{(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}{Y_{i}})\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});a_{t})}{\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid H_{t})}\ \bigg|\ f=\text{tran}(g),g\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\bigg\}.

Note that
\mathcal{D}\mapsto\sum_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}/\prod_{t=1}^{T}\pi_{t}(a_{t}\mid H_{t})
is a fixed function and it is also bounded because the
Y_{i}
’s are bounded by a constant, say
C_{\text{max}}
, by Assumption IV, and the
\pi_{t}
’s are bounded away from
C_{\pi}>0
by Assumption I. Since the
Y_{t}
’s are also positive by Assumption V, the above function takes value in
[0,TC_{\text{max}}/C_{\pi}^{T}]
. Let us denote
c=TC_{\text{max}}/C_{\pi}^{T}
. Letting

\mathcal{C}_{2}=\bigg\{\mathcal{D}\mapsto\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\mid f=\text{tran}(g),\ g\in\mathcal{U}_{n}\bigg\},

we note that

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N(\epsilon/c,\mathcal{C}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

(S10.6)

Thus to apply Fact
S10.1
, it suffices to show that there exists
\rho_{n}^{\prime}>0
so that

N_{[]}(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho^{\prime}_{n}}\text{ for all }\epsilon>0.

Recall that
g_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{tn}^{k_{t}-1}
and
g_{ti}\in\mathcal{U}_{tn}
for each
i\in[k_{t}]
. For a fixed
(i,t)
pair with
t\in[T]
and
i\in[k_{t}]
, we define the class of functions

\mathcal{C}(i,t):=\bigg\{u:\mathcal{H}_{t}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\mid u(h_{t})=\phi_{t}(f_{t}(h_{t});i),\ f_{t}=\text{tran}(g_{t}),\ g_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{tn}^{k_{t}-1}\bigg\}.

We will apply Lemma
S10.3
in Section
S10.2
with
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}(i,t)
,
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{U}_{tn}
,
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}_{t}
,
k=k_{t}
,
\phi=\phi_{t}
, and
u(\cdot)=\phi_{t}(f_{t}(\cdot),i)
to obtain its covering number. Lemma
S10.3
applies here because,
by Condition
1
, for fixed
i\in[k_{t}]
, the function
\mathbf{x}_{t}\mapsto\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x}_{t},i)
is globally Lipschitz. Lemma
S10.3
yields

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}(i,t),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim N(\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k_{t}}),\mathcal{U}_{tn},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})^{k_{t}-1}\leq N(\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k_{t}}),\mathcal{U}_{tn},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})^{k_{t}}.

(S10.7)

Now consider the function class

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}(t):=\bigg\{u:\mathcal{H}_{t}\times[k_{t}]\mapsto\mathbb{R}\mid

\displaystyle\ u(h_{t},i)=\phi_{t}(f_{t}(h_{t}),i)\text{ for all }h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t},

\displaystyle\ i\in[k_{t}],\text{ where }f_{t}=\text{tran}(g_{t}),\ g_{t}\in\mathcal{U}_{tn}^{k_{t}-1}\bigg\}.

We now use Lemma
S10.4
in Section
S10.2
to bound the covering number of
\mathcal{C}(t)
. To this end, we take
\mathcal{X}=\mathcal{H}_{t}
,
\mathcal{G}=\mathcal{C}(t)
,
u_{a}=\phi_{t}(f_{t}(\cdot),a)
,
\mathcal{C}_{i}=\mathcal{C}(i,t)
, and
\mathcal{A}=[k_{t}]
to obtain

\displaystyle N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}(t),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}(i,t),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})^{k_{t}}.

(S10.8)

Now note that the functions in
\mathcal{C}_{2}
are
T
-fold products of functions from function-classes
\mathcal{C}(1),\ldots,\mathcal{C}(T)
. Moreover, the functions in
\mathcal{C}(t)
are positive for each
t\in[T]
because
\phi_{t}
’s are positive. We also showed that the
\phi_{t}
’s are also uniformly bounded by one.
Hence, the functions in
\mathcal{C}(t)
are also bounded by one. Therefore,

\displaystyle N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\prod_{t=1}^{T}N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}(t),\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

(S10.9)

Combining (
S10.7
), (
S10.8
), and (
S10.9
) with (
6.6
), we obtain that

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{2},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim\prod_{t=1}^{T}N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}(t),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\prod_{t=1}^{T}\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}C\sqrt{k_{t}}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho_{n}k_{t}^{2}}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}C\sqrt{\mathcal{K}}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho_{n}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}k_{t}^{2}}

where
\mathcal{K}=\sum_{t=1}^{T}k_{t}
.
Since the
k_{t}
’s,
T
, and
C
are finite,

\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}C\sqrt{\mathcal{K}}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho_{n}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}k_{t}^{2}}\lesssim\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho_{n}\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T}k_{t}^{2}}=\bigg(\frac{\mathcal{I}_{n}}{\epsilon}\bigg)^{\rho^{\prime}_{n}},

where
\rho_{n}^{\prime}=\rho_{n}\sum_{t=1}^{T}k_{t}^{2}
.
Since the
k_{t}
’s are finite integers,
\liminf_{n}\rho_{n}>0
,
\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}=o(n)
, and
\liminf_{n}\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}>0
, it holds that
\liminf_{n}\rho^{\prime}_{n}>0
,
\rho^{\prime}_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}=o(n)
, and
\liminf_{n}\rho^{\prime}_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}>0
. Therefore, (
S10.6
) implies
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{1},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim(\mathcal{I}_{n}/\epsilon)^{\rho^{\prime}_{n}}
. Therefore, we have established
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim(\mathcal{I}_{n}/\epsilon)^{\rho^{\prime}_{n}}
. By Lemma 9.1 of
Wellner (2005)
,
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),L_{2}(Q))\lesssim N(\epsilon/2,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
for any measure
Q
. In particular, when we take
Q=\mathbb{P}_{n}
, we obtain that
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n}),L_{2}(\mathbb{P}_{n}))\lesssim(\mathcal{I}_{n}/\epsilon)^{\rho_{n}^{\prime}}
. Therefore, Fact
S10.1
can be applied on
\mathcal{G}_{n}(\epsilon_{n})
with
\rho_{n}
replaced by
\rho_{n}^{\prime}
.
The rest of the proof is similar to the proof of Step 2 of Theorem 5 of
Laha et al. (2024b)
, and hence omitted.

S10.2
Proof of auxiliary lemmas for proving Theorem
6.2

Lemma S10.3
.

Let
\mathcal{X}
be a space, and
\mathcal{C}
is a space of functions mapping
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Let
\phi
be a fixed Lipschitz map with constant
C>0
from the metric space
(\mathbb{R}^{k-1},l_{2})
to the metric space
\mathbb{R}
endowed with the usual absolute value metric. Define

\mathcal{G}=\bigg\{u:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\ \mid\ u=\phi(0,g_{1},\ldots,g_{k-1}),g_{i}\in\mathcal{C}\text{ for all }i\in[k-1]\bigg\}.

Further suppose the covering number
N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
is finite for each
\epsilon>0
.
Then

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N(\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k-1}),\mathcal{C},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})^{k-1}.

Proof of Lemma
S10.3
.

Any
u\in\mathcal{G}
is of the form
u=\phi(0,g_{1},\ldots,g_{k-1})
where the
g_{i}
’s are in
\mathcal{C}
. Suppose
N=N(\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k-1}),\mathcal{C},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})
. Then there exists an
\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k-1})
covering
\Theta
of
\mathcal{C}
of size
N
.
Suppose
\{\mathfrak{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{A}_{k-1}\}\subset\Theta
is such that

\|\mathfrak{A}_{i}-g_{i}\|_{\infty}<\epsilon/(C\sqrt{k-1})\quad\text{ for all }i\in[k-1].

Let us denote
u_{cov}=\phi(0,\mathfrak{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{A}_{k-1})
.
For any
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X}
,

\displaystyle|u(\mathbf{x})-u_{cov}(\mathbf{x})|=

\displaystyle\ |\phi(g_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,g_{k-1}(\mathbf{x}))-\phi(\mathfrak{A}_{1}(\mathbf{x}),\ldots,\mathfrak{A}_{k-1}(\mathbf{x}))|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ C\sqrt{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{k-1}(g_{i}(\mathbf{x})-\mathfrak{A}_{i}(\mathbf{x}))^{2}}

because
\phi
is Lipschitz with constant
C
.
Thus

\displaystyle\|u-u_{cov}\|_{\infty}\leq C\sqrt{k-1}\sup_{1\leq i\leq k-1}\|g_{i}-\mathfrak{A}_{i}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon.

Therefore,

\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}=\bigg\{g:\mathcal{X}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\ \mid\ g=\phi(0,\mathfrak{A}_{1},\ldots,\mathfrak{A}_{k-1}),\mathfrak{A}_{i}\in\Theta\text{ for all }i\in[k-1]\bigg\}

is an
\epsilon
-covering of
\mathcal{G}
. Note that the cardinality of
\mathcal{G}_{\Theta}
is
N^{k-1}
. Thus, the proof follows.

∎

Lemma S10.4
.

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a space, and
\mathcal{A}
is a finite set.
For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\mathcal{C}_{a}
be a space of functions mapping
\mathcal{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
. Let us define

\mathcal{G}=\bigg\{f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\mid f(\mathbf{x},a)=u_{a}(\mathbf{x})\text{ where }u_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}\bigg\}.

Then

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{G},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{a},\|\cdot\|_{\infty}).

Proof of Lemma
S10.4
.

We will construct an
\epsilon
-covering of
\mathcal{G}
. For each
a\in\mathcal{A}
, let
\Theta_{a}
be a covering of
\mathcal{C}_{a}
. Suppose the cardinality of
\Theta_{a}
is
N_{a}
. Then

N(\epsilon,\mathcal{C}_{a},\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\leq N_{a}.

Every
f\in\mathcal{G}
is of the form

f(\mathbf{x},i)=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}u_{a}(\mathbf{x})1[a=i],\quad\text{for all }\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{X},\ i\in\mathcal{A},

where the
u_{a}
’s are some functions satisfying
u_{a}\in\mathcal{C}_{a}
for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
. There exist
\mathfrak{A}_{a}\in\Theta_{a}
for each
a\in\mathcal{A}
so that
\|\mathfrak{A}_{a}-u_{a}\|_{\infty}\leq\epsilon
.
Then
f_{\text{cover}}(\mathbf{x},i)=\sum_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathfrak{A}_{a}(\mathbf{x})1[i=a]

satisfies

\displaystyle\|f_{\text{cover}}-f\|_{\infty}\leq\sup_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\|\mathfrak{A}_{a}-u_{a}\|_{\infty}=\epsilon.

Therefore

\bigg\{f:\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{A}\mapsto\mathbb{R}\mid f(\mathbf{x},i)=\sum\limits_{a\in\mathcal{A}}\mathfrak{A}_{a}(\mathbf{x})1[i=a]\text{ where }\mathfrak{A}_{a}\in\Theta_{a}\text{ for each }a\in\mathcal{A}\bigg\}

is an
\epsilon
-cover of
\mathcal{G}
. The cardinality of the above set is bounded by
\prod_{a\in\mathcal{A}}N_{a}
, which completes the proof of the current lemma.

∎

S10.2.1
Proof of Lemma
S10.2

Proof of Lemma
S10.2
.

We will first show that for any
f\in\mathcal{F}
,

\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}\leq C\bigg(V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\bigg)^{\alpha/(1+\alpha)}.

Note that
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}
equals

\displaystyle\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}}{\prod_{i=1}^{T}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg|\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\prod_{t=1}^{T}\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\bigg|\right]

where we used the fact that
Y_{i}
’s and
\pi_{i}
’s are positive. Now we will use the following lemma, which is proved in Section
S10.2.3
.

Lemma S10.5
.

Under the setup of Theorem
6.2
, any
f\equiv(f_{1},\ldots,f_{T})\in\mathcal{F}
satisfies

\displaystyle\ \prod_{i=1}^{T}\phi(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{T}\phi(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\Big(\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i}),

where products over
\prod_{i=1}^{0}
or
\prod_{i=T+1}^{T}
are taken to be one.

Since the
\phi_{t}
’s are positive, using Lemma
S10.5
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}|]=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{T}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\right.
