\displaystyle\ \times\left.\bigg|\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\Big(\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\Big)\bigg|\right],

where the products over
\prod_{1}^{0}
and
\prod_{T+1}^{T}
are taken to be one.
By triangle inequality, the above expression is bounded above by

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i})\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{T}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\right.

\displaystyle\ \times\left.|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})|\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\Big(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}\right]\right.

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\ \left.\frac{|\Big(\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})|}{\prod_{i=1}^{t}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\right].

Combining (
S10.3
), which gives
\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})=1[A_{i}=d_{i}^{*}(H_{i})]
for all
i\in[T]
, with Fact
S11.4
, we obtain that

\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{(\sum\limits_{i=1}^{T}Y_{j})\prod_{i={t+1}}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i={t+1}}^{T}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}\right]=Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},A_{t}).

Therefore, we have obtained that
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}
is bounded by

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\frac{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},A_{t})|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})|}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},A_{t})|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})|}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\bigg|H_{t}\right]\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(b)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)|}\bigg]\

(S10.10)

where step (a) follows because
(H_{i},A_{i})\subset H_{t}
for any
i\in[t-1]
for all
t\in[2:T]
, and step (b) follows because

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},A_{t})|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\psi(f_{i}(H_{t});A_{t})|}{\pi_{t}(A_{t}\mid H_{t})}\mid H_{t}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)|\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)-\psi(f_{i}(H_{t});i)|},

which is obtained by (
S6.6
).

S10.2.2
Lower bound on
V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)

Since
V^{\psi}_{*}=V^{\psi}(f^{*})
by Lemma
S10.1
,
V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)=V^{\psi}_{*}-V^{\psi}(f)
, which, by Lemma
S9.1
, equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)\bigg\}\right].

Equation
S10.3
implies
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)=0
for
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
. Therefore,

\displaystyle V(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\right.

\displaystyle\times

\displaystyle\ \left.\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)\bigg\}\right]

Note that the integrated is always positive because for
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
,

\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)\geq 0.

Let us fix
z>0
and denote

\mathcal{E}_{t}=\{h_{t}\in\mathcal{H}_{t}:\mu(Q_{t}^{*}(h_{t}))\leq z\}.

Let us denote the random variables
1[H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{t}]
and
1[H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}^{c}_{t}]
by
1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}
and
1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}
, respectively.

Then
V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)
is bounded below by

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\geq

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i))\right.

\displaystyle\ \left.\times\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)\bigg\}1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\right]

(S10.11)

By Assumptions IV,
Y_{t}\leq C_{max}
for some
C_{max}>0
for all
t\in[T]
. Thus,
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\leq 2C_{max}
. Hence,
For
H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}
and
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
,

Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)\geq\mu(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))>z\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{\geq}}\frac{z\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\Big)}{2C_{max}},

where (a) follows because

\{Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\}/(2C_{max})\leq 1
.
When
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
,

\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)=\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)\geq 0,

which implies that if
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
and
H_{t}\in\mathcal{E}^{c}_{t}
, then

\displaystyle\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \frac{z}{2C_{max}}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big).

(S10.12)

However,

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)

\displaystyle\ +\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

(S10.13)

where in step (a), we used the fact that
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)=0
for all
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
, which follows from (
S10.3
). Since
\sum_{i\in[k_{t}]}\phi_{t}(\mathbf{x};i)=1
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{t}}
, the above equals

\displaystyle Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\Big(1-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\Big(\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\Big)

\displaystyle\ +\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

(S10.14)

where in step (a), we have used the fact that
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=1
, which follows from (
S10.3
).
Now we will show that

\displaystyle\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\geq 0\text{ for all }i\in[k_{t}]\text{ and }t\in[T].

(S10.15)

To this end, first consider the case when
i=d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
. Since
\phi_{t}\leq 1
and

\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))=1
,

\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))\geq 0.

When
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
, (
S10.15
) still holds since
\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)=0
for all
i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})
by (
S10.3
), and
\phi_{t}\geq 0
.
Then (
S10.15
), combined with (
S10.13
) and (
S10.14
), implies that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]:i\neq d_{t}^{*}(H_{t})}\Big(Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};d_{t}^{*}(H_{t}))+Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\Big)\Big(\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)\Big)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|.

The above, combined with (
S10.11
) and (
S10.12
), implies that
V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)
is bounded below by

\displaystyle\frac{z}{2C_{max}}\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|\bigg\}1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}^{c}}\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \frac{z}{2C_{max}}\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|\bigg\}\right]

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\ \frac{z}{2C_{max}}\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|\bigg\}1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\right]

Because
\phi_{t}\leq 1
for all
t\in[T]
,
\sum_{i=1}^{T}|Y_{i}|\leq TC_{\text{max}}
, and
\pi_{t}\geq C_{\pi}
for all
t\in[T]
,
it follows that

\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\left({\sum}\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}\begin{matrix}|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|\\
\times Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)\end{matrix}\right)1_{\mathcal{E}_{t}}\right]\leq\frac{k_{t}C_{max}\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{t})}{C_{\pi}^{T}},

which is less than
Cz^{\alpha}
,
where the last step follows from Assumption
1
. Here
C>0
is a constant depending only on
\mathbb{P}
. Thus we have showed that

\displaystyle\ V(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\geq-Cz^{1+\alpha}

\displaystyle\ +\frac{z}{2C_{max}}\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\mathbb{E}\left[\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\frac{\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})}{\pi_{i}(A_{i}\mid H_{i})}\bigg\{\sum\limits_{i\in[k_{t}]}Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t};i)|\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});i)-\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});i)|\bigg\}\right],

which, combined with the upper bound on
\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}

from (
S10.10
), leads to

\displaystyle V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\geq Cz\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}-Cz^{1+\alpha}

(S10.16)

for some constant
C>0
. Since
\pi_{t}\geq C_{\pi}
,
\sum_{t\in[T]}|Y_{t}|\leq TC_{\text{max}}
and
\phi_{t}\leq 1
, it follows that

\absolutevalue{\slb\sum\limits_{t\in[T]} Y_t\srb\prod_{j=1}^T\frac{\psi(f_j(H_j);A_j)}{\pi_j(A_j\mid H_j)}}\leq\frac{TC_{\text{max}}}{C_{\pi}^{T}}.

Therefore
\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}
is uniformly bounded by
TC_{max}C_{\pi}^{-T}
. Therefore,

\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq TC_{max}C_{\pi}^{-T}\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},1}.

Hence, (
S10.16
) leads to

V(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\geq C\Big(z\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}-z^{1+\alpha}\Big),

which leads to

\|\mathfrak{U}_{f^{*},f}\|_{\mathbb{P},2}\leq z^{\alpha}+\Big(V(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f)\Big)z^{-1}.

Optimizing over
z
, we find that the upper bound is smallest when
z=(V^{\psi}(f^{*})-V^{\psi}(f))^{1/(1+\alpha)}
, which completes the proof.
∎

S10.2.3
Proof of Lemma
S10.5

Proof of Lemma
S10.5
.

The proof follows noting the following telescoping sum

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{t\in[T]}\Big(\phi_{t}(f^{*}_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})-\phi_{t}(f_{t}(H_{t});A_{t})\Big)\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{t}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \prod_{i=1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})+\sum\limits_{t=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{t}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})

\displaystyle\ -\sum\limits_{t=1}^{T-1}\prod_{i=1}^{t}\phi_{t}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})-\prod_{i=1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i}).

(S10.17)

Replacing
t
by
t^{\prime}=t-1
in the second sum on the RHS of (
S10.17
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\sum\limits_{t=2}^{T}\prod_{i=1}^{t-1}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sum\limits_{t^{\prime}=1}^{T-1}\prod_{i=1}^{t^{\prime}}\phi_{i}(f_{i}(H_{i});A_{i})\prod_{i=t^{\prime}+1}^{T}\phi_{i}(f^{*}_{i}(H_{i});A_{i}),

which cancels the third term on the RHS of (
S10.17
). Hence, the proof follows from (
S10.17
).
∎

S10.3
Proof of Corollary
1

Proof of Corollary
1
.

We have already mentioned in Section
6.3
that
\mathcal{U}_{tn}={\mathcal{F}}(\mathcal{N}_{n},W_{n},s_{n})
satisfies (
6.6
) with
\rho_{n}=s_{n}+1
and
\mathcal{I}_{n}=(\mathcal{N}_{n}+1)(s_{n}+1)^{2\mathcal{N}_{n}+4}
. We will apply Theorem
6.2
.
To this end, we first show that
\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}=o(n)
and
\liminf_{n}\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}>0
. Since
\mathcal{N}_{n},s_{n}\geq 1
, we have
\mathcal{I}_{n}\geq 2\times 2^{6}
. Thus
\log\mathcal{I}_{n}>0
trivially follows. Also,
\rho_{n}=s_{n}>0
. Therefore
\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}>0
for all
n>e
. Also since
\log(s_n)=O(\log n)
,
\log\mathcal{I}_{n}
is of the order
O(\log n)
. Noting
\rho_{n}=O(s_{n})=O\left(n^{\frac{q}{(2+\alpha)\beta+q}}\right)
, we thus conclude that
\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}=o(n)
.
Therefore, it suffices to show that (
6.6
) holds for some
b_{n}\gg\sqrt{n}
. We will take
b_{n}=n
. Since
s_{n}=c_{2}n^{q/((2+\alpha)\beta+q)}
and
\mathcal{N}_{n}=c_{1}\log n
, we have

\displaystyle\bigg(\frac{\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}}{n}\bigg)^{1/(2+\alpha)}\geq C\bigg(\frac{s_{n}(2\mathcal{N}_{n}+4)\log(s_n)+s_{n}\log\log n}{n}\bigg)^{1/(2+\alpha)}

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle\ C\bigg((\log n)^{2}\frac{s}{n}\bigg)^{1/(2+\alpha)}\geq C(\log n)^{\frac{2}{2+\alpha}}n^{-\frac{\beta}{(2+\alpha)\beta+q}}\gtrsim(\log n)^{\frac{2}{2+\alpha}}s_{n}^{-\beta/q},

(S10.18)

where the last step uses
s_{n}^{-\beta/q}=n^{-\frac{\beta}{(2+\alpha)\beta+q}}
.
Let
t\in[T]
. Under Assumption
2
,
following the proof of Supplementary Lemma M.2 of
Laha et al. (2024b)
, we can show that, for all
i\in[k_{t}-1]
, there exists a network
\tilde{g}_{ti}\in\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N}_{n},W_{n},s_{n})
and a constant
C_{q,\beta}>0
so that under our condition on
\mathcal{N}_{n}
,
W_{n}
, and
s_{n}
, the following holds for all
t\in[T]
:

\|\tilde{g}_{ti}/b_{n}-Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,1)+Q_{t}^{*}(\cdot,i+1)\|_{\infty}\leq C_{q,\beta}s_{n}^{-\beta/q},

which is bounded above by a constant multiple of
\bigg(\frac{\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}}{n}\bigg)^{1/(2+\alpha)}
by (
S10.18
). Hence, (
6.6
) holds. Therefor, the rest of the proof follows from Theorem
6.2
noting

\bigg(\frac{\rho_{n}\log\mathcal{I}_{n}}{n}\bigg)^{\frac{1+\alpha}{2+\alpha}}\lesssim(\log n)^{\frac{2(1+\alpha)}{2+\alpha}}n^{-\frac{1+\alpha}{(2+\alpha)+q/\beta}}.

∎

S10.4
Proof of Result
5

Proof of Result
5
.

Fix
j\in[k]
. If we can show that
\phi(\cdot;j)
is differentiable and the gradient is uniformly bounded in
l_{2}
norm, then the global Lipschitz continuity follows from the mean value property. First, we will show
\phi(\cdot;j)
is differentiable. To this end, we first show that
K
is differentiable with uniformly bounded partial derivatives.

Since
K
satisfies
K(\mathbf{x})=\prod_{i=1}^{k}\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}_{i})
for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbb{K}
is differentiable, the partial derivatives of
K
exist everywhere and equals

\partialderivative{K(\mx)}{\mx_i}=\mathbb{K}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{i})\prod_{r\in[k]:r\neq i}\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}_{r})

for all
i\in[k]
.
Since
\mathbb{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{K}
are bounded, the partial derivatives are also bounded.

To show that
\phi_{t}(\cdot,j)
is differentiable, it suffices to show that its partial derivatives exist and they are continuous.
Since all partial derivatives of
K
exist and bounded for all
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, by Leibniz integral rule,

\displaystyle\partialderivative{\phi(\mx;j)}{\mx_i}=

\displaystyle\ \partialderivative{\lbs\dint_{\RR^k}1[\pred(\mt)=j] K(\mx-\mt)d\mt\rbs}{\mx_i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{t})=j]\partialderivative{ K(\mx-\mt)}{\mx_i}d\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{t})=j]\mathbb{K}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{t}_{i})\prod_{r\in[k]:r\neq i}\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{t}_{r})d\mathbf{t}

for all
i\in[k]
. Thus all partial derivatives of
\phi_{t}(\cdot,j)
exist.

Now we will show that the partial derivatives of
\phi(\cdot;j)
are continuous.
Suppose
\mathbf{x}^{(n)}\to\mathbf{x}
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}\partialderivative{\phi(\mx_n;j)}{\mx_i}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{t})=j]\mathbb{K}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}^{(n)}_{i}-\mathbf{t}_{i})\prod_{r\in[k]:r\neq i}\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}^{(n)}_{r}-\mathbf{t}_{r})d\mathbf{t}.

Since
\mathbb{K}^{\prime}
and
\mathbb{K}
are bounded, the continuity of
\partial\phi(\cdot;j)/\partial\mathbf{x}_{i}
follows from the dominated convergence theorem and the continuity of
\mathbb{K}
and
\mathbb{K}^{\prime}
.

Thus we have shown that all partial derivatives of
\phi(\cdot;j)
exist and they are continuous at every
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. Hence, it follows that
\phi(\cdot;j)
is differentiable with gradient

\gradient{\phi(\mx;j)}=\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}1[\text{pred}(\mathbf{t})=j]\gradient K(\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{t})d\mathbf{t}.

To show that
\phi(\cdot;j)
is Lipschitz, it remains to show that
\gradient{\phi(\mx;j)}
is bounded in
l_{2}
norm for each
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
. To this end, it is enough to show that the partial derivatives are uniformly bounded. However, the above follows since for any
i
and
j
in
[k]
,

\displaystyle\sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\absolutevalue{\pdv{\phi(\mx;j)}{\mx_i}}=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\absolutevalue{\dint_{\RR^k}1[\pred(\mt)=j]\KK'(\mx_i-\mt_i)\prod_{r\in[k]:r\neq i}\KK(\mx_r-\mt_r)d\mt}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{k}}\absolutevalue{\KK'(\mx_i-\mt_i)}\prod_{r\in[k]:r\neq i}\mathbb{K}(\mathbf{x}_{r}-\mathbf{t}_{r})d\mathbf{t}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \sup_{\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}}\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\mathbb{K}^{\prime}(\mathbf{x}_{i}-\mathbf{t}_{i})|d\mathbf{t}_{i}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}|\mathbb{K}^{\prime}(t)|dt,

which is bounded because
\mathbb{K}^{\prime}
is integrable.
∎

S11
Additional facts

Fact S11.1
.

Suppose
f_{1}
,
\ldots
,
f_{k}
are real-valued functions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}
. If they are closed and bounded below, then their sum is closed.

Proof of Fact
S11.1
.

Suppose
f_{1}
,
\ldots
,
f_{k}
are bounded below by
C\in\mathbb{R}
. Consider the function
h=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(f_{k}-C)
. Clearly,
h
is a sum of non-negative closed functions. Closedness is equivalent to lower-semicontinuity
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, pp. 78)
. However, sum of non-negative lower semicontinuous functions is lower semicontinuous (this fact follows trivially noting
f
is lower semicontinuous if and only if
\liminf_{\mathbf{x}\to\mathbf{x}_{0}}f(\mathbf{x})\geq f(\mathbf{x}_{0})
for all
\mathbf{x}_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
). Therefore,
h
is lower semicontinuous, and hence closed. However, since
\sum_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}=h+kC
, the function
\sum_{i=1}^{k}f_{i}
is closed as well.
∎

Fact S11.2
.

Let
\mathbf{u}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}-1}
and
\mathbf{w}\in\mathbb{R}^{k_{2}-1}
be two fixed vectors.
If
f:\mathbb{R}^{k_{1}+k_{2}}\to\mathbb{R}
is closed, then so is the bivariate function
(x,y)\mapsto f(x\mathbf{u},y\mathbf{w})
, where
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
.

Proof of Fact
S11.2
.

To show closedness, it suffices to show that the above function is lower-semicontinuous everywhere.
Let
(x_{k})
and
(y_{k})
be two real sequences. Let us denote
\mathbf{x}_{k}=x_{k}\mathbf{u}
and
\mathbf{y}_{k}=y_{k}\mathbf{v}
. For any
x^{0},y^{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, it holds that

\liminf_{x_{k}\to x^{0},\ y_{k}\to y^{0}}f(x_{k}\mathbf{u},y_{k}\mathbf{v})=\liminf_{\mathbf{x}_{k}\to x^{0}\mathbf{u},\ \mathbf{y}_{k}\to y^{0}\mathbf{v}}f(\mathbf{x}_{k},\mathbf{y}_{k})\geq f(x^{0}\mathbf{u},y^{0}\mathbf{v})

because
f
is lower semicontinuous
(
Hiriart-Urruty and Lemaréchal, 2004
, pp. 78)
.
∎

Fact S11.3

(Lemma 5 of
Schmidt-Hieber (2020)
)
.

Suppose
\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N},W,s)
is a class of ReLU neural networks with linear output layer, depth
\mathcal{N}
, width vector
W
, sparsity
s
, and weights uniformly bounded by one.
Denote by
C_{\mathcal{N}}
the constant
(\mathcal{N}+1)\prod_{i=0}^{L+1}(W_{i}+1)^{2}
. Then for any
\epsilon>0
,

\log\mathcal{N}(\epsilon,\mathcal{F}(\mathcal{N},W,s),\|\cdot\|_{\infty})\lesssim(s+1)\log(2\epsilon^{-1} C_{\N}).

Also since
W_{i}\leq s
for each
i\in\{0,\ldots,L+1\}
,
C_{\mathcal{N}}\lesssim\mathcal{N}(s+1)^{2\mathcal{N}+4}
.

Fact S11.4
.

For
t\in[T]
, the Q-functions satisfy

Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},a_{t})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=a_{t}\bigg].

Proof of Fact
S11.4
.

We will prove this fact by induction where the induction hypothesis

Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},a_{t})=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=a_{t}\bigg]

holds for
t=T
because
Q_{T}^{*}(H_{T},A_{T})=\mathbb{E}[Y_{T}\mid H_{T},A_{T}]
and we have defined products over an empty range to be one. Suppose the induction hypothesis holds for
1+t
where
t\in[1:T-1]
. We will show that the induction hypothesis holds for
t
as well.
Note that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[Y_{t}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t}\bigg]

(S11.1)

\displaystyle\ +\Big(\sum_{i=t+1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(a)}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}[Y_{t}\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t+1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\ \bigg|\ H_{1+t}\bigg]\ \bigg|\ H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

(S11.2)

where (a) follows by applying (
S6.10
) on the first term. Now applying (
S6.5
) on the second term of the RHS of (
S11.1
) with

V=\Big(\sum_{i=t+1}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=2+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})},

we see that the RHS of (
S11.1
) equals

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i]

\displaystyle+

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1+t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=2+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t},A_{1+t}=d_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t})\bigg]\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg],

where the product is one if
t=T-1
, when the range of the product is empty.
Since the induction hypothesis holds for
1+t
, it follows that
Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t},d_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))
equals

\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=1+t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=2+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{1+t},A_{1+t}=d_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t})\bigg].

Thus (
S11.1
) leads to

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigg[\Big(\sum_{i=t}^{T}Y_{i}\Big)\prod_{j=1+t}^{T}\frac{1[A_{j}=d_{j}^{*}(H_{j})]}{\pi_{j}(A_{j}\mid H_{j})}\bigg|H_{t},A_{t}=i\bigg]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\ \mathbb{E}\Big[Y_{t}+Q_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t},d_{1+t}^{*}(H_{1+t}))\mid H_{t},A_{t}=i\Big],

which equals
Q_{t}^{*}(H_{t},i)
.
∎

Fact S11.5
.

If
X_{n}
is bounded and
X_{n}\to_{P}0
, then
E[X_{n}]\to_{n}0
.

Proof.

Uniform integrability and convergence in probability implies
L_{1}
convergence. Bounded sequences of random variables are uniformly integrable
(
Billingsley, 2017
)
. Hence
E[|X_{n}|\to_{n}0
.
∎
